A Guide to Krivine Realizability for Set Theory
Richard Matthews

Abstract

Contents

1
Introduction

Realizability is a technique in mathematical logic which attempts to extract computational content from mathematical proofs. Originally invented by Kleene,
[
Kle45
]
, to establish a correspondence between intuitionistic logic and Turing computable programs, it was then extended by Kleene to produce models of Heyting arithmetic and later by Friedman,
[
Fri73
]
, to produce models of intuitionistic set theory. Realizability is seen as an extension of the
proofs-as-programs
correspondence, which is also known as the
Curry-Howard isomorphism
. Within intuitionistic logic, the analogous specification is known as the Brouwer-Heyting-Kolmogorov interpretation. The idea is that one can consider propositions as “
problems
” which can be solved by being broken down into simpler problems. For example, the problem
A\rightarrow B
represents the problem of producing a method which, when given a solution to the problem
A
, solves the problem
B
. From this, it follows that
p
is a “
proof
” of
A\rightarrow B
if and only if whenever
q
is a “
proof
” of
A
then the “
application
” of
p
to
q
is a “
proof
” of
B
.

Intuitionistic realizability models can be built using
\lambda
-calculus which is a general method constructed by Church to create a formalisation of mathematics using a notion of computability in terms of application of functions. The notion of
\lambda
-calculus forms the basis for many functionally programming languages, for example Lisp, the Wolfram Language and Haskell. A dialect of Lisp is the programming language Scheme which extends
\lambda
-calculus with the additional control
call-with-current-continuation
. Using this extended calculus, in
[
Gri89
]
Griffin showed how the Curry-Howard isomorphism could be extended to classical logic in a “
computationally interesting way
” by producing a correspondence between control instructions and Peirce’s law, a statement which is equivalent to the principle of Excluded Middle.

Building upon this idea, in a series of papers (notably
[
Kri01
,
Kri09
,
Kri12
,
Kri15
,
Kri18
]
) Krivine used this extended calculus to extend the notion of realizability models to models of classical logic and then to models of
ZF
. By adding the standard additional instruction
quote
it was then showed how to produce realizability models of
ZF +DC
. This idea was further generalised in
[
FG20
]
to give realizability models of
\textnormal{{DC}}_{\kappa}
for any fixed regular cardinal
\kappa
.

Furthermore, in
[
Kri21
]
, Krivine went one step further to show that one can build programs which realize the Axiom of Choice and in fact any axiom which can be produced by the method of forcing.

The framework of Krivine’s method of realizability gives a new method to produce independence results in set theory. For example,
[
Kri12
,
Kri18
]
construct interesting new models of
ZF + DC
with complicated algebraic structures on subsets of reals that are incompatible with the Axiom of Choice. It is worthwhile remarking that every known independence result using Krivine’s method of realizability can also be produced using the more well-studied method of symmetric extensions (see
[
Kar19
]
for details). However, what this machinery provides is a new technique where the “simpler” realizability algebras can already produce complicated sets which would involve technical results on symmetric forcing to otherwise produce. There is also the added bonus that, because we are using realizability, alongside the set theoretic structures we produce we also have interesting computational content.

1.1
Outline of the paper

The main purpose of these notes is to explain what Krivine’s method of realizability is and how it relates to classic constructions, in particular
intuitionistic realizability
and
double negation translations
. As such, most of the results we give are not new and most of the proof structures are heavily influenced by the original proofs by Krivine, notably in
[
Kri12
]
. We will also not explicitly reproduce any of Krivine’s models for independence results over
ZF
. Instead, these notes will focus on the theory of realizability itself and the general framework in which it can be used.

The principle new material is a modified formalisation of how to construct realizability models in
Section

9
. Instead of taking the entire ground model universe for the domain of our realizability model, we will instead recursively produce a class of names in a similar way to the class of forcing names or McCarty’s construction of realizability models for intuitionistic set theory. The benefit of this construction is it allows for much cleaner and explicit proof of various concepts in realizability. We also give a reformulation of Krivine’s
adequacy lemma
, which is that the realizability structure is closed under classical deductions, using a formulation involving closure under rules of natural deduction without the need for types. Following the aim to give a more rigorous formalisation of realizability structures, has also led to a reformulation of the gimel operator, which we have call the
reish
, or
recursive
, operator. This is a general method to recursively construct a name in the realizability model from any given set in the ground model.

The second significant contribution in this work is an explicit construction of a name for the ordered pair of two sets in the realizability structure in
Section

14
. This method allows us to work with names for functions in a realizability model rather than having to enrich the language with function symbols. This more concrete notion gives us the ability to “lift” functions in the ground model to names for functions in the realizability model, from which it is possible to explicitly prove the preservation of characteristic properties of such functions. As a concrete example, we give a precise description of the Boolean algebra on the set of truth values.

The final notable piece of original work is a significant expansion of how to view any forcing model as a realizability algebra. Building on the work of Krivine and the presentation by Fontanella and Geoffroy in
[
FG20
]
we explain a way to translate any Boolean valued model into a realizability model. We then give a precise characterisation of the relationship between the two resulting structures, allowing us to conclude that they give rise to the same theory.

The outline to these notes is as follows:

•

In
Sections

2
,
3
and
4
we give an overview of the main ingredients that combine to produce Krivine’s method of realizability: intuitionistic realizability, double negation translations and lambda calculus.

•

In
Section

5
we discuss the intuition behind realizability models.

•

Section

6
describes what a realizability algebra for Krivine’s method is.

•

Sections

7
and
8
concerns the non-extensional theory
\ZFepsilon
which is the underlying theory of the realizability models. We give the axioms of this theory before showing that it is a conservative extension of
ZF
, and that one can restrict any model of
\ZFepsilon
to one of
ZF
in a precise way.

•

In
Section

9
we give a modified formalisation of how to construct realizability models using a class of names.

•

Sections

10
and
11
gives some initial realizers for various properties of predicate logic. We also introduce the additional symbol of
non-extensional equality
which can be used to simplify many later constructions. In
Section

10.2
we give a new formulation of Krivine’s
adequacy lemma
to show that any realizability model is a model of classical logic.

•

In
Section

12
we prove that if the ground model is a model of
ZF
then the realizability model satisfies every axiom of
\ZFepsilon
. Following on from this, in
Section

13
we give a general discussion of ordinals in the realizability model. Using the generalisation of the gimel operator, we show how to form a proper class of ordinals in any given realizability structure. We also discuss an alternative construction of ordinals which appears in
[
FG20
]
that is a more restrictive, but fruitful concept.

•

Section

14
concerns the concept of pairing in realizability models. Given names
a
and
b
in a realizability model we show how to produce names for sets which are extensionally equal to the singleton of
a
, the unordered pair of
a
and
b
and the ordered pair of
a
and
b
. Using this, we show how to define the Cartesian Products of certain sets.

•

Building upon the notion of ordered pairs, we define the concept of functions in models of
\ZFepsilon
.
Section

15
gives the basic definitions of some of the different ways to formulate functions in non-extensional theories. In
Section

16
we then give a general methodology to lift certain functions from the ground model to names for functions in the realizability model and show that many of the desired properties can be conserved in this lift.

•

Section

17
discusses the
Non-extensional Axiom of Choice
. This is an additional axiom which naturally holds in many realizability models, in particular any countable realizability model which contains the additional instruction
quote
. It is shown how to derive the Axiom of Dependent Choices from this axiom.

•

The final sections treat multiple interesting additional topics in realizability. Firstly, in
Section

18
, we show that it is consistent for
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
, which is the class of truth values, to have size
4
. Then, in
Section

19
, we give an explicit translation of any Boolean valued model into a realizability model and show that the resulting model satisfies the same underlying theory. Finally, in
Section

20
we give a partial converse to the previous result, which is to give a sufficient condition for a countable realizability algebra to be equivalent to a Boolean valued model; this is that the class of truth values is trivial, that is, it has size
2
.

2
Intuitionistic Realizability

Krivine’s method of realizability can be seen as the combination of two classical techniques from intuitionistic mathematics; intuitionistic realizability and double negation translations. Therefore, in order to better understand how the machinery works, we will briefly explain these two techniques.

The study of intuitionistic logic was developed by Brouwer at the beginning of the twentieth century and, in its simplest description, can be considered as classical logic without the law of Excluded Middle. This statement can either be read as the claim that Excluded Middle fails (and thus the logic is incompatible with classical logic) or just that Excluded Middle is not included as an axiom of the logic (in which case classical logic is intuitionistic logic plus Excluded Middle). This then leads to the question of what it means to suggest “Excluded Middle is incompatible with intuitionistic logic”. In the first scenario the addition of Excluded Middle would lead to a contradiction whereas in the second scenario it merely leads to classical logic which was what we were trying to avoid assuming. For ease of presentation, we will take the second of these approaches and therefore the aim will be to avoid assumptions that would lead to full classical logic.

As such, we wish to avoid logical principles such as double negation implication (
\neg\neg\varphi\rightarrow\varphi
) or equivalences between some logical symbols (for example
(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow(\neg\varphi\lor\psi)
). Furthermore, it is well known that the scheme of Foundation implies the law of Excluded Middle and, by Diaconescu’s Theorem
[
Dia75
]
, as does the Axiom of Choice (in either the form that there exist choice functions or that every set can be well-ordered). On the other hand, it is known that Foundation’s classically equivalent couterpart of the
\in
-Induction Scheme is intuitionistically compatible and is often included in the formulation of intuitionistic theories. Similarly, Grayson (
[
Gra75
,
Gra78
]
) proves that, starting from a model of
ZFC
, Zorn’s lemma holds in every Heyting-valued model and is thus consistent with
IZF
. We refer the interested reader to the last section of
[
Bel11
]
for a proof of this. It is worth observing here that when we formulate the theory
\ZFepsilon
we shall do so with Induction. Moreover, for any cardinal
\kappa
it is possible to realize a version of Zorn’s lemma of length
\kappa
in Krivine’s realizability models, see
[
FG20
]
for more details.

A general guiding principle for intuitionistic logic is the
Brouwer-Heyting-Kolmogorov Interpretation
which is an analogous statement to the Curry-Howard correspondence. This is an informal idea as to how one should interpret logical connectives in terms of what they can prove. The interpretation is purposely stated in a vague way without a formal definition of any of the concepts such as “proof” or “program”.

{definition}

[The Brouwer-Heyting-Kolmogorov Interpretation]

•

There is no proof of
\perp
.

•

p
is a proof of
\varphi\land\psi
iff
p
is a pair
(q,r)
where
q
proves
\varphi
and
r
proves
\psi
.

•

p
is a proof of
\varphi\lor\psi
iff
p
is a pair
(n,q)
where
n=0
and
q
proves
\varphi
or
n=1
and
q
proves
\psi
.

•

p
proves
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
iff
p
is a program which transforms any proof of
\varphi
into a proof for
\psi
.

•

p
proves
\neg\varphi
iff
p
proves
\varphi\rightarrow\perp
.

•

p
proves
\exists x\varphi(x)
iff
p
is a pair
(a,q)
where
q
is a proof of
\varphi(a)
.

•

p
proves
\forall x\varphi(x)
iff
p
is a program such that for any set
a
,
p(a)
is a proof of
\varphi(a)
.

Kleene’s original method of realizability is now seen as a realisation of this interpretation
1
1

1

However it is worth pointing out that while Kleene was aware of the BHK interpretation he considered it more of a hindrance than help in formalising realizability
[
Kle73
]
.
and gives an explicit connection between intuitionistic logic and computable functions. In the original paper,
[
Kle45
]
, S.C. Kleene developed the idea of realizability in order to show that Church’s thesis
2
2

2

which is the claim that if
\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\exists m\in\mathbb{N}\varphi(x,y)
then there exists a recursive function
f
such that
\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\varphi(n,f(n))
can be consistently added to Heyting arithmetic. This was done by recursively defining what it means for a natural number
n
to realize a formula
\varphi
in arithmetic, written
n\Vdash\varphi
, as follows:

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash t=s

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad t=s,

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash\varphi\land\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad\mathbf{1^{st}}(n)\Vdash\varphi\text{ and }\mathbf{2^{nd}}(n)\Vdash\psi,

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad\text{for every }m\in\mathbb{N},\text{ if }m\Vdash\varphi\text{ then }\{n\}(m)\downwards\text{ and }\{n\}(m)\Vdash\psi,

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash\varphi\lor\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad\mathbf{1^{st}}(n)=0\text{ and }\mathbf{2^{nd}}(n)\Vdash\varphi\text{ or }\mathbf{1^{st}}(n)\neq 0\text{ and }\mathbf{2^{nd}}(n)\Vdash\psi,

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash\forall x\varphi(x)

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad\text{for all }m\in\mathbb{N},\,\{n\}(m)\downwards\text{ and }\{n\}(m)\Vdash\varphi(m),

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash\exists x\varphi

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad\mathbf{2^{nd}}(n)\Vdash\varphi(\mathbf{1^{st}}(n)),

where
(\cdot)\colon\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
is some primitive recursive bijection whose projections are
\mathbf{1^{st}}
and
\mathbf{2^{nd}}
,
\{n\}
is the
n^{th}
Turing machine (according to some fixed enumeration) and
\{n\}(m)\downwards
is the assertion that the
n^{th}
Turing machine halts on input
m
.

Kleene extended this idea to replace the natural numbers with other realizers such as recursive functions or more general algebras. The notion of realizability was further generalised from Heyting arithmetic to all Intuitionistic set theory by Myhill and Friedman
[
Myh73
]
and McCarty
[
McC85
]
and then to Constructive set theory by Aczel and Rathjen
[
Rat06
]
. For further details on the history of realizability we refer the reader to the survey article
[
VO02
]
.

It is worthwhile to finish this section with a few more details on McCarty’s definition of the realizability structure because this will influence the presentation of Krivine realizability that we shall give. To fit with Kleene’s original number realizability we will continue to use natural numbers, noting that
\omega
can be replaced with any appropriately defined algebra. For simplicity, we will also use
ZFC
as our background universe even though this is not necessary.

We begin by defining a hierarchy
\textnormal{{V}}(Kl)_{\alpha}
by recursion on the ordinals as:

\textnormal{{V}}(Kl)_{\alpha}\coloneqq\bigcup_{\beta\in\alpha}\mathcal{P}(\omega\times\textnormal{{V}}(Kl)_{\beta})

and set the universe of realizable sets to be
\textnormal{{V}}(Kl)\coloneqq\bigcup_{\alpha\in\textnormal{{Ord}}}\textnormal{{V}}(Kl)_{\alpha}
. It then follows that each element,
a
, of the realizable universe is a collection of pairs
(n,b)
where
n\in\omega
can be seen as a witness to the claim that
b
is in
a
. For
a,b\in\textnormal{{V}}(Kl)
one then defines what it means for
n\in\omega
to realize the atomic formulas
a\in b
and
a=b
by transfinite recursion as

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash a\in b

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad\text{there exists }c\in\textnormal{{V}}(Kl)\text{ such that }(\mathbf{1^{st}}(n),c)\in b\text{ and }\mathbf{2^{nd}}(n)\Vdash a=c,

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash a=b

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad\text{for all }m\in\omega\text{ and }c\in\textnormal{{V}}(Kl),\text{ if }(m,c)\in a\text{ then }\{\mathbf{1^{st}}(n)\}(m)\Vdash c\in b,

\displaystyle\phantom{\quad\text{ iff }\qquad}\text{ and if }(m,c)\in b\text{ then }\{\mathbf{2^{nd}}(n)\}(m)\Vdash c\in a.

Finally, one extends Kleene’s notion of realizability to all first-order formulas as

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash\varphi\land\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad\mathbf{1^{st}}(n)\Vdash\varphi\text{ and }\mathbf{2^{nd}}(n)\Vdash\psi,

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad\text{for every }m\in\omega,\text{ if }m\Vdash\varphi\text{ then }\{n\}(m)\Vdash\psi,

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash\varphi\lor\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad\mathbf{1^{st}}(n)=0\text{ and }\mathbf{2^{nd}}(n)\Vdash\varphi\text{ or }\mathbf{1^{st}}(n)\neq 0\text{ and }\mathbf{2^{nd}}(n)\Vdash\psi,

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash\forall x\varphi(x)

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad\text{for all }a\in\textnormal{{V}}(Kl),\,n\Vdash\varphi(a),

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\Vdash\exists x\varphi

\displaystyle\quad\text{ iff }\qquad\text{there exists }a\in\textnormal{{V}}(Kl)\text{ such that }n\Vdash\varphi(a).

McCarty proves that in this way one can build a theory which is closed under deductions in intuitionistic logic and satisfies every axiom of
IZF
.

3
Double Negation Translations

The second ingredient that influenced Krivine’s method of realizability is the
double negation translation
. This is a technique developed by Kleene
[
Kle52
]
and further extended to set theory by Friedman
[
Fri73
]
in order to interpret classical mathematics in intuitionistic mathematics.

The essential idea is, for given first-order formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
, to produce two translations
\varphi^{\star}
and
\psi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}
such that

•

If
\textnormal{{IZF}}\vdash\varphi
(respectively
\textnormal{{ZF}}\vdash\varphi
) then
\textnormal{{IZF}}\setminus\textnormal{{Ext.}}\vdash\varphi^{\star}
(respectively
\textnormal{{ZF}}\setminus\textnormal{{Ext.}}\vdash\varphi^{\star}
),

•

If
\textnormal{{ZF}}\setminus\textnormal{{Ext.}}\vdash\psi
then
\textnormal{{IZF}}\setminus\textnormal{{Ext.}}\vdash\psi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}
.

From this, one can conclude that the four theories above are all equiconsistent. This is because if
ZF
were to derive a contradiction then, by the first interpretation, so would
\textnormal{{ZF}}\setminus\textnormal{{Ext.}}
and so, by the second interpretation,
\textnormal{{IZF}}\setminus\textnormal{{Ext.}}
would also derive a contradiction. Finally, since
\textnormal{{IZF}}\setminus\textnormal{{Ext.}}
is a subtheory of
IZF
, we must also be able to derive a contradiction in
IZF
.

{remark}

In the axiomatisation of
\textnormal{{ZF}}\setminus\textnormal{{Ext.}}
, Friedman replaces the Replacement Scheme with the Collection Scheme and the Power Set axiom with the Axiom of Weak Power Set and this will also be how we formulate the theory
\ZFepsilon
in
Section

7
. The reason for replacing Power Set is because the double negation of this axiom does not appear to be provable in the theory
IZF
, however the intuitionistically weaker (but under
ZF
without Power Set equivalent) notion of weak power set is provable.

The reason for replacing the Replacement Scheme with the Collection Scheme is much clearer. This is because, by work of Scott
[
Sco61
]
, it is known that
ZF
without Extensionality formulated with only the Replacement Scheme is equiconsistent with the much weaker theory of Zermelo Set Theory.

The first interpretation,
⋆
, works by “
simulating
” extensionality through a new definable relation,
\sim
. Essentially,
a\sim b
indicates that there is some equivalence relation
E
with
(a,b)\in E
and for any
x\in\textnormal{{trcl}}(a)
there is some
y\in b
such that
(x,y)\in E
and vice versa. Therefore, two sets are related if there is a single equivalence relation witnessing that;
a
is related to
b
, every element of
\textnormal{{trcl}}(a)
is related to an element of
\textnormal{{trcl}}(b)
and every element of
\textnormal{{trcl}}(b)
is related to an element of
\textnormal{{trcl}}(a)
.

We can then define a new relation
\in^{\star}
by

a\in^{\star}b\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\exists x\in b(x\sim a).

Using this, the
⋆
interpretation is defined by:

{definition}

For
\varphi
a first-order formula, let
\varphi^{\star}
be the formula which is abbreviated by the result of replacing each instance of
\in
in
\varphi
with
\in^{\star}
and
=
by
\sim
.

For the second interpretation, we take a
\neg\neg
-translation
, the details of which can be found in Section 81 of
[
Kle52
]
. The idea is to define a translation that transforms classically valid propositions into ones that are classically equivalent but still intuitionistically valid. In particular, we will have that if classical logic deduces
\varphi
then intuitionistic logic deduces
\varphi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}
and classical logic deduces
\varphi
if and only if it deduces
\varphi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}
.

{definition}

The translation
\varphi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}
is defined recursively for
\varphi
a first-order formula by the following specifications:

a.

(a\in b)^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}

\equiv

\neg\neg(a\in b)
,

b.

(\varphi\land\psi)^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}

\equiv

\varphi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}\land\psi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}
,

c.

(\varphi\lor\psi)^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}

\equiv

\neg\neg(\varphi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}\lor\psi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}})
,

d.

(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}

\equiv

\varphi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}\rightarrow\psi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}
,

e.

(\neg\varphi)^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}

\equiv

\neg(\varphi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}})
,

f.

\forall x~\varphi(x,u)

\equiv

\forall x~\varphi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}(x,u)
,

g.

\exists x~\varphi(x,u)

\equiv

\neg\neg\exists x~\varphi^{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$-$}}}(x,u)
.

In
Section

5
we will discuss how intuitionistic realizability and the double negation translation naturally led to Krivine’s notion of realizability. However, before that we will briefly introduce the notion of
lambda terms
which are an important part of the theory of realizability.

4
Lambda terms

The final preliminary information we will need is the idea of lambda terms. We will only explain the basic concepts of this extensive area of research that are needed for the rest of this paper. We then refer the reader to
[
Bar84
]
for a more comprehensive study of
\lambda
calculus.

\lambda
-calculus was devised by Church in the 1930s as a simple semantics for defining computations, with the idea being to produce a formalisation of mathematics using functions as the basic concepts. One main principle of
\lambda
-calculus is that it treats functions “
anonymously
”. By this we mean that instead of defining the function
f
such that
f(x)=x^{2}+1
, we write this as
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u^{2}+1
. The class of terms will be defined recursively using two rules:
application
and
abstraction
.

{definition}

The class of
\lambda
-terms is defined to be the smallest class
X
such that

•

Every variable is in
X
,

•

If both
s
and
t
are in
X
then so is
st
, (
application
)

•

If
s
is in
X
and
u
is a variable, then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}s
is in
X
. (
abstraction
)

{remark}

•

Application will be a left associative operation. So
tsr
is
(ts)r
,

•

Abstraction will have higher priority than application. So
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}ts
is
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(ts)
.

{definition}

The free variables of a term
t
,
\textnormal{{FV}}(t)
, are defined inductively as follows:

•

If
u
is a variable, then
\textnormal{{FV}}(u)=\{u\}
,

•

\textnormal{{FV}}(ts)=\textnormal{{FV}}(t)\cup\textnormal{{FV}}(s)
,

•

\textnormal{{FV}}(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}t)=\textnormal{{FV}}(t)\setminus\{u\}
.

A term
t
is said to be
closed
if
\textnormal{{FV}}(t)=\emptyset
and
open
otherwise.

Two terms are said to be
\alpha
-equivalent
, written
t\equiv_{\alpha}s
, if they are equal up-to a change in variables. For example,
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}uw\equiv_{\alpha}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}vw
, however
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}uw
it is not equivalent to
\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}ww
because
w
occurs freely in the first expression but becomes bound in the second one. We will consider terms to be equal up-to
\alpha
-equivalence and freely change between such equivalences when it aids readability.

The main way we shall compare two different terms is through
\beta
-reduction
:

(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}t)s\rightarrow_{\beta}t[u\coloneqq s],

where
t[u\coloneqq s]
is the substitution of every free occurrence of
u
in the term
t
with the term
s
.

We end this section by noting a few
\lambda
-terms that will be used frequently in this work: the
identity term
and the
Church numerals
.

{definition}

•

I
denotes the
\lambda
-term
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u
,

•

\underline{0}
denotes the
\lambda
-term
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}v
,

•

For
n\in\omega
,
\underline{n+1}
denotes the
\lambda
-term
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}(\underline{n}u)(uv)
. In particular,
\underline{1}
denotes the
\lambda
-term
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}uv
.

{remark}

Let
\textnormal{{s}}=\lambda n\boldsymbol{.}\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}(nu)(uv)
. Then, for every
n\in\omega
we have
\textnormal{{s}}\underline{n}\rightarrow_{\beta}\underline{n+1}
.

5
Introduction to Realizability

Bringing all of the threads together, we now discuss the intuition behind the realizability models before formally constructing them in the next sections. Since Kleene’s original method results in intuitionistic models and we wish to produce classical models, the idea is to combine realizability with a double negation translation. Therefore, instead of working with realizers providing evidence for the “truth” of an assertion we will want to provide evidence that an assertion is not false.

Our algebra will consist of two sets: the
terms
,
\Lambda
, and the
stacks
,
\Pi
. There will also be a special subset of the terms,
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\Lambda
, called the
realizers
, which will be used to indicate the formulas that are true in our resulting model.

To each formula,
\varphi
we will then assign two values: its “
truth
” value,
\lvert\varphi\rvert\subseteq\Lambda
, which can be seen as providing evidence for the formula and its “
falsity
” value,
\lVert\varphi\rVert\subseteq\Pi
, which can be seen as providing evidence against the formula.

Finally, a
process
will consist of a pair
(t,\pi)
where
t\in\Lambda
and
\pi\in\Pi
. We will specify some set of processes
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}\subseteq\Lambda\times\Pi
, called the
pole
, which can be thought of as a specification of when a truth value is incompatible with a falsity value.

Now, to say that
t
provides evidence that a statement is not false, or alternative
t

realizes
a statement, is to say that
t
is incompatible with anything that falsifies the statement. In other words,
t\in\lvert\varphi\rvert
if and only if
\forall\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rVert
,
(t,\pi)\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. This leads to the idea that a formula
\varphi
is “
true
” if there is nothing falsifying it, i.e.
\lVert\varphi\rVert=\emptyset
and “
false
” if everything falsifies it, i.e.
\lVert\varphi\rVert=\Pi
. Thus if we let
\top
and
\perp
symbolise true and false this leads to the definitions
\lVert\top\rVert=\emptyset
and
\lVert\perp\rVert=\Pi
.

We next consider what it means to realize an implication. Firstly,
t\in\lvert\varphi\rightarrow\psi\rvert
if for any
\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rightarrow\psi\rVert
,
(t,\pi)\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. So what does
\pi
look like? Well evidence against
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
would be a combination of evidence for
\varphi
, say
s\in\lvert\varphi\rvert
, and evidence against
\psi
, say
\sigma\in\lVert\psi\rVert
. This is written
\pi=s\boldsymbol{.}\sigma
, which leads to the following “definition”,

\lVert\varphi\rightarrow\psi\rVert=\{s\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\divline s\in\lvert\varphi\rvert,\sigma\in\lVert\psi\rVert\}.

This leads to the conclusion that it may in general be very difficult for an implication to be “
true
” in our realizability model. This is because we have no control over what
t
is. As an example, consider the sentence
\perp\rightarrow\perp
, i.e. the sentence false implies false (which should be “true” in the realizability model). Now suppose we had picked
t
such that for any
\pi\in\Pi
,
(t,\pi)\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
3
3

3

This is indeed possible in any realizability models whose pole in non-empty. For example, using the formalised notation we will introduce in the next section, suppose that
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}\neq\emptyset
and fix
t_{0}\star\pi_{0}\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Then if
s\coloneqq\textnormal{{k}}_{t_{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi_{0}}\textnormal{{I}}
, for any
\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
we can prove that
s\star\pi\succ\textnormal{{k}}_{t_{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi_{0}}\star\textnormal{{I}}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\textnormal{{I}}\star t_{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi_{0}\succ t_{0}\star\pi_{0}\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
Then, in particular, for any
\pi\in\lVert\perp\rVert=\Pi
,
(t,\pi)\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
so
t\in\lvert\perp\rvert
. Thus
t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\perp\rightarrow\perp\rVert
for any
\pi\in\Pi
and so the implication is not fully “true” in the model. Instead we will need to restrict our witnesses to the truth of a formula to the “
well-behaved
” terms, which is the set of
realizers
,
\mathcal{R}
.

The other logical symbol we consider is the universal quantifier. Since all other logical symbols can be deduced from
\rightarrow
and
\forall
(alongside
\top
,
\perp
and the atomic formulas) it will suffice to only have explicit realizers for these two things. Again to do this we consider the falsity value of a universal quantifier,
\lVert\forall x\varphi(x)\rVert
. Evidence against
\forall x\varphi(x)
would be some witness
a
and evidence against
\varphi(a)
. This leads us to the following “definition” where the union should be taken over the “
universe of discourse
” which we shall define later

\lVert\forall x\varphi(x)\rVert=\bigcup\lVert\varphi(a)\rVert.

It only remains to discuss the “universe of discourse” and atomic formulas, and for this we will follow McCarty’s construction of realizability structures. Recall that we defined a hierarchy
\textnormal{{V}}(Kl)_{\alpha}
recursively using
\textnormal{{V}}(Kl)_{\alpha+1}=\mathcal{P}(\omega\times\textnormal{{V}}(Kl)_{\alpha})
where
\omega
was the set of realizers. Since in our construction the falsity values are more fundamental than the truth values we will instead use
\Pi
and take
\textnormal{{N}}_{\alpha+1}\coloneqq\mathcal{P}(\textnormal{{N}}_{\alpha}\times\Pi)
4
4

4

we note the unfortunate feature that the ordering of the pairs switches between the two different notions of realizability, so for Kleene’s method the realizers come first and for Krivine’s method the stacks come second
and also take unions at limit stages. Then the universe of discourse will be
\textnormal{{N}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{\alpha\in\textnormal{{Ord}}}\textnormal{{N}}_{\alpha}
. It will be shown, using a combination of
Sections

12
and
8
, that we can find binary relations
\in
and
\simeq
for which
(\textnormal{{N}},\in,\simeq)\models\textnormal{{ZF}}
.

We are now left with the most technical task, which is to define the atomic formulas. In the double negation translation, one worked in a model of
\textnormal{{ZF}}\setminus\textnormal{{Ext.}}
which contained the original elementhood relation
\in
and used this to simulate a new elementhood relation
\in^{\star}
which was compatible with extensionality. An important thing to note about this simulated relation is that it extends the real relation in that if
a\in b
then
a\in^{\star}b
. We will do the same here, however for notational reasons the “original” non-extensional relation will be denoted by
\mathop{\varepsilon}
while the “simulated” relation will be denoted by
\in
. Moreover, since we are working primarily with negations it will turn out that it is much simpler to realize
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}
and
\not\in
.

For
\mathop{\varepsilon}
, evidence that a set
b
is not in a set
a
is simply some
\pi\in\Pi
for which
(b,\pi)\in a
. Therefore,
\lVert b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert=\{\pi\in\Pi\divline(b,\pi)\in a\}
. However, to see why this is not an extensional relation, let
\theta
be any fixed realizer and consider the names
1_{A}=\{(\emptyset,\pi)\divline\pi\in\Pi\}
and
1_{B}=\{(\emptyset,\theta\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline\pi\in\Pi\}
. It is possible to realize that
0\mathop{\varepsilon}1_{A}
and
0\mathop{\varepsilon}1_{B}
and
0
is the only set in either
1_{A}
or
1_{B}
. However, these two sets are not equal (this is similar to how, in forcing, we can construct two names for the same object). In fact, it will turn out that the realizability model does not satisfy the statement
\forall x(1_{A}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x\leftrightarrow 1_{B}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)
. Instead we shall use
\mathop{\varepsilon}
to simulate a relation which is compatible with extensionality.

The extensional relation
\in
will be defined simultaneously alongside the relation
\subseteq
which will give the extensional subset relation. Extensional equality,
\simeq
, can then be defined as a conjunction of
a\subseteq b
and
b\subseteq a
. We observe here that the constructions of these atomic formulas are influenced by their intuitionistic counterparts that were defined in
Section

2
. There we defined
n\Vdash a\in b
whenever there was some
c
such that
(\mathbf{1^{st}}(n),c)\in b
and
\mathbf{2^{nd}}(n)\Vdash a=c
and
n\Vdash a\subseteq b
whenever it was the case that for any realizer
m\in\omega
and set
c
, if
(m,c)\in a
then
\{\mathbf{1^{st}}(n)\}(m)\Vdash c\in b
. Mirroring this,
a\not\in b
can be written as
\forall c(a\simeq c\rightarrow c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b)
. Thus, using the falsity values for implications, evidence against the assertion
a\not\in b
would be to find some name
c
, stack
\pi\in\Pi
and terms
t,t^{\prime}\in\Lambda
for which
(c,\pi)\in b
,
t\Vdash c\subseteq a
and
t^{\prime}\Vdash a\subseteq c
. This leads to the definition,

\lVert a\not\in b\rVert=\bigcup_{c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(b)}\{t\boldsymbol{.}t^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline(c,\pi)\in b\land t\Vdash c\subseteq a\land t^{\prime}\Vdash a\subseteq c\}.

Similarly, evidence against the assertion
a\subseteq b
would be some name
c
, stack
\pi\in\Pi
and realizer
s
for which
(c,\pi)\in a
and
t\Vdash c\not\in b
. This leads to the final definition,

\lVert a\subseteq b\rVert=\bigcup_{c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)}\{(c,\pi)\in a\land t\Vdash c\not\in b\}.

We end this section by remarking on how much of the Brouwer-Heyting-Kolmogorov interpretation we should expect to get in a model which satisfies classical logic. Firstly, both
\forall
and
\rightarrow
will follow the interpretation. For example, it will be shown in
Section

10.1
that if
t\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
and
s\Vdash\varphi
then
ts\Vdash\psi
where
ts\in\Lambda
is a term that represents the
application
of
t
and
s
. Secondly, while we will not do it in these notes, it is possible to explicitly realize conjunctions in such a way that a realizer for
\varphi\land\psi
is a term of two parts, the first of which realizes
\varphi
while the second realizes
\psi
. However, this is as far as we should expect the interpretation to go. Being able to realize a disjunction gives a theory the
disjunction property
. This is a very useful tool in intuitionistic mathematics because it tells us that if our theory can deduce
\varphi\lor\psi
then either it can deduce
\varphi
or it can deduce
\psi
. However, in classical mathematics this is not possible. For example, by Excluded Middle we can always prove
\textnormal{{Con}}(T)\lor\neg\textnormal{{Con}}(T)
for any theory
T
. On the other hand, we cannot in general say which of the two is actually true.

In a similar way, the specification for existential statements leads to the
existence property
which essentially says that if
\exists x\varphi(x)
is deducible in a theory then we can explicitly find a witness
a
for which
\varphi(a)
holds. As before, in classical logic this cannot be possible. For example, let
A
be equal to
\{1\}
is
\varphi
holds and
\{0\}
otherwise. Then it is clear that we have
\exists x(x\in A)
but to explicitly determine the value of
x
would be to determine
\varphi\lor\neg\varphi
.

6
Realizability Algebras

In order to construct a realizability algebra that can handle classical logic it will be necessary to extend the
\lambda
-calculus to also include what is known as
\lambda_{c}
-terms. This is an expanded calculus which also includes the term cc, which is known as
call-with-current-continuation
. We refer the reader to
[
FFKD87
]
for a presentation of this calculus. In order to state realizability algebras in full generality, we will also include two sets; the set of
special instructions

A
, and the set of
stack bottoms

B
, both of which may be empty.

We will define three sets;
\Lambda^{\textnormal{{open}}}_{(A,B)}
, which consists of all (possibly open)
\lambda_{c}
-terms;
\Lambda_{(A,B)}
, which consists of all
closed

\lambda_{c}
-terms; and
\Pi_{(A,B)}
, which consists of all stacks. Recall that a closed term was defined in
Section

4
to be a term in which every variable occurs within the scope of a
\lambda
-abstraction and we restrict our attention to the closed terms to have a well-defined notion of computation. To ease notation, we shall refer to elements of
\Lambda_{(A,B)}
simply as
terms
and therefore assume that all terms are closed unless explicitly stated.

{definition}

The classes
\Lambda^{\textnormal{{open}}}_{(A,B)}
,
\Lambda_{(A,B)}
and
\Pi_{(A,B)}
are defined simultaneously by induction as the smallest classes closed under the following rules:

\Lambda^{\textnormal{{open}}}_{(A,B)}
:

•

Any variable is in
\Lambda^{\textnormal{{open}}}_{(A,B)}
,

•

If
t
and
s
are in
\Lambda^{\textnormal{{open}}}_{(A,B)}
then so is
ts
, (
application
)

•

If
u
is a variable and
t
is in
\Lambda^{\textnormal{{open}}}_{(A,B)}
then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}t
is in
\Lambda^{\textnormal{{open}}}_{(A,B)}
, (
\lambda
-abstraction
),

•

cc
is in
\Lambda^{\textnormal{{open}}}_{(A,B)}
(
call-with-current-continuation
),

•

If
\pi
is in
\Pi_{(A,B)}
then
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}
is in
\Lambda^{\textnormal{{open}}}_{(A,B)}
, (
continuation constant
),

•

t_{a}
is in
\Lambda^{\textnormal{{open}}}_{(A,B)}
for
a<A
. (
special instructions
)

\Lambda_{(A,B)}
:

•

Any element of
\Lambda^{\textnormal{{open}}}_{(A,B)}
which is
closed
is in
\Lambda_{(A,B)}
.

\Pi_{(A,B)}
:

•

If
t
is in
\Lambda_{(A,B)}
5
5

5

so, in particular,
t
is
closed
and
\pi
is in
\Pi_{(A,B)}
then
t\boldsymbol{.}\pi
is in
\Pi_{(A,B)}
, (
push
),

•

\omega_{b}
is in
\Pi_{(A,B)}
for
b\in B
. (
stack bottoms
)

{definition}

A term
t
is called a
realizer
if it contains no occurrence of a continuation constant. We denote by
\mathcal{R}_{(A,B)}
the collection of all realizers.

{definition}

A
process
is an ordered pair
(t,\pi)
where
t\in\Lambda_{(A,B)}
and
\pi\in\Pi_{(A,B)}
and will be denoted
t\star\pi
. We denote by
\Lambda_{(A,B)}\star\Pi_{(A,B)}
the set of all processes.

Given such a process,
t
will be called the
head
and
\pi
the
tail
of the process.

We now give a pre-order relation,
\succ
, on the collection of process which is defined as the smallest ordering satisfying four simple rules. Often the ordering is further extended by additional specifications, possibly using the special instructions or stack bottoms.

{definition}

A
one-step evaluation
of the set of processes
\Lambda_{(A,B)}\star\Pi_{(A,B)}
is a relation
\succ_{1}
which satisfies the four rules

\displaystyle ts\star\pi

\displaystyle\quad\succ_{1}\quad t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\text{(push)},

\displaystyle\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\quad\succ_{1}\quad t[u\coloneqq s]\star\pi

\displaystyle\text{(grab)},

\displaystyle\textnormal{{{cc}} }\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\quad\succ_{1}\quad t\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\text{(save)},

\displaystyle\textnormal{{k}}_{\sigma}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\quad\succ_{1}\quad t\star\sigma

\displaystyle\text{(restore)}.

The relation
\succ
denotes the reflexive-transitive closure of
\succ_{1}
.

{remark}

An example of an additional instruction that is regularly added to a countable realizability algebra is the instruction
quote
,
q
, which allows one to compute the “
code
” of a given stack. The instruction comes with the additional rule

\textnormal{{q}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ_{1}t\star\underline{\eta}_{s}\boldsymbol{.}\pi,

where
s\mapsto\eta_{s}
is some fixed enumeration of
\Lambda
in order-type
\omega
.

Finally we fix a final segment of the processes, known as the
pole
and denoted
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. The pole will be used to determine which terms realize a given formula. Namely, given a formula
\varphi
we shall define its falsity value
\lVert\varphi\rVert\subseteq\Pi_{(A,B)}
and then say that a term
t
realizes
\varphi
if and only if for any
\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rVert
,
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

{definition}

A
pole
of the relation
\succ
over the set of processes
\Lambda_{(A,B)}\star\Pi_{(A,B)}
is a set
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}\subseteq\Lambda_{(A,B)}\star\Pi_{(A,B)}
such that

((s\star\sigma\succ t\star\pi)\;\land\;(t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}))\;\rightarrow\;s\star\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}.

{definition}

A
realizability algebra
is a tuple
\mathcal{A}\coloneqq(\Lambda_{(A,B)},\Pi_{(A,B)},\prec,\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}})
.

{notation}

Brackets around terms will mainly be used to assist in comprehension and application will be a left associative operation.

To see an example, we will use the intuition behind realizability from
Section

5
. So, suppose that
t_{i}\Vdash\varphi_{i}
and
\pi\in\Pi
. Then
t_{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
would be a falsifier for the statement
\varphi_{1}\rightarrow\perp
. Next, a realizer for the statement
\varphi_{2}\rightarrow(\varphi_{1}\rightarrow\perp)
would be the process
t_{2}\star t_{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
and
t_{2}t_{1}\star\pi\succ t_{2}\star t_{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
. Taking another step, a realizer for
\varphi_{3}\rightarrow(\varphi_{2}\rightarrow(\varphi_{1}\rightarrow\perp))
would correspond to
t_{3}\star t_{2}\boldsymbol{.}t_{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
and
(t_{3}t_{2})t_{1}\star\pi\succ t_{3}t_{2}\star t_{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t_{3}\star t_{2}\boldsymbol{.}t_{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
.

{remark}

When it is either clear from the context or not important to the discussion, we will drop mention of
A
and
B
and instead refer to the realizability algebra as
(\Lambda,\Pi,\prec,\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}})
.

7
The Theory
\ZFepsilon

Throughout these notes, we will work in first-order logic
without equality
. In addition, the only primitive logical symbols we will include in are language are
\rightarrow,\top,\perp
and
\forall
.

{definition}

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\in}
be the first-order language with non-logical symbols the binary relations
\in
and
\simeq
(which will be interpreted as the usual equality symbol,
=
).

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
be the first-order language with non-logical symbols the binary relations
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}
,
\not\in
and
\subseteq
. By abusing notation, this will also be written as the language with non-logical symbols the binary relations
\mathop{\varepsilon}
,
\in
and
\subseteq
for readability.

Let
Fml_{\in}
and
Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
denote the collection of all
\mathcal{L}_{\in}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
formulas respectively.

Using the three binary relation symbols,
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}
,
\not\in
and
\subseteq
, in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
, we then have the following abbreviations:

\bullet

a\in b

should be read as

a\not\in b\rightarrow\perp
,

\bullet

a\mathop{\varepsilon}b

should be read as

a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rightarrow\perp
,

\bullet

\varphi\land\psi

should be read as

(\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\perp))\rightarrow\perp
,

\bullet

\varphi\lor\psi

should be read as

(\varphi\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow((\psi\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow\perp)
,

\bullet

\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\varphi(x)

should be read as

\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)
,

\bullet

\exists x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\varphi(x)

should be read as

\neg\forall x(\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathop{\varepsilon}a)
,

\bullet

\exists x\varphi(x)

should be read as

\forall x(\varphi(x)\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow\perp
,

\bullet

a\simeq b

should be read as

(a\subseteq b)\land(b\subseteq a)
,

\bullet

a\simeq b\rightarrow\varphi

should be read as

(a\subseteq b)\rightarrow((b\subseteq a)\rightarrow\varphi)
.

{remark}

Technically there is a clash in notation for
a\simeq b\rightarrow\varphi
because
(a\subseteq b)\land(b\subseteq a)\rightarrow\varphi
is not the same as
(a\subseteq b)\rightarrow((b\subseteq a)\rightarrow\varphi)
. However, it can easily be seen that
(\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\theta))
and
(((\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\perp))\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow\theta)
are logically equivalent statements. Using the facts that
\ZFepsilon
is built using classical logic and that the realizability interpretation will be closed under natural deductions (
Section

10.2
) will allow us to free move between logically equivalent statements and therefore we can take the abbreviation to be the “simplest” logically equivalent form of what we want.

We now give the axioms of
\ZFepsilon
. The first axiom defines
\subseteq
and gives the relationship between
\mathop{\varepsilon}
and
\in
, which intuitively says that
\in
is obtained as the “extensional collapse” of
\mathop{\varepsilon}
. The other axioms essentially state that every axiom of Zermelo-Fraenkel set theory holds with respect to the relation
\mathop{\varepsilon}
. In the rest of these notes, we will differentiate between the standard definition of a concept and the version using
\mathop{\varepsilon}
using the prefixes
\in
and
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-. However, when it is clear from the context which theory we are working in, these prefixes will be dropped.

1.

\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Extensionality axioms.

\forall x\forall y\Big(x\in y\leftrightarrow\exists z\mathop{\varepsilon}y(x\simeq z)\Big);\quad\forall x\forall y\Big(x\subseteq y\leftrightarrow\forall z\mathop{\varepsilon}x(z\in y)\Big).

2.

\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Induction Scheme.

\forall u_{1}\dots\forall u_{n}\Big(\forall x\big(\forall y\mathop{\varepsilon}x\,\varphi(y,u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\rightarrow\varphi(x,u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\big)\rightarrow\forall x\varphi(x,u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\Big)

for every formula
\varphi(u,u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
in
Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
.

3.

\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Separation Scheme.

\forall u_{1}\dots\forall u_{n}\forall a\exists b\forall x(x\mathop{\varepsilon}b\leftrightarrow(x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\land\varphi(x,u_{1},\dots,u_{n})))

for every formula
\varphi(u,u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
in
Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
.

4.

Axiom of
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Pairing.

\forall a\forall b\exists c(a\mathop{\varepsilon}c\land b\mathop{\varepsilon}c).

5.

Axiom of
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Unions.

\forall a\exists b\,\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\forall y\mathop{\varepsilon}x(y\mathop{\varepsilon}b).

6.

Axiom of
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Weak Power Sets.

\forall a\exists b\forall x\,\exists y\mathop{\varepsilon}b\,\forall z(z\mathop{\varepsilon}y\leftrightarrow(z\mathop{\varepsilon}a\land z\mathop{\varepsilon}x)).

7.

\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Collection Scheme.

\forall u_{1}\dots\forall u_{n}\forall a\exists b\,\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\Big(\exists y\varphi(x,y,u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\rightarrow\exists y\mathop{\varepsilon}b\,\varphi(x,y,u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\Big)

for every formula
\varphi(u,v,u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
in
Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
.

8.

\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Infinity Axiom.

\forall a\,\exists b\,\Big(a\mathop{\varepsilon}b\land\forall x(x\mathop{\varepsilon}b\rightarrow\exists y(y\mathop{\varepsilon}b\land x\mathop{\varepsilon}y)\Big).

{remark}

Note that in the formulation of the axioms of
\ZFepsilon
we are taking the Axiom of Weak Power Sets instead of the standard Power Set Axiom. The same is also done by Friedman in his proof that
ZF
is equiconsistent with
IZF
,
[
Fri73
]
. The reason for this is that when one does the translation to set theories without extensionality, this is a much easier axiom to prove, primarily because proving that one set is a subset of another is normally very difficult to see. In fact, it is unclear if the
\mathop{\varepsilon}
version of the Power Set Axiom can in general be realized.

However, in a theory with extensionality it is very easy to see that the above formulation is equivalent to the standard axiomatisation of Power Set. The Axiom of Weak Power Sets essentially says that for any set
a
there is a set
b
such that for all
x
,
x\cap a\in b
. In particular, if
x\subseteq a
then
x=x\cap a\in b
. On the other hand, if the Power Set Axiom holds, then
\mathcal{P}(a)
trivially satisfies this condition.

8
Going from
\ZFepsilon
to
ZF

As proven in
[
Kri01
]
and
[
Kri12
]
, every axiom of
ZF
is provable from the axioms of
\ZFepsilon
. By reproducing this proof, in this section we shall make something explicit which has been implicitly assumed in the literature. Namely, given a realizability model
\mathcal{N}=(\textnormal{{N}},\mathop{\varepsilon},\in,\subseteq)
, we obtain a model of
ZF
by “dropping”
\mathop{\varepsilon}
.

{theorem}

Suppose that
\mathcal{N}=(\textnormal{{N}},\mathop{\varepsilon},\in,\subseteq)
is a model of
\ZFepsilon
. Then
(\textnormal{{N}},\in,\simeq)\models\textnormal{{ZF}}
, where
\simeq
interprets equality of sets.

{notation}

To ease notation, whenever
\mathcal{N}=(\textnormal{{N}},\mathop{\varepsilon},\in,\subseteq)
is a model of
\ZFepsilon
, we will denote by
\mathcal{N}_{\in}
the reduced structure
(\textnormal{{N}},\in,\simeq)
.

Since
\ZFepsilon
is a classical theory which is closed under natural deductions, the proof will consist of two parts; first we show that
\mathcal{N}_{\in}
satisfies every axiom of
ZF
, then we show that
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\in}
prove the same sentences in the langauge
\mathcal{L}_{\in}
. Before proving the theorem, we will give a list of some of the many simple statements provable in
\ZFepsilon
. All of the proofs can be found in Section 3 of
[
Kri12
]
. Note that, by
\thetheorem.(viii)
, we do not need to consider
\subseteq
as a primitive symbol in the structure
\mathcal{N}_{\in}
.

{theorem}

i.

\ZFepsilon\vdash\forall a(a\subseteq a)
;

ii.

\ZFepsilon\vdash\forall a\forall x(x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\rightarrow x\in a)
;

iii.

\ZFepsilon\vdash\forall a(a\simeq a)
;

iv.

\ZFepsilon\vdash\forall a\forall b(a\simeq b\rightarrow b\simeq a)
;

v.

\ZFepsilon\vdash\forall a\forall x\forall y(x\simeq a\land a\in y\rightarrow x\in y)
;

vi.

\ZFepsilon\vdash\forall a\forall b\forall c(a\simeq b\land a\simeq c\rightarrow b\simeq c)
;

vii.

\ZFepsilon\vdash\forall a\forall x\forall y(a\subseteq y\land x\in a\rightarrow x\in y)
;

viii.

\ZFepsilon\vdash\forall a\forall b\big(a\subseteq b\leftrightarrow\forall x(x\in a\rightarrow x\in b)\big)
.

Proof.

(i)
. This is proved by
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Induction. So suppose that the claim holds for all
x\mathop{\varepsilon}a
and fix
x\mathop{\varepsilon}a
. Then, since
x\simeq x
, we have that
\exists y\mathop{\varepsilon}a(y\simeq x)
which, by the
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Extensionality is equivalent to saying that
x\in a
.

(ii)
and
(iii)
then follow immediately from
(i)
, while
(iv)
is true by definition.

Next, let
\varphi_{1}(a)\equiv\forall x\forall y(x\simeq a\land a\in y\rightarrow x\in y)
and
\varphi_{2}(a)\equiv\forall x\forall y(a\simeq x\land a\simeq y\rightarrow x\simeq y)
. We shall prove that
\forall b\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\varphi_{1}(b)
implies
\varphi_{2}(a)
and
\varphi_{1}(a)
. Then
(v)
and
(vi)
will both follow by
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Induction. So suppose that
\forall b\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\varphi(b)
.

To prove
\varphi_{2}(a)
, fix
x
and
y
and suppose that
a\simeq x
and
a\simeq y
. We shall prove that
x\subseteq y
, with the argument for
y\subseteq x
being symmetric. To do this, fix
z\mathop{\varepsilon}x
. Since
x\subseteq a
we have that
z\in a
which means that we can fix some
b\mathop{\varepsilon}a
for which
z\simeq b
. Next, since
a\subseteq y
,
b\in y
. Now, by the inductive hypothesis,
\varphi_{1}(b)
holds, from which it follows that
z\in y
.

To prove
\varphi_{1}(a)
, fix
x
and
y
and suppose that
x\simeq a
and
a\in y
. Next, take
z\mathop{\varepsilon}y
such that
a\simeq z
. By
\varphi_{3}(a)
,
x\simeq z
from which it follows that
x\in y
.

(vii)
. Fix
x
and
y
and suppose that
a\subseteq y
and
x\in a
. Since
x\in a
, we can fix
b\mathop{\varepsilon}a
such that
b\simeq x
. Then, since
a\subseteq y
and
\varphi_{1}(b)
holds by
(v)
,
b\in y
. Thus
x\in y
, as desired.

(viii)
. For the first direction, suppose that
\forall x(x\in a\rightarrow x\in b)
and fix
x\mathop{\varepsilon}a
. Then, by
(ii)
,
x\in a
and therefore
x\in b
by the assumption. Thus
a\subseteq b
. For the reverse direction, suppose that
a\subseteq b
and
x\in a
. Then we can fix
y\mathop{\varepsilon}a
such that
y\simeq x
. Now, since
a\subseteq b
,
y\in b
and therefore, by
(v)
, we obtain that
x\in b
.

∎

{theorem}

Let
\varphi(u)
be a formula in the language of
\{\in,\simeq\}
. If
a\simeq b
, then
\ZFepsilon\vdash\varphi(a)\leftrightarrow\varphi(b)
.

Proof.

Formally, this is proven by induction on the complexity of formulas. However, it is clear that the only non-trivial case is for atomic formulas, as the proof for connectives and quantifiers is immediate. For the atomic case there are five formulas to check:
a\in x
;
x\in a
;
a\simeq x
;
x\simeq a
and
a\simeq a
. The proof of the first follows from
\thetheorem.(v)
, the second from
\thetheorem.(vii)
and the others from a combination of
\thetheorem.(iii)
,
\thetheorem.(iv)
and
\thetheorem.(vi)
.
∎

We are now in a position to prove that every axiom of
ZF
is derivable from
\ZFepsilon
.

Extensionality

(\forall a,b\,\forall x(x\in a\leftrightarrow x\in b)\rightarrow a\simeq b)

Fix
a
and
b
in
N
and suppose that
\forall x(x\in a\leftrightarrow x\in b)
. By Property
\thetheorem.(viii)
,
a\subseteq b
and
b\subseteq a
. Therefore,
a\simeq b
.

Induction

\forall\overrightarrow{u}\big(\forall x\big(\forall y\in x\,\varphi(y,\overrightarrow{u})\rightarrow\varphi(x,\overrightarrow{u})\big)\rightarrow\forall z\varphi(z,\overrightarrow{u})\big)

Let
\varphi(x,\overrightarrow{u})
be a formula in the language of
\{\in,\simeq\}
. For notational simplicity, we will drop mention of
\overrightarrow{u}
. Let
\psi(x)
be the formula
\forall z(z\simeq x\rightarrow\varphi(z))
. Observe that for any
b
in
N
, we have
\ZFepsilon\vdash\varphi(b)\leftrightarrow\psi(b)
. To see this, suppose that
\varphi(b)
holds and take
z\simeq b
. By
Section

8
we have that
\varphi(z)
holds and thus
\psi(b)
is true. On the other hand, if
\psi(b)
holds then, since
b\simeq b
,
\varphi(b)
must also hold.

Next, we shall prove that

\forall x(\forall y\in x\,\varphi(y)\rightarrow\varphi(x))\longrightarrow\forall v(\forall w\mathop{\varepsilon}v\,\psi(w)\rightarrow\psi(v)).

To do this, suppose that this premise holds, fix
v
and suppose that
\forall w\mathop{\varepsilon}v\,\psi(w)
. Take
y\in v
. Then we can find some
w\mathop{\varepsilon}v
such that
w\simeq y
. Since
\psi(w)
holds,
\varphi(y)
also holds. Thus,
\forall y\in v\,\varphi(y)
so, by the assumed premise,
\varphi(v)
holds which means that
\psi(v)
holds.

Now, by Induction in
\ZFepsilon
, we know that

\forall v(\forall w\mathop{\varepsilon}v\,\psi(w)\rightarrow\psi(v))\rightarrow\forall z\psi(z)

and therefore
\forall x(\forall y\in x\,\varphi(y)\rightarrow\varphi(x))\rightarrow\forall z\psi(z)\rightarrow\forall z\varphi(z)

as required.

Separation

(\forall\overrightarrow{u}\forall a\exists b\forall x(x\in b\leftrightarrow(x\in a\land\varphi(x)))

Let
\varphi(x,\overrightarrow{u})
be a formula in the language of
\{\in,\simeq\}
and let
a
and
\overrightarrow{u}
be in
N
. By Separation in
\ZFepsilon
, there is some
b
in
N
such that
\forall z(z\mathop{\varepsilon}b\leftrightarrow(z\mathop{\varepsilon}a\land\varphi(z,\overrightarrow{u}))
. Now, if
x\in b
then we can fix some
z\mathop{\varepsilon}b
such that
x\simeq z
. Therefore,
z\mathop{\varepsilon}a
and
\varphi(z,\overrightarrow{u})
. Next, since
x\simeq z
and
z\mathop{\varepsilon}a
, Properties
\thetheorem.(ii)
and
\thetheorem.(v)
give us that
z\in a
and
x\in a
. Finally, by
Section

8
,
\varphi(x,\overrightarrow{u})
.

For the reverse direction, suppose that
\varphi(x,\overrightarrow{u})
and
x\in a
. Then we can fix some
z\mathop{\varepsilon}a
such that
x\simeq z
. Again by
Section

8
,
\varphi(z,\overrightarrow{u})
, and thus
z\mathop{\varepsilon}b
from which we can deduce that
x\in b
.

Pairing

(\forall a\forall b\exists c(a\in c\land b\in c))

Fix
a
and
b
in
N
. By Pairing in
\ZFepsilon
, there exists some
c
in
N
such that
a\mathop{\varepsilon}c
and
b\mathop{\varepsilon}c
. Therefore, by Property
\thetheorem.(ii)
,
a\in c
and
b\in c
.

Unions

(\forall a\exists b\forall x\in a\forall y\in x(y\in b))

Fix
a
in
N
. By Unions in
\ZFepsilon
, there exists some
b
in
N
such that
\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\forall y\mathop{\varepsilon}x(y\mathop{\varepsilon}b)
. Take
x\in a
and
y\in x
. First, we can fix
u\mathop{\varepsilon}a
such that
x\simeq u
. Next, since
y\in x
, by Property
\thetheorem.(vii)
,
y\in u
and therefore we can fix some
v\mathop{\varepsilon}u
such that
y\simeq v
. By definition,
v\mathop{\varepsilon}b
so, by Property
\thetheorem.(ii)
,
v\in b
from which Property
\thetheorem.(v)
gives us that
y\in b
.

Weak Power Set

(\forall a\exists b\forall x\exists y\in b\forall z(z\in y\leftrightarrow(z\in a\land z\in x)))

Fix
a
in
N
. By Weak Power Set in
\ZFepsilon
, there exists some
b
in
N
witnessing this axiom. Given
x
we define
x^{\prime}
by the specification

\forall z(z\mathop{\varepsilon}x^{\prime}\leftrightarrow(z\mathop{\varepsilon}a\land z\in x)),

noting that such as
x^{\prime}
exists by the Separation Scheme in
\ZFepsilon
. By definition of
b
, we can find some
y\mathop{\varepsilon}b
such that
\forall z(z\mathop{\varepsilon}y\leftrightarrow(z\mathop{\varepsilon}a\land z\mathop{\varepsilon}x^{\prime}))
. Then, by construction,
\forall z(z\mathop{\varepsilon}y\leftrightarrow(z\mathop{\varepsilon}a\land z\in x))
. We shall conclude by showing that
\forall z(z\in y\leftrightarrow(z\in a\land z\in x))
.

Firstly, suppose that
z\in y
. Then we can fix some
u\mathop{\varepsilon}y
such that
z\simeq u
. By definition of
y
, this means that
u\mathop{\varepsilon}a\land u\in x
, from which Property
\thetheorem.(ii)
gives us that
u\in a
. Finally, two uses of Property
\thetheorem.(v)
gives us that
z\in a\land z\in x
.

For the reverse direction, suppose that
z\in a\land z\in x
. Since
z\in a
we can fix some
u\mathop{\varepsilon}a
such that
u\simeq z
. By Property
\thetheorem.(v)
,
u\in x
from which we can conclude that
u\mathop{\varepsilon}y
. By the Extensionality axiom of
\ZFepsilon
this means that
z\in y
.

Collection

(\forall a\exists b\forall x\in a(\exists y\varphi(x,y,\overrightarrow{u})\rightarrow\exists y\in b\varphi(x,y,\overrightarrow{u})))

Let
\varphi(x,\overrightarrow{u})
be a formula in the language of
\{\in,\simeq\}
and let
a
be in
N
. For notational simplicity, we will drop mention of
\overrightarrow{u}
. By the Collection Scheme in
\ZFepsilon
we can find some
b
in
N
such that
\forall z\mathop{\varepsilon}a(\exists y\varphi(z,y)\rightarrow\exists y\mathop{\varepsilon}b\varphi(z,y))
.

Fix
x\in a
and suppose that
\exists y\varphi(x,y)
. Then we can find some
z\mathop{\varepsilon}a
such that
z\simeq a
. Since
\varphi
is a formula in the language of
\{\in,\simeq\}
, by
Section

8
,
\exists y\varphi(z,y)
. Therefore, by definition of
b
,
\exists y\mathop{\varepsilon}b\varphi(z,y)
. Hence, by Property
\thetheorem.(ii)
and
Section

8
,
\exists y\in b\varphi(x,y)
.

Infinity

(\forall a\exists b(a\in b\land\forall x(x\in b\rightarrow\exists y(y\in b\land x\in y))))

Fix
a
in
N
. By the axiom of Infinity in
\ZFepsilon
, there exists some
b
in
N
such that
a\mathop{\varepsilon}b
and
\forall x(x\mathop{\varepsilon}b\rightarrow\exists y(y\mathop{\varepsilon}b\land x\mathop{\varepsilon}y))
. Firstly, by Property
\thetheorem.(ii)
,
a\in b
. So, suppose
x\in b
. Then we can find some
u\mathop{\varepsilon}b
such that
x\simeq u
. Then by the definition of
b
, there is some
y
such that
y\mathop{\varepsilon}b\land u\mathop{\varepsilon}y
. Using Property
\thetheorem.(ii)
again, we get that
y\in b\land u\in y
and, finally, Property
\thetheorem.(v)
gives us that
y\in b\land x\in y
.

To finish the argument, we show that any model
(\textnormal{{N}},\mathop{\varepsilon},\in,\subseteq)
of
\ZFepsilon
is in fact a
conservative
extension of the model
(\textnormal{{N}},\in,\simeq)
. Namely, if the
\ZFepsilon
model proves a statement in the language
\mathcal{L}_{\in}
then the statement is already true in the restricted
ZF
model.

{theorem}

Suppose that
\mathcal{N}=(\textnormal{{N}},\mathop{\varepsilon},\in,\subseteq)
is a model of
\ZFepsilon
and
\varphi(\overrightarrow{x})
is a formula in
Fml_{\in}
. Then, for any
\overrightarrow{a}\subset\textnormal{{N}}
,
\mathcal{N}\models\varphi(\overrightarrow{a})\Longleftrightarrow(\textnormal{{N}},\in,\simeq)\models\varphi(\overrightarrow{a})
.

Proof.

We prove the theorem by induction on the complexity of the class
Fml_{\in}
. For the atomic cases,
\in
is a subclasses of
\textnormal{{N}}\times\textnormal{{N}}
which is the same class for both the models
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\in}
. Therefore,

\textnormal{{N}}\models a\in b\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(a,b)\in\,``\in"\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\textnormal{{N}}_{\in}\models a\in b

The same argument will also hold for the relation
\subseteq
from which we will obtain that
\mathcal{N}\models a\simeq b
if and only if
\mathcal{N}_{\in}\models a\simeq b
by combining this with the proof for implications.

For the case
\varphi\equiv\psi\rightarrow\theta
,
\mathcal{N}\models\psi\rightarrow\theta
if and only if whenever
\mathcal{N}\models\psi
,
\mathcal{N}\models\theta
. Using the inductive hypothesis,

\mathcal{N}_{\in}\models\psi\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{N}\models\psi\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{N}\models\theta\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{N}_{\in}\models\theta

and therefore
\mathcal{N}_{\in}\models\psi\rightarrow\theta
. The same argument also shows the reverse implication.

For the final case,
\varphi\equiv\forall x\psi(x)
, we have that
\mathcal{N}\models\forall x\psi(x)
if and only if for every
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
,
\mathcal{N}\models\psi(a)
. Then

\mathcal{N}\models\forall x\psi(x)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\forall a\in\textnormal{{N}}\,\mathcal{N}\models\psi(a)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\forall a\in\textnormal{{N}}\,\mathcal{N}_{\in}\models\psi(a)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{N}_{\in}\models\forall x\psi(x).

As before, the same argument also works for the reverse direction.
∎

9
Construction of Realizability Models

Suppose that
V
is a model of
ZF
and
\mathcal{A}
is a realizability algebra
(\Lambda,\Pi,\prec,\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}})
which is defined in
V
. We shall define a realizability model,
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}
, over the universe as follows:

\displaystyle\text{For $\alpha$ an ordinal, let }\textnormal{{N}}_{\alpha}\coloneqq\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}\mathcal{P}(\textnormal{{N}}_{\beta}\times\Pi)

\displaystyle\textnormal{{N}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{\alpha\in\textnormal{{Ord}}}\textnormal{{N}}_{\alpha}.

Given an element
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
we will denote by
\textnormal{{dom}}(a)
the set of first co-ordinates of
a
. That is,

\textnormal{{dom}}(a)\coloneqq\{b\divline\exists\pi\in\Pi\,(b,\pi)\in a\}.

{definition}

Given a closed formula
\varphi
in
Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
with parameters in
N
, we define two truth values:
\lVert\varphi\rVert\subseteq\Pi
and
\lvert\varphi\rvert\subseteq\Lambda
.
\lVert\varphi\rVert
will be defined recursively, and then
\lvert\varphi\rvert
is defined from it as:

t\in\lvert\varphi\rvert\Longleftrightarrow\forall\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rVert(t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}).

{notation}

We shall write
t\Vdash\varphi
and say “
t
realizes
\varphi
” when
y\in\lvert\varphi\rvert
.

{definition}

[Definition of
\lVert\varphi\rVert
]

\lVert\varphi\rVert
is defined by recursion on the complexity of
\varphi
for
\varphi
in
Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
:

•

If
\varphi
is atomic, then case
\varphi
is of one of the following forms:
\top
,
\perp
,
a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b
,
a\not\in b
and
a\subseteq b
, where
a,b
are in
N
. Then,

–

\lVert\top\rVert\coloneqq\emptyset
,

–

\lVert\perp\rVert\coloneqq\Pi
,

–

\lVert a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rVert\coloneqq\{\pi\in\Pi\divline(a,\pi)\in b\}
,

–

\lVert a\not\in b\rVert\coloneqq\displaystyle{\bigcup}_{c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(b)}\{t\boldsymbol{.}t^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline t,t^{\prime}\in\Lambda\land\pi\in\Pi\land(c,\pi)\in b\land t\Vdash a\subseteq c\land t^{\prime}\Vdash c\subseteq a\}
,

–

\lVert a\subseteq b\rVert\coloneqq\displaystyle{\bigcup}_{c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)}\{t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline t\in\Lambda\land\pi\in\Pi\land(c,\pi)\in a\land t\Vdash c\not\in b\}
.

Where the definitions of
\lVert a\not\in b\rVert
and
\lVert a\subseteq b\rVert
are formally defined simultaneously by induction on
(\textnormal{{rk}}(a),\textnormal{{rk}}(b))
under the lexicographical ordering.

•

If
\varphi\equiv\psi\rightarrow\theta
, then
\lVert\varphi\rVert\coloneqq\{t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline t\Vdash\psi\land\pi\in\lVert\theta\rVert\}
.

•

If
\varphi\equiv\forall x\,\varphi(x)
, then
\lVert\varphi\rVert\coloneqq{\displaystyle\bigcup}_{a\in\textnormal{{N}}}\lVert\varphi[a/x]\rVert
.

{definition}

Given a formula
\varphi\in Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
, we say that
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\varphi
if there exists a realizer
t\in\mathcal{R}
such that
t\Vdash\varphi
.

Given a set of formulas
\Gamma
, we say that
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\Gamma
if for every
\varphi\in\Gamma
,
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\varphi
.

{remark}

We remark here that in general the theory
\textnormal{{T}}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}\coloneqq\{\varphi\in Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}\divline\exists t\in\mathcal{R}\;t\Vdash\varphi\}
is not complete. That is, there will exist formulas
\varphi
of
\ZFepsilon
such that
\mathcal{N}\not\Vdash\varphi
and
\mathcal{N}\not\Vdash\neg\varphi
. In particular, it is possible for there to be formulas which are true in some model of
\textnormal{{T}}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}
but which will not be realizable by any realizer.

Therefore what we want to do is consider the theory
\textnormal{{T}}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}
. We shall show that
\textnormal{{T}}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}
is closed under classical predicate calculus (the
adequacy lemma
,
Section

10.2
), that is if
\varphi
is in
\textnormal{{T}}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}
and
\varphi
entails
\psi
in classical logic then
\psi
is in
\textnormal{{T}}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}
. Moreover, under a small assumption of coherency, starting from a model of
ZF
,
\textnormal{{T}}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}
contains every axiom of
\ZFepsilon
and
\perp\not\in\textnormal{{T}}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}
. Therefore
\textnormal{{T}}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}
is a coherent theory extending
\ZFepsilon
.

In a similar way, we can also consider the theory
\textnormal{{T}}_{\in}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}\coloneqq\{\varphi\in Fml_{\in}\divline\exists t\in\mathcal{R}\;t\Vdash\varphi\}
. Using
Section

8
, it will be seen that, again under the same assumptions,
\textnormal{{T}}_{\in}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}
is a coherent theory extending
ZF
.

Finally, on occasion we will want to work internally within our realizability model. To do this, we will work in an arbitrary model of the theory
\textnormal{{T}}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}
. Since this theory extends
\ZFepsilon
we will use the same notation for an arbitrary model and the structure that has been defined in this section. That is, we will also use the notation
\mathcal{N}=(\textnormal{{N}},\mathop{\varepsilon},\in,\subseteq)
for an arbitrary model of the realizable theory.

The first principle that we shall realize is
Peirce’s Law
. This can be seen to be equivalent to the law of Excluded Middle and from this it will follow that
\mathcal{N}
satisfies a classical theory.

{proposition}

Suppose that
\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rVert
. Then for any formula
\psi
,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. In particular,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\Vdash\neg\varphi
.

Proof.

Suppose that
t\Vdash\varphi
and
\pi\in\lVert\psi\rVert
. Then
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\pi
so, since
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
by the assumption that
t\Vdash\varphi
,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

{proposition}

[Peirce’s Law]
For any formulas
\varphi
and
\psi\in Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
,
\textnormal{{{cc}} }\Vdash((\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow\varphi)\rightarrow\varphi
.

Proof.

Suppose that
t\Vdash(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow\varphi
and
\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rVert
. By
Section

9
,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
from which it follows that

\textnormal{{{cc}} }\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}.

∎

{definition}

A realizability algebra is said to be coherent (consistent) if for every realizer
t\in\mathcal{R}
there is a stack
\pi
such that
t\star\pi\not\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

{theorem}

A realizability algebra is consistent if and only if there is no realizer
t\in\mathcal{R}
which realizes the formula
\perp
.

9.1
Adding Defined Functions

Often when working directly in the theory
\ZFepsilon
we want to use predefined notions in order to simplify the definitions we want to realize. For example, that a function consists of ordered pairs. However, without Extensionality,
the
ordered pair of
a
and
b
is not a well-defined notion. That is, we can be in the situation where both
z
and
z^{\prime}
satisfy “
I am an ordered pair of
a
and
b
” but
z
and
z^{\prime}
are not equal.

In the realizability model we can regularly circumvent issues such as these by defining a
canonical
definition of the function we want. For example, in
Section

14
we will introduce canonical interpretations of singletons, unordered pairs and ordered pairs. In order to use such objects in definitions directly in
\ZFepsilon
, it is useful to extend the language of
\ZFepsilon
to include those operations which are uniformly realized.

{definition}

We say that a formula
\varphi(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},v)\in Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
is
\mathcal{A}
-definable by

f\colon\textnormal{{N}}^{n}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
if there exists a
realizer

t\in\mathcal{R}
such that for any
a_{1},\dots,a_{n},b\in\textnormal{{N}}
,

b=f(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad t\Vdash\varphi(a_{1},\dots,a_{n},b).

\varphi
is said to be
\mathcal{A}
-definable
if there exists such a function
f
and in this case we say
f

\mathcal{A}
-defines

\varphi
.

For example, we shall see that the function
\textnormal{{up}}\colon\textnormal{{N}}^{2}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
,

(u_{1},u_{2})\mapsto\{(u_{1},\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline\pi\in\Pi\}\cup\{(u_{2},\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline\pi\in\Pi\}

\mathcal{A}
-defines
an unordered pair. That is, there exists some realizer
t
such that for any
a_{1},a_{2}\in\textnormal{{N}}

t\Vdash\textnormal{{up}}(a_{1},a_{2})\text{ is (extensionally) equal to the unordered pair of }a_{1}\text{ and }a_{2}.

We note here that for the construction to go through in the realizability model, we need
uniformity
: there is a
single
realizer
t
which realizes the formula for
any
selection of parameters.

Given
\mathcal{A}
, we can then expand the language
\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
by adding a
n
-ary function symbol
\tilde{f}
whenever
f

\mathcal{A}
-defines some formula
\varphi\in Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
. Then in the realizability model we will interpret
\tilde{f}
as a “
universal lift
” (this will formally be defined in
Section

16.2
where we also prove it satisfies the desired properties, but this is essentially just a way to internalise the construction within the realizability model). It will then be the case that the universal lift of a function will extend the structural property that
f
satisfied. For example, we will have that

\mathcal{N}\Vdash\forall a_{1}\forall a_{2}(\textnormal{{up}}(a_{1},a_{2})\text{ is (extensionally) equal to the unordered pair of }a_{1}\text{ and }a_{2}).

From this it follows that we can add
\mathcal{A}
-definable functions to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
and reason with them as “canonical” witnesses to the properties we are trying to prove. In order to simplify notation, we will still refer to this extension as
\ZFepsilon
and implicitly assume that whenever
f

\mathcal{A}
-defines some formula then we have added a symbol to the language
\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
which will be interpreted as
\tilde{f}
.

10
Realizers and Predicate Logic

10.1
Realizers for Logical Connectives

We note here some useful propositions which we shall frequently use in later analysis.
Section

10.1
gives a
sufficient criterion to prove that the identity term,
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u
, witnesses an implication.
Sections

10.1
and
10.1
prove that the realizability model satisfies the BHK interpretations for implications and universal quantifiers.
Section

10.1
gives a sufficient condition to realizer an existential quantifier.
Section

10.1
gives a realizer for the conjunction of two formulas.
Section

10.1
tells us that if we can realize
\varphi
and
\neg\psi
then we can realize
\neg(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)
.
Section

10.1
gives us information on the relationship between realizers for
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
and
\neg\psi\rightarrow\neg\varphi
.
Section

10.1
gives an alternative way to express bounded universal quantifiers.

{proposition}

If
\lVert\varphi\rVert\supseteq\lVert\psi\rVert
then
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
.

Proof.

Take
t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rightarrow\psi\rVert=\{s\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\divline s\Vdash\varphi,\sigma\in\lVert\psi\rVert\}
. Since
\pi\in\lVert\psi\rVert\subseteq\lVert\varphi\rVert
,
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Therefore, since
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\pi
and the latter is in
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
,
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Hence, by definition,
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
.
∎

{proposition}

If
t\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
and
s\Vdash\varphi
, then
ts\Vdash\psi
.

Proof.

We need to prove that for every
\pi\in\lVert\psi\rVert
,
ts\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. So suppose that
t
and
s
satisfy the assumptions of the proposition and fix
\pi\in\lVert\psi\rVert
. Observe that
\lVert\varphi\rightarrow\psi\rVert=\{r\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\divline r\Vdash\varphi,\sigma\in\lVert\psi\rVert\}
and therefore
s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rightarrow\psi\rVert
. This means that
t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Therefore, the claim follows from the fact that
ts\star\pi\succ t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi
.
∎

{proposition}

t\Vdash\forall x\varphi(x)\Longleftrightarrow\forall a\in\textnormal{{N}}\,t\Vdash\varphi(a).

Proof.

First, assume that
t\Vdash\forall x\,\varphi(x)
. Fix
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
and
\pi\in\lVert\varphi(a)\rVert\subseteq\bigcup_{b\in\textnormal{{N}}}\lVert\varphi(b)\rVert=\lVert\forall x\varphi(x)\rVert
. Then,
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
and therefore
t\Vdash\varphi(a)
.

For the other direction, suppose that for every
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
,
t\Vdash\varphi(a)
. Fix
\pi\in\lVert\forall x\varphi(x)\rVert=\bigcup_{b\in\textnormal{{N}}}\lVert\varphi(b)\rVert
. Then, by definition, we can fix some
b\in\textnormal{{N}}
such that
\pi\in\lVert\varphi(b)\rVert
. Since,
t\Vdash\varphi(b)
by assumption,
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
and therefore
t\Vdash\forall x\varphi(x)
.
∎

{proposition}

If
t\Vdash\varphi(a)
for some
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}ut\Vdash\exists x\,\varphi(x)
.

Proof.

Note that
\exists x\,\varphi(x)
is an abbreviation for
(\forall x\,(\varphi(x)\rightarrow\perp))\rightarrow\perp
. So suppose that
t\Vdash\varphi(a)
for some
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
and suppose that
