s\Vdash\forall x\,(\varphi(x)\rightarrow\perp)
while
\pi\in\Pi
. This means that for any
\sigma\in\Pi
,
b\in\textnormal{{N}}
and term
r
, if
r\Vdash\varphi(b)
then
s\star r\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. In particular,
s\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Thus, since
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}ut\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ st\star\pi\succ s\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi
,
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}ut\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, proving the claim.
∎

{proposition}

If
t\Vdash\varphi
and
s\Vdash\psi
, then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(ut)s\Vdash\varphi\land\psi
.

Proof.

First, note that
\varphi\land\psi\equiv(\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\perp))\rightarrow\perp
. Now suppose that
r\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\perp)
and
\pi\in\Pi
. By construction we then have that
s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\psi\rightarrow\perp\rVert
and
t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\perp)\rVert
. Therefore,

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(ut)s\star r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ(rt)s\star\pi\succ r\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}.

∎

{proposition}

If
t\Vdash\varphi
and
s\Vdash\psi\rightarrow\perp
then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}s(ut)\Vdash(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow\perp
.

Proof.

Suppose that
r\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
and
\pi\in\Pi
. Then, by multiple uses of
Section

10.1
,
rt\Vdash\psi
and thus
s(rt)\Vdash\perp
, which means that
s(rt)\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Thus, since
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}s(ut)\star r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ s(rt)\star\pi
,
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}s(ut)\star r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, proving the claim.
∎

{proposition}

If
t\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}u(tv)\Vdash((\psi\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\perp))
. Moreover, if
t\Vdash((\psi\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\perp))
then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{{cc}} }(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(tk)u)\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
.

Proof.

Firstly, suppose that
t\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
,
s\Vdash\psi\rightarrow\perp
,
r\Vdash\varphi
and
\pi\in\Pi
. Then, by repeated use of
Section

10.1
,
tr\Vdash\psi
and
s(tr)\Vdash\perp
. Thus
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}u(tv)\star s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ s(tr)\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

For the second claim, suppose that
t\Vdash(\psi\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\perp)
,
s\Vdash\varphi
and
\pi\in\lVert\psi\rVert
. Then, by
Section

9
,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\Vdash\psi\rightarrow\perp
. Thus
t\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\perp
and hence
t\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Finally,

\displaystyle\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{{cc}} }(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(tk)u)\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(tk)s)\star\pi\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }\star(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(tk)s)\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(tk)s\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ(t\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi})s\star\pi\succ t\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}.

∎

Recall that we use
\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\varphi(x)
as an abbreviation for
\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)
. The next proposition gives an easier, alternative way to view realizers of bounded universal quantification. We observe here that the reverse direction requires an essential instance of
cc
along with an additional assumption on the falsity values.

{definition}

For a set
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
, we define the restricted quantifier
\forall x^{a}
to have the following meaning:

\lVert\forall x^{a}\varphi(x)\rVert=\{\pi\divline\exists b\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)\>\pi\in\lVert\varphi(b)\rVert\}=\bigcup_{b\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)}\lVert\varphi(b)\rVert.

{proposition}

i.

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}vu\Vdash\forall x^{a}\,\varphi(x)\rightarrow\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)
;

ii.

If
\lVert\varphi(b)\rVert\subseteq\lVert b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert
for every
b\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)
, then

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{{cc}} }(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}uk)\Vdash\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rightarrow\forall x^{a}\,\varphi(x)
.

Proof.

(i)
. Suppose that
t\Vdash\forall x^{a}\,\varphi(x)
,
s\Vdash\neg\varphi(b)
for some
b\in\textnormal{{N}}
and
\pi\in\lVert b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert
. Since
\lVert b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert=\{\sigma\divline(b,\sigma)\in a\}
and this is non-empty by assumption, we must have that
b\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)
. We need to prove that
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}vu\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
or, in other words, that
s\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. By the hypothesis on
t
, since
b\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)
,
t\star\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
for any
\sigma\in\lVert\varphi(b)\rVert
. Therefore, this gives us that
t\Vdash\varphi(b)
. Finally, since
s\Vdash\varphi(b)\rightarrow\perp
,
s\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
as desired.

(ii)
. Suppose that
t\Vdash\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)
and
\pi\in\lVert\forall x^{a}\,\varphi(x)\rVert
. First, fix
b\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)
such that
\pi\in\lVert\varphi(b)\rVert
. Now

\lVert\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rVert=\bigcup_{c\in\textnormal{{N}}}\lVert\neg\varphi(c)\rightarrow c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert=\bigcup_{c\in\textnormal{{N}}}\{s\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\divline s\Vdash\neg\varphi(c),\sigma\in\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert\}.

Therefore, for any
s\boldsymbol{.}\sigma
in the above set,
t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Next, since
\pi\in\lVert\varphi(b)\rVert
,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\Vdash\neg\varphi(b)
by
Section

9
. Also, by our initial assumptions,
\pi\in\lVert b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert
. Thus,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rVert
and
t\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Finally, the result follows by evaluating the realizer in the proposition:

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{{cc}} }(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}uk)\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}tk)\star\pi\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }\star(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}tk)\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}tk\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\star\pi\succ t\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi.

∎

Using the same proof as in
Section

10.1
, we immediately get the corollary that the quantifier
\forall x^{a}\varphi(x)
behaves as we would expect a bounded universal quantifier would.

{corollary}

For any term
t
,
t\Vdash\forall x^{a}\varphi(x)\Longleftrightarrow\forall b\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)\>t\Vdash\varphi(b)
. Therefore, if
\lVert\varphi(b)\rVert\subseteq\lVert b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert
for every
b\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)
then
t\Vdash\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\varphi(x)\Longleftrightarrow\forall b\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)\>t\Vdash\varphi(b)
.

10.2
Adequacy

We shall conclude this section by showing that any realizability model is closed under classical deductions, which is called the
adequacy lemma
by Krivine. From this and
Section

12
it will follow that any realizability model satisfies
\ZFepsilon
and thus the extensional part satsifes
ZF
. This will be done by proving that the structure is closed under a certain collection of logical axioms and inference rules, for more details on this we refer the reader to Chapter V of
[
Kle52
]
or Section 1.4 and 2.3 of
[
Men09
]
.

In
[
Men09
]
, Mendelson gives a definition of First Order Logic using only the symbols
\rightarrow
,
\neg
and
\forall
and proves that this is sufficient to build a system of natural deduction for first order logic. It is then shown that this is a semantically complete calculus in which first order classical logic can be expressed. The system is built from the following axioms, where we have replaced
\neg\varphi
with
\varphi\rightarrow\perp
, which hold for any well-founded formulas
\varphi
,
\psi
and
\theta
built from the symbols
\rightarrow
,
\perp
, variables and
\forall
.

Propositional Axioms

•

\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\varphi)
,

•

(\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\theta))\rightarrow((\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\theta))
,

•

((\varphi\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\perp))\rightarrow(((\varphi\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow\varphi)
.

First-order Axioms

•

\forall x\varphi\rightarrow\varphi[y/x]
where
y
is any term which may be substituted for
x
in
\varphi
,

•

\forall x(\varphi\rightarrow\psi(x))\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\forall x\psi)
where
x
does not occur freely in
\varphi
.

Rules of Inference

•

Modus Ponens:
\psi
follows from
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
and
\varphi
,

•

Universal Generalisation:
\forall x\varphi(x)
follows from
\varphi[y/x]
, where
x
appears free in
\varphi
and
y
is any term which may be substituted for
x
in
\varphi
.

{lemma}

Let
\varphi
be a formula in
Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
. If
\textnormal{{CPC}}\vdash\varphi
, where
CPC
denotes classical predicate calculus, then there exists a realizer
t
such that
t\Vdash\varphi
.

Proof.

Since the above set of axioms forms a semantically complete calculus for first order logic, it suffices to prove that each of the axioms can be realized for any formulas
\varphi
,
\psi
and
\theta
in
Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
.

Firstly, we see that
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}u\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\varphi)
. This is because any element of
\lVert\psi\rightarrow\varphi\rVert
is of the form
s\boldsymbol{.}\pi
where
s\Vdash\psi
and
\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rVert
. Therefore, if
t\Vdash\varphi
then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}u\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

For the second propositional axiom, a realizer for
(\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\theta))\rightarrow((\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\theta))
is
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}(uw)(vw)
. Suppose that
t\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\theta)
,
s\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
,
r\Vdash\varphi
and
\pi\in\lVert\theta\rVert
. Then, by
Section

10.1
,
sr\Vdash\psi
from which it follows that
r\boldsymbol{.}sr\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\theta)\rVert
and
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}(uw)(vw)\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ(tr)(sr)\star\pi\succ t\star r\boldsymbol{.}sr\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

For the final propositional axiom, a realizer for
((\varphi\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\perp))\rightarrow(((\varphi\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow\varphi)
will be
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{{cc}} }(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(uk)(vk))
. Suppose that
t\Vdash(\varphi\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow\perp)
,
s\Vdash(\varphi\rightarrow\perp)\rightarrow\psi
and
\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rVert
. Then, by
Section

9
,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\perp
and thus, by
Section

10.1
,
t\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\Vdash\psi\rightarrow\perp
and
s\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\Vdash\psi
. From this it follows that
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{{cc}} }(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(uk)(vk))\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(tk)(sk))\star\pi\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }\star(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(tk)(sk))\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(tk)(sk)\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ(t\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi})(s\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi})\star\pi\succ t\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\star s\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

Moving on to the axioms which discuss universal quantifiers, it is easy to see that the identity,
I
, is a realizer for both of the statements. For the second axiom, a realizer for
(\forall x\varphi\rightarrow\psi(x))\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\forall x\psi)
is
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}uv
. To see this, suppose that
t\Vdash\forall x(\varphi\rightarrow\psi(x))
,
s\Vdash\varphi
and
\pi\in\lVert\forall x\psi(x)\rVert
. Then we can fix
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
such that
\pi\in\lVert\psi(a)\rVert
. By definition of
t
and
Section

10.1
,
t\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi(a)
and thus
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

Finally, for the rules of inference, to prove Modus Ponens, one shows that whenever
t\Vdash\varphi
and
s\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
then there exists a term realizing
\psi
, which is the term
ts
by
Section

10.1
. For Universal Generalizations, suppose that
t\Vdash\varphi[y/x]
for some
y
which does not appear freely in
\varphi
. Then, for any
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
we must have that
t\Vdash\varphi(a)
and so, by
Section

10.1
,
t\Vdash\forall x\varphi(x)
.
∎

11
Equality and Elementhood

11.1
The relations
\subseteq
,
\mathop{\varepsilon}
and
\in

Here we show how to realize a few of the simple statements from
Section

8
which can be derived in
\ZFepsilon
.

{proposition}

Let
\theta=\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\big(v(uu)\big)\big(uu\big)
. Then
\theta\theta\Vdash\forall x\,(x\subseteq x)
.

Proof.

We shall prove this by induction on the rank of elements (in
V
) of
N
. So fix
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
and assume that the claim holds for every set of rank less than that of
a
. Since
\lVert z\not\in a\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert=\{t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline t\Vdash z\not\in a,(z,\pi)\in a\}
, we only need to consider the
z
in
\textnormal{{dom}}(a)
. So fix
b\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)
and
\pi
such that
(b,\pi)\in a
. By the inductive hypothesis,
\theta\theta\Vdash b\subseteq b
. Now suppose that
t\Vdash b\not\in a
. Then, by definition of
\lVert b\not\in a\rVert
,
t\star\theta\theta\boldsymbol{.}\theta\theta\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Finally,

\theta\theta[t]=\theta[u\coloneqq X,v\coloneqq t]=\big(t(\theta\theta)\big)\big(\theta\theta\big)

and therefore,

\theta\theta\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\big(t(\theta\theta)\big)\big(\theta\theta\big)\star\pi\succ t\star(\theta\theta)\boldsymbol{.}(\theta\theta)\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}.

∎

Using
Section

10.1
and the fact that
a\simeq b
is an abbreviation for
a\subseteq b\land b\subseteq a
, we can now immediately produce a realizer for
a\simeq a
,

{corollary}

Suppose that
t\Vdash\forall x(x\subseteq x)
, then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(ut)t\Vdash\forall x(x\simeq x)
.

{proposition}

Suppose that
t\Vdash\forall x(x\subseteq x)
, then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(ut)t\Vdash\forall x\forall y\,(x\not\in y\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}y)
.

Proof.

Fix
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
and suppose that
s\Vdash a\not\in b
and
\pi\in\lVert a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rVert
. Then
(a,\pi)\in b
while
t\Vdash a\subseteq a
. By definition, this means that
t\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert a\not\in b\rVert
and thus
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(ut)t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ s\star t\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

11.2
Non-extensional Equality

We next introduce two further symbols,
=
and
\xhookrightarrow{}
, whose additions will simplify many of the later arguments. The symbol
=
will be used to denote non-extensional
Leibniz
equality. Informally, we will have
a=b
if and only if for any property
P
,
P(a)\Leftrightarrow P(b)
, which is stronger than asserting that
a
and
b
have the same
\mathop{\varepsilon}
elements. The symbol
\xhookrightarrow{}
can be seen as an equational version of implication which will give a much simpler way to realize implications when the premise involves the symbol
=
.

{definition}

Suppose that
a,b
are closed terms and
\varphi
is a formula in
Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
. Then

\lVert a\neq b\rVert=\begin{cases}\lVert\perp\rVert\;(=\Pi)&\text{if }a=b\\
\lVert\top\rVert\;(=\emptyset)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and

\lVert a=b\xhookrightarrow{}\varphi\rVert=\begin{cases}\lVert\varphi\rVert&\text{if }a=b\\
\lVert\top\rVert&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

{remark}

As per usual,
a=b
should be read as an abbreviation for
a\neq b\rightarrow\perp
.

We shall now see that both of these symbols have an equivalent expression in the realizability model using the language
\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
.

{proposition}

i.

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash(a=b\rightarrow\varphi)\rightarrow(a=b\xhookrightarrow{}\varphi)
;

ii.

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{{cc}} }(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}v(ku))\Vdash(a=b\xhookrightarrow{}\varphi)\rightarrow(a=b\rightarrow\varphi)
.

Proof.

For the first claim, suppose that
t\Vdash a=b\rightarrow\varphi
while
\pi\in\lVert a=b\xhookrightarrow{}\varphi\rVert
. Since
\lVert\top\rVert=\emptyset
, we must have that
a=b
and
\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rVert
. Next, since
a=b
,
\lVert a\neq b\rVert=\emptyset
and therefore
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash a=b
(any other fixed term would also do). Thus,
t\star\textnormal{{I}}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. The conclusion then follows from the observation that
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\textnormal{{I}}\star\pi\succ t\star\textnormal{{I}}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
.

For the second claim, suppose that
t\Vdash a=b\xhookrightarrow{}\varphi
,
s\Vdash a=b
and
\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rVert
. We have that

\displaystyle\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{{cc}} }(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}v(ku))\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}s(kt))\star\pi\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }\star(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}s(kt))\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}s(kt)\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ s(\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t)\star\pi\succ s\star(\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t)\boldsymbol{.}\pi

and therefore it suffices to prove that
s\star(\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t)\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. To this end, it will suffice to prove that
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t\Vdash a\neq b
. Now, if
a=b
then
\lVert a=b\xhookrightarrow{}\varphi\rVert=\lVert\varphi\rVert
, so
t\Vdash\varphi
and therefore
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t\Vdash\perp
(since for any
\sigma\in\Pi
,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t\star\sigma\succ t\star\pi
) and thus
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t\Vdash a\neq b
. On the other hand, if
a\neq b
then
\lVert a\neq b\rVert=\lVert\top\rVert=\emptyset
, for which it follows that
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t\Vdash a\neq b
.
∎

{proposition}

[Leibniz Equality]

i.

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash a=b\xhookrightarrow{}\forall x(b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x\rightarrow a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)
;

ii.

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x(b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x\rightarrow a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)\rightarrow a=b
.

Proof.

If
a\neq b
then
\lVert a=b\xhookrightarrow{}\forall x(b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x\rightarrow a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)\rVert=\emptyset
, therefore it suffices to show that if
a=b
then
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x(b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x\rightarrow a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)
. But this follows immediately from the observation that for any
c\in\textnormal{{N}}
, if
t\Vdash b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c
then
t\Vdash a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c
since
a=b
.

For the second claim, suppose that
t\Vdash\forall x(b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x\rightarrow a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)
,
s\Vdash a\neq b
and
\pi\in\Pi
. Then, for any
c\in\textnormal{{N}}
we must have that
t\Vdash b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c\rightarrow a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c
. In particular, this holds for
c\coloneqq\{a\}\times\Pi
and, for this
c
,
\pi\in\lVert a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c\rVert
. If
a=b
then
\lVert a\neq b\rVert=\lVert\perp\rVert=\Pi
and therefore
s\Vdash\perp
. From this, it follows that
s\Vdash b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c
. On the other hand, if
a\neq b
then
\lVert a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c\rVert=\emptyset
and therefore we again have that
s\Vdash b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c
. Hence, we must have that
s\Vdash b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c
and
ts\Vdash a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c
, from which it follows that
t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

We can also show that the usual axioms of equality are realized for
=
, noting in
\thetheorem.(v)
that we only have one direction of the implication.

{proposition}

i.

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x(x=x)
;

ii.

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x\forall y\,(x=y\xhookrightarrow{}y=x)
;

iii.

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x\forall y\forall z\,(x=y\xhookrightarrow{}(y=z\xhookrightarrow{}x=z))
;

iv.

For any formula
\varphi(u)\in Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
,
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x\forall y\,(x=y\xhookrightarrow{}(\varphi(x)\rightarrow\varphi(y)))
;

v.

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x\forall y\,(x=y\xhookrightarrow{}\forall z(z\mathop{\varepsilon}x\leftrightarrow z\mathop{\varepsilon}y))
.

Proof.

i.

Fix
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
,
t\Vdash a\neq a
and
\pi\in\Pi
. Then
\pi\in\Pi=\lVert a\neq a\rVert
and therefore
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

ii.

If
a\neq b
then
\lVert a=b\xhookrightarrow{}b=a\rVert=\emptyset
and therefore
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash a=b\xhookrightarrow{}b=a
. On the other hand, if
a=b
then
\lVert a=b\xhookrightarrow{}b=a\rVert=\lVert b=a\rVert=\lVert b\neq a\rightarrow\perp\rVert=\{t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline t\Vdash b\neq a,\,\pi\in\Pi\}
. Therefore, since
\lVert b\neq a\rVert=\Pi
, if
t\Vdash b\neq a
and
\pi\in\Pi
, then
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
and hence
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

iii.

Fix
a,b,c\in\textnormal{{N}}
. If
a\neq b
then
\lVert a=b\xhookrightarrow{}(b=c\xhookrightarrow{}a=c)\rVert=\emptyset
and therefore
I
realizes the desired statement. So, suppose that
a=b
. Then we have
\lVert a=b\xhookrightarrow{}(b=c\xhookrightarrow{}a=c)\rVert\linebreak=\lVert b=c\xhookrightarrow{}a=c\rVert
. Again, if
b\neq c
then
\lVert b=c\xhookrightarrow{}a=c\rVert=\emptyset
and therefore
I
realizes the desired statement. So, suppose that
a=b=c
. Then
\lVert a=b\xhookrightarrow{}(b=c\xhookrightarrow{}a=c)\rVert=\linebreak\lVert a=c\rVert
and the same argument as in
(ii)
gives us that
I
realizes the desired statement.

iv.

As per usual, if
a\neq b
then the falsity value is empty and
I
realizes the statement. So, suppose that
a=b
. Then
\lVert a=b\xhookrightarrow{}(\varphi(a)\rightarrow\varphi(b))\rVert=\lVert\varphi(a)\rightarrow\varphi(b)\rVert
. But then if
t\Vdash\varphi(a)
and
\pi\in\lVert\varphi(b)\rVert=\lVert\varphi(a)\rVert
then
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
and thus
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
as required.

v.

Follows from
(iv)
.

∎

12
The axioms of
\ZFepsilon
are realized in
\mathcal{N}

We can now show that if
V
is a model of
ZF
and
\mathcal{A}=(\Lambda,\Pi,\prec,\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}})
is a realizability algebra then the realizability model
\mathcal{N}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}
is a model of
\ZFepsilon
. We shall realize each axiom in turn, following the proof structure from
[
Kri12
]
, often showing the equivalence by using the double negation translation of each of our axioms.

Extensionality

We begin by proving the first clause. That is, we shall show that

\displaystyle\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x\forall y\Big(x\not\in y\rightarrow\forall z\Big(x\subseteq z\rightarrow(z\subseteq x\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}y)\Big)\Big),

\displaystyle\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x\forall y\Big(\forall z\Big(x\subseteq z\rightarrow(z\subseteq x\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}y)\Big)\rightarrow x\not\in y\Big).

For this, by
Section

10.1
it will suffice to show that the two respective
\lVert\cdot\rVert
values are equal for any
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
. So fix
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
. Then,

\lVert\forall z(a\subseteq z\rightarrow(z\subseteq a\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b))\rVert=\bigcup_{c\in\textnormal{{N}}}\{t\boldsymbol{.}t^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline t\Vdash a\subseteq c,t^{\prime}\Vdash c\subseteq a,\pi\in\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rVert\}.

Next,

\lVert a\not\in b\rVert=\bigcup_{c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(b)}\{t\boldsymbol{.}t^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline(c,\pi)\in b,t\Vdash a\subseteq c,t^{\prime}\Vdash c\subseteq a\}.

But, by definition,
(c,\pi)\in b
if and only if
\pi\in\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rVert
. This means that the only sets
c
which can appear in the first union are those which are in
\textnormal{{dom}}(b)
and therefore these two sets are indeed equal. Which means that
I
realizes the desired implications.

Moving on to the second clause, this will be proved in the same way by showing that

\displaystyle\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x\forall y\Big(x\subseteq y\rightarrow\forall z(z\not\in y\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)\Big)

\displaystyle\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x\forall y\Big(\forall z(z\not\in y\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)\rightarrow x\subseteq y\Big)

For this, it will again suffice to show that the two respective
\lVert\cdot\rVert
values are equal for any
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
. So fix
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
. Then,

\lVert\forall z(z\not\in b\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rVert=\bigcup_{c\in\textnormal{{N}}}\{t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline t\Vdash c\notin b,\pi\in\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert\}

and

\lVert a\subseteq b\rVert=\bigcup_{c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)}\{t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline(c,\pi)\in a,t\Vdash c\not\in b\}.

From this is it again easy to see that there two sets are indeed equal which means that
I
realizes the desired implications.

Induction

Let
\psi(z,z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
be a formula, it what follows we shall drop the free variables for readability. We want to show that

\mathcal{N}\Vdash\forall x\big(\forall y(y\mathop{\varepsilon}x\rightarrow\psi(y))\rightarrow\psi(x)\big)\rightarrow\forall z\psi(z).

Taking
\varphi(z)\equiv\psi(z)\rightarrow\perp
, it will suffice to show that

\mathcal{N}\Vdash\forall x\big(\forall y(\varphi(y)\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)\rightarrow(\varphi(x)\rightarrow\perp)\big)\rightarrow\forall z(\varphi(z)\rightarrow\perp).

We shall prove this by making use of a “Turing fixed point combinator”.

{proposition}

Let
\textnormal{{A}}\coloneqq\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}v((uu)v)
and set
\textnormal{{Y}}\coloneqq\textnormal{{A}}\textnormal{{A}}
. Then for any
s,t\in\Lambda
and
\pi\in\Pi
,
\textnormal{{Y}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\textnormal{{Y}}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi
.

Proof.

\textnormal{{Y}}[v\coloneqq t]=\textnormal{{A}}[u\coloneqq\textnormal{{A}},v\coloneqq t]=t((AA)t)=t(\textnormal{{Y}}t)

Therefore,
\textnormal{{Y}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\textnormal{{Y}}[v\coloneqq t]\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi=t(\textnormal{{Y}}t)\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\textnormal{{Y}}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi
.
∎

{lemma}

For every formula
\varphi(u,u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
,

\textnormal{{Y}}\Vdash\forall x\Big(\forall y(\varphi(y)\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)\rightarrow(\varphi(x)\rightarrow\perp)\Big)\rightarrow\forall z(\varphi(z)\rightarrow\perp).

Proof.

Fix a term
t
such that
t\Vdash\forall x\Big(\forall y(\varphi(y)\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)\rightarrow(\varphi(x)\rightarrow\perp)\Big)
. Then for any set
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
we have

t\Vdash\Big(\forall y(\varphi(y)\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rightarrow(\varphi(a)\rightarrow\perp)\Big).

Since
\lVert\forall z(\varphi(z)\rightarrow\perp)\rVert=\bigcup_{b\in\textnormal{{N}}}\{s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline s\Vdash\varphi(b),\pi\in\Pi\}
, the proof will follow from the following lemma.

{lemma}

For any set
b\in\textnormal{{N}}
,
\pi\in\Pi
and
s\in\lvert\varphi(b)\rvert
,
\textnormal{{Y}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

We shall prove the claim by induction on the rank (in
V
) of
b
. So suppose that the claim holds for every
c\in\textnormal{{N}}
with
\textnormal{{rk}}(c)<\textnormal{{rk}}(b)
. Since
\textnormal{{Y}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\textnormal{{Y}}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi
, it suffices to prove that
t\star\textnormal{{Y}}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

Next, we observe that, by the assumptions on
t
and
s
, the claim will follow from proving that
\textnormal{{Y}}t\Vdash\forall y\,(\varphi(y)\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b)
. So fix
c\in\textnormal{{N}}
, and assume that
r\Vdash\varphi(c)
and
\sigma\in\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rVert
. Then
(c,\sigma)\in b
which gives us that
\textnormal{{rk}}(c)<\textnormal{{rk}}(b)
. Therefore, by the inductive hypothesis,
\textnormal{{Y}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, from which it follows that
\textnormal{{Y}}t\star r\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
as required.
∎

Separation

Let
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
and
\varphi
be a formula (with parameters). We shall prove that a set which realizes this related instance of Separation is
b\coloneqq\{(x,t\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline(x,\pi)\in a,t\Vdash\varphi(x)\}
. Now,

\lVert x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rVert=\{\sigma\in\Pi\divline(x,\sigma)\in b\}=\{t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline t\Vdash\varphi(x),\pi\in\Pi,(x,\pi)\in a\}=\lVert\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert.

Therefore,
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x(x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rightarrow(\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a))
and
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x((\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b).

Pairing

Fix
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
. We shall prove that a set which realizes this instance of pairing is
c\coloneqq\{a,b\}\times\Pi
. This is because
\lVert a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c\rVert=\lVert b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c\rVert=\Pi=\lVert\perp\rVert
. Therefore,
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash a\mathop{\varepsilon}c
and
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash b\mathop{\varepsilon}c
(note that
a\mathop{\varepsilon}c
is the formula
a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c\rightarrow\perp
).

Union

Fix
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
. We shall prove that a set which realizes this instance of unions is
b\coloneqq\{(c,\sigma)\divline\exists(x,\pi)\in a\,(c,\sigma)\in x\}
. By
Section

10.1
, it will suffice to prove that for any
x,c\in\textnormal{{N}}
,

\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert\subseteq\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert

because from this we will obtain
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x\forall c\Big((c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rightarrow(c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\Big)
which is equivalent to the axiom of unions.

So suppose that
s\Vdash c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b
and
(x,\pi)\in a
. We first prove that
s\Vdash c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x
. To see this, take
\sigma\in\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x\rVert
. Then, by definition,
(c,\sigma)\in x
from which it follows that
(c,\sigma)\in b
and therefore
s\star\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, which implies that
s\Vdash c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x
. Therefore,
s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert
as required.

Weak Power Set

Fix
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
. We shall prove that a set which realizes this instance of weak power set is
b\coloneqq\mathcal{P}(\textnormal{{dom}}(a)\times\Pi)\times\Pi
. To do this, given
x\in\textnormal{{N}}
, let

y_{x}\coloneqq\{(z,t\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline t\Vdash z\mathop{\varepsilon}x,(z,\pi)\in a\}\in\mathcal{P}(\textnormal{{dom}}(a)\times\Pi).

Then
\lVert z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}y_{x}\rVert=\lVert z\mathop{\varepsilon}x\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert
and
\lVert y_{x}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rVert=\Pi
. So,
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall z(z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}y_{x}\rightarrow(z\mathop{\varepsilon}x\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a))
and
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall z((z\mathop{\varepsilon}x\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}y_{x})
. Therefore, for any
\pi\in\Pi
,

\textnormal{{I}}\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{I}}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\forall y\big(\forall z(z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}y\rightarrow(z\mathop{\varepsilon}x\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a))\rightarrow\big(\forall z((z\mathop{\varepsilon}x\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}y)\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\big)\big)\rVert.

From which it follows that

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(u\textnormal{{I}})\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x\bigg(\forall y\bigg(\forall z(z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}y\rightarrow(z\mathop{\varepsilon}x\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a))\\
\rightarrow\Big(\forall z((z\mathop{\varepsilon}x\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}y)\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\Big)\bigg)\rightarrow\perp\bigg).

Collection

Let
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
and
\varphi(u,v)
be a formula (with parameters). Using Collection in
V
we can find a set
Y
such that

\forall(x,\pi)\in a\,\forall t\in\Lambda\,\exists y\,t\Vdash\varphi(x,y)\rightarrow\forall(x,\pi)\in a\,\forall t\in\Lambda\,\exists y\in Y\,t\Vdash\varphi(x,y).

We shall prove that a set which realizes the instance of collection for
a
and
\varphi
in
\mathcal{N}
is
b\coloneqq\{(y,\pi)\divline\exists t\in\Lambda,\exists x\,(x,\pi)\in a,t\Vdash\varphi(x,y),y\in Y\}.
For this, it will suffice to prove that for any
x\in\textnormal{{N}}

\lVert\forall y(\varphi(x,y)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rVert\subseteq\lVert\forall y(\varphi(x,y)\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b)\rVert

as this will then imply that
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x\Big(\forall y(\varphi(x,y)\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b)\rightarrow\forall y(\varphi(x,y)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\Big)
which one can see is equivalent to Collection. So, fix
x\in\textnormal{{N}}
and suppose that
t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\forall y(\varphi(x,y)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rVert=\bigcup_{c\in\textnormal{{N}}}\lVert\varphi(x,c)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rVert
. Then, for some
c\in\textnormal{{N}}
,
t\Vdash\varphi(x,c)
and
(x,\pi)\in a
. By definition of
Y
, we can fix some
c^{\prime}\in Y
such that
t\Vdash\varphi(x,c^{\prime})
. Moreover, by definition,
(c^{\prime},\pi)\in b
and therefore
\pi\in\lVert c^{\prime}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rVert
. From which we can conclude that
t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\varphi(x,c^{\prime})\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c^{\prime}\rVert\subseteq\lVert\forall y(\varphi(x,y)\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b)\rVert
as required.

Infinity

Note that the axiom of infinity is equivalent to

\forall a\,\exists b\,\Big(a\mathop{\varepsilon}b\land\forall x(\forall y(x\mathop{\varepsilon}y\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b)\Big).

Fix
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
. We shall define a set
b
witnessing the axiom of infinity as follows: first set
a^{0}\coloneqq a
and, for
n\in\omega
, let
a^{n+1}\coloneqq\{a^{n}\}\times\Pi
.
6
6

6

this will in fact be a name for a set which is extensionally equal to
\{a\}
by
Section

14.1
Finally, let

b\coloneqq\{(a^{n},\pi)\divline n\in\omega,\pi\in\Pi\}.

Since
\{a\}\times\Pi\subseteq b
,
\lVert a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rVert=\Pi
and therefore
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash a\mathop{\varepsilon}b
. Therefore, it will suffice to prove that

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x(\forall y(x\mathop{\varepsilon}y\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b).

So, fix
c
and suppose that we can find some
t
and
\pi
such that
t\Vdash\forall y(c\mathop{\varepsilon}y\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b)
and
\pi\in\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rVert
. Note that, by definition,
(c,\pi)\in b
. For our witness
y
we shall take
d=\{c\}\times\Pi
, noting that for any
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi
, we shall have that
(d,\pi^{\prime})\in b
.

We shall first prove that
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}d\rightarrow\perp
. To do this suppose
s\Vdash c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}d
and
\sigma\in\Pi
. By definition,
\forall\tau\in\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}d\rVert=\{\pi^{\prime}\divline(c,\pi^{\prime})\in d\}=\Pi
,
s\star\tau\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Therefore,
\textnormal{{I}}\star s\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\succ s\star\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, which completes this observation. Then, since
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash c\mathop{\varepsilon}d
,
t\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash d\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b
and thus,
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\textnormal{{I}}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, which completes the proof.

We can now conclude that our realizability model satisfies
\ZFepsilon
.

{theorem}

Suppose that
\textnormal{{V}}\models\textnormal{{ZF}}
and
\mathcal{A}=(\Lambda,\Pi,\prec,\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}})
is a realizability algebra. Then for every axiom
\varphi
of
\ZFepsilon
,
\mathcal{N}^{\mathcal{A},\textnormal{{V}}}\Vdash\varphi
.

13
Names and Ordinals

13.1
Reish Names

In this short subsection we introduce a way to interpret every ground model set in the realizability model, by defining the
\emph{reishof}a
, where reish stands for
recursive
. In the traditional presentation where the sets of the realizability model are all sets in the ground model, one considers the gimel of
a
,
\text{\Large\cjRL{g}}(a)
, defined by
\text{\Large\cjRL{g}}(a)\coloneqq a\times\Pi
. The issue with this operation is that if
a
is not a name in
N
, then in the extensional model
\text{\Large\cjRL{g}}(a)
may be very impoverished. Notably, if
a
does not contain any pair of the form
(b,\pi)
where
\pi\in\Pi
then one can easily see that
\lVert\forall x(x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rVert=\emptyset
from which it follows that in the extensional model
\Vdash a\simeq\emptyset
. In particular, from this we can conclude that for any ordinal
\alpha
,
\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{g}}(\alpha)}(x\simeq 0)
.

Instead, we shall consider
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})
which is constructed by applying the gimel process recursively to all elements of
a
. This is a very blunt tool which in general will be difficult to work with because for any
b\in a
,
\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})\rVert=\Pi
. This will cause problems in practice because if
\pi\in\lVert\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})}\varphi(x)\rVert
then we cannot specify the
b\in a
for which
\pi\in\lVert\varphi(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b}))\rVert
. Therefore, in order to have
t\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})}\varphi(x)
one needs
t
to uniformly realize
t\Vdash\varphi(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b}))
for all
b\in a
without reference to which witness we are working with. For example, it is not known if it is possible to prove

\forall n\mathop{\varepsilon}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\omega})\,\forall x((\forall y(y+1\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)\rightarrow n\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)\rightarrow\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x),

which would imply that every element of
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\omega})
was an integer and therefore that
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\omega})
was extensionaly equal to the first limit ordinal. However, it is a good starting point to work from and we will consider an alternative approach in
13.3
.

{definition}

We define an interpretation
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x})
recursively for
x\in\textnormal{{V}}
as

\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x})\coloneqq\{(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({y}),\pi)\divline y\in x,\,\pi\in\Pi\}.

{proposition}

If
a\subseteq b
then
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})\subseteq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})
.

Proof.

Fix
t
to be a realizer such that
t\Vdash\forall x(x\subseteq x)
(for example, see
Section

11.1
) and fix
s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})\subseteq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})\rVert=\bigcup_{c}\{s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),\pi)\in\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a}),s\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c})\not\in\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})\}
. Now, if
(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),\pi)\in\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})
, then we must have that
c\in a
and therefore
c\in b
. Thus,
t\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c})\not\in\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})\rVert
which means that
s\star t\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Thus,
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(ut)t\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})\subseteq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})
.
∎

{observation}

If
a\in b
then
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})\mathop{\varepsilon}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})
. Thus, if
a\in b
then
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})\not\simeq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})
.

13.2
Ordinals

We now wish to discuss ordinals in
\ZFepsilon
. In order to do this, we will first specify precisely what we mean by this concept. For the purpose of this section, we will be very careful to differentiate between the
\mathop{\varepsilon}
version and the
\in
version of each definition.

{definition}

(
\ZFepsilon
) We say that a set
a
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-ordinal
if it is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-transitive set of
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-transitive sets. That is,

\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\forall y\mathop{\varepsilon}x\,(y\mathop{\varepsilon}a)\quad\land\quad\forall z\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}z\,\forall y\mathop{\varepsilon}x\,(y\mathop{\varepsilon}z).

Note that over
\ZFepsilon
,
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-ordinals are not always the same as the standard definition of ordinals as transitive sets well-ordered by the
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-relation. For example, if
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
has size greater than 2 then there are two ordinals,
a
and
b
, such that
a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b
,
b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a
but
(a\cup\{a\})\cup(b\cup\{b\})
is an
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-ordinal on which the
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-relation does not linearly order the set. On the other hand, it is easy to see in
ZF
that
\alpha
is an ordinal if and only if it is a transitive set of transitive sets.

{proposition}

(\ZFepsilon)

i.

If
a
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-transitive set, then it is a
\in
-transitive set.

ii.

If
a
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-ordinal, then it is a
\in
-ordinal.

Proof.

Suppose that
a
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-transitive set and take
c\in b\in a
. Then there exists some
x\mathop{\varepsilon}a
such that
x\simeq b
and there exists some
y\mathop{\varepsilon}x
such that
y\simeq c
. Since
a
is assumed to be
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-transitive,
y\mathop{\varepsilon}a
. Therefore
x\in a
by definition of
\in
.

Next, suppose that
a
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-ordinal. We have already shown that
a
is
\in
-transitive, so it suffices to prove that every
b\in a
is
\in
-transitive. So let
d\in c\in b\in a
. Then we can find
z\mathop{\varepsilon}y\mathop{\varepsilon}x\mathop{\varepsilon}a
such that
x\simeq b
,
y\simeq c
and
z\simeq d
. Since
a
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-ordinal,
z\mathop{\varepsilon}x
and therefore
d\in x
. Finally,
d\in x
and
x\simeq b
gives us
d\in b
, as required.
∎

{proposition}

There exists a realizer
\theta\in\mathcal{R}
such that whenever
\delta
is an ordinal in
V
then
\theta\Vdash``\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\delta})\text{ is a }\mathop{\varepsilon}\text{-ordinal''}
.

Proof.

Let
\delta
be an ordinal in
V
. We shall show that
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\delta})
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-transitive set. The fact that it consists of
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-transitive sets will follow by a similar argument. To do this, we shall show that

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\delta})}\forall y(y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\delta})\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x).

To do this, fix
\beta\in\delta
,
c\in\textnormal{{N}}
,
t\Vdash c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\delta})
and
\pi\in\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\beta})\rVert
. Now
\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\beta})\rVert=\{\sigma\divline(c,\sigma)\in\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\beta})\}
. So, since this set is non-empty, it must be the case that
\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\beta})\rVert=\Pi
and
c=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\gamma})
for some
\gamma\in\beta
. Therefore,
\lVert c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\delta})\rVert=\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\gamma})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\delta})\rVert=\Pi
and therefore
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, from which the result follows.
∎

13.3
Ordinal Representations

We now present an alternative method to represent certain ordinals in the realizability model which will give a much more usable interpretation. This interpretation will give a representation for every ordinal less than the size of the realizability model. This method is explored in much more detail in
[
FG20
]
where it was shown that the objects in the realizability model named by these ordinals behave like their ground model counterparts.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(\Lambda_{(A,B)},\Pi_{(A,B)},\prec,\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}})
be a realizability algebra where
|A|=\kappa
is infinite and
|B|\leq\kappa
and let
(\nu_{\alpha}\divline\alpha\in\kappa)
be an enumeration of
\Lambda
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that for every
n\in\omega
,
\nu_{n}=\underline{n}
with just the small caveat that if
\kappa=\omega
then this will in fact be an enumeration of order type
\omega+\omega
rather than
|\Lambda|
.

{definition}

For
\alpha\in\kappa
, let
\hat{\alpha}\coloneqq\{(\hat{\beta},\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline\pi\in\Pi,\beta\in\alpha\}
.

{remark}

The precise ordering for the term structure does not matter, what is important is that we have a way to “decode” which element of
\hat{\alpha}
we have picked. Namely, if
\lVert x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha}\rVert\neq\emptyset
then it must be of the form
\{\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline\pi\in\Pi\}
for some
\beta\in\alpha
and therefore our falsity value tells us which element of
\hat{\alpha}
our
x
is meant to correspond to.

{proposition}

If
\beta<\alpha\leq\kappa
then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\nu_{\beta}\Vdash\hat{\beta}\mathop{\varepsilon}\hat{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
t\Vdash\hat{\beta}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha}
and
\pi\in\Pi
. Then, by definition, we have that
\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\hat{\beta}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha}\rVert
from which it follows that
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\nu_{\beta}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\nu_{\beta}\star\pi\succ t\star\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

Recall
Section

10.1
where we defined the restricted quantifier
\forall x^{a}
to have the falsity value

\lVert\forall x^{a}\varphi(x)\rVert=\{\pi\divline\exists b\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)\,\pi\in\lVert\varphi(b)\rVert\}

and then proved that this behaves as a restricted quantifier in certain cases. Here we want to modify the definition of the restricted quantifier to take into account the
\nu_{\alpha}
. In order to ease notation we shall use the same notation for both these notions and note that it will always be clear from the context which of these interpretations we are using.

{definition}

For
\alpha\leq\kappa
, we define the restricted quantifier
\forall x^{\hat{\alpha}}
to have the following meaning:

\lVert\forall x^{\hat{\alpha}}\varphi(x)\rVert=\bigcup_{\beta\in\alpha}\{\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline\pi\in\lVert\varphi(\hat{\beta})\rVert\}.

As before, we can see that this behaves as the bounded quantifier.

{proposition}

For every
\alpha\leq\kappa
,

i.

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}v(uw)\Vdash\forall x^{\hat{\alpha}}\,\varphi(x)\rightarrow\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha})
;

ii.

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{{cc}} }\,u\Vdash\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha})\rightarrow\forall x^{\hat{\alpha}}\,\varphi(x)
.

Proof.

(i)
. Suppose that
t\Vdash\forall x^{\hat{\alpha}}\,\varphi(x)
,
s\Vdash\neg\varphi(b)
for some
b\in\textnormal{{N}}
and
\pi\in\lVert b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha}\rVert
. Since
\lVert b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha}\rVert=\{\sigma\divline(b,\sigma)\in\hat{\alpha}\}
and this is non-empty by assumption, we must have that
b=\hat{\beta}
for some
\beta\in\alpha
and
\pi=\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi^{\prime}
for some
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi
. We need to prove that
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}v(uw)\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi^{\prime}\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
or, in other words, that
s\star(t\nu_{\beta})\boldsymbol{.}\pi^{\prime}\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. By the hypothesis on
t
, since
\beta\in\alpha
,
t\star\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
for any
\sigma\in\lVert\varphi(\hat{\beta})\rVert
. Therefore, this gives us that
t\nu_{\beta}\Vdash\varphi(\hat{\beta})
. Finally, since
s\Vdash\varphi(\hat{\beta})\rightarrow\perp
,
s\star t\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
as desired.

(ii)
. Suppose that
t\Vdash\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha})
and
\sigma\in\lVert\forall x^{\hat{\alpha}}\,\varphi(x)\rVert
. First, fix
\beta\in\alpha
and
\pi\in\Pi
such that
\sigma=\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\varphi(\hat{\beta})\rVert
. Now

\lVert\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha})\rVert=\bigcup_{c\in\textnormal{{N}}}\lVert\neg\varphi(c)\rightarrow c\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha}\rVert=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\alpha}\{s\boldsymbol{.}\nu_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\divline s\Vdash\neg\varphi(\hat{\gamma}),\nu_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\lVert\hat{\gamma}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha}\rVert\}.

Therefore, for any
s\boldsymbol{.}\nu_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma
in the above set,
t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\nu_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Next, since
\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\varphi(\hat{\beta})\rVert
,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi}\Vdash\neg\varphi(\hat{\beta})
by
Section

9
. Thus,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha})\rVert
and
t\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Finally, the result follows by evaluating the realizer in the proposition:

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{{cc}} }\,u\star t\boldsymbol{.}\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }\,t\star\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }\star t\boldsymbol{.}\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}.

∎

{proposition}

There exists a realizer
\theta\in\mathcal{R}
such that for every
\alpha\leq\kappa
,

\theta\Vdash``\hat{\alpha}\text{ is a }\mathop{\varepsilon}\text{-ordinal''}.

Proof.

As in the case for
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({\alpha})
we shall show that
\hat{\alpha}
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-transitive set. The fact that it consists of
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-transitive sets will follow by a similar argument. To do this, we shall show that

\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}w(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}uk)\Vdash\forall x^{\hat{\alpha}}\forall y(y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha}\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x).

First, observe that
\lVert\forall x^{\hat{\alpha}}\forall y(y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha}\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)\rVert=\bigcup_{\beta\in\alpha}\bigcup_{\gamma\in\beta}\{\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}\nu_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline t\Vdash\hat{\gamma}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha},\,\pi\in\Pi\}
. So, fix
\gamma<\beta<\alpha
,
\pi\in\Pi
and
t\Vdash\hat{\gamma}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\alpha}
. Since
\gamma\in\alpha
, by
Section

13.3
,
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\nu_{\gamma}\Vdash\hat{\gamma}\mathop{\varepsilon}\hat{\alpha}
and therefore

\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}w(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}uk)\star\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}\nu_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\nu_{\gamma})\star\pi\succ t\star(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\nu_{\gamma})\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}.

∎

A key difference between
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-ordinals and
\in
-ordinals is the principle of
trichotomy
; for any
\in
-ordinal
\alpha
, if
\beta,\gamma\in\alpha
then either
\beta\in\gamma
,
\gamma\in\beta
or
\beta\simeq\gamma
. We end this section with a discussion of
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-ordinals which satisfy
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-trichotomy
. Such ordinals are particularly useful because they have a much richer structure within the non-extensional language. For example, we shall see that such ordinals contain unique representatives of their
\in
-elements and satisfy the
Least Ordinal Principle
; if
X
is a non-empty subset of
\hat{\alpha}
then
X
has a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-least element. To ease notation it is useful to add in the symbol
\subseteq_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
for non-extensional subsets.

{definition}

(\ZFepsilon)

a\subseteq_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}b
is an abbreviation for the formula
\forall x(x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\rightarrow x\mathop{\varepsilon}b)
.

{definition}

(\ZFepsilon)
We say that a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-ordinal
a
is
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Trichotomous
if

\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\forall y\mathop{\varepsilon}a(x\mathop{\varepsilon}y\lor x=y\lor y\mathop{\varepsilon}x).

{proposition}

\ZFepsilon\vdash\forall a(a\text{ is a }\mathop{\varepsilon}\text{-Trichotomous ordinal }\rightarrow\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\forall y\mathop{\varepsilon}a(x\simeq y\rightarrow x=y)).

Proof.

Suppose that
a
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Trichotomous ordinal and fix
x,y\mathop{\varepsilon}a
such that
x\neq y
. Then, by trichotomy, we must have that either
x\mathop{\varepsilon}y
or
y\mathop{\varepsilon}x
from which it follows that either
x\in y
or
y\in x
. But, from either of these cases it follows that
x\not\simeq y
, otherwise we would obtain the contradictory statement
x\in x
.
∎

{proposition}

[Least Ordinal Principle]

\ZFepsilon\vdash\forall a\forall X(a\text{ is a }\mathop{\varepsilon}\text{-Trichotomous ordinal }\land X\subseteq_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}a\land\exists z(z\mathop{\varepsilon}X)\\
\longrightarrow\quad\exists z\mathop{\varepsilon}X\forall y\mathop{\varepsilon}X(z\mathop{\varepsilon}y\lor z=y)).

Proof.

Suppose that
a
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Trichotomous ordinal and fix
X\subseteq_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}a
such that
\exists z(z\mathop{\varepsilon}X)
. Using
\in
-Foundation (which follows classically from
\in
-Induction) we can fix
z\mathop{\varepsilon}X
such that
z\cap X\simeq\emptyset
, that is
\forall y\neg(y\in z\land y\in X)
. To see that this
z
witnesses the claim, by trichotomy it suffices to show that
\forall y\mathop{\varepsilon}X(y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}z)
. But, if
y\mathop{\varepsilon}X
then
y\in X
and therefore
y\not\in z
by
\in
-minimality of
z
. Thus,
y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}z
.
∎

One would like to show that for any
\alpha\in\kappa
,
\hat{\alpha}
is a trichotomous
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-ordinal. However, in order to do that we need to add a special instruction,
\chi
, which allows one to compare the indices of ordinals. This is done in section 3 of
[
FG20
]
.

{definition}

Let
\chi\in\Lambda
be a special instruction. We extend the order
\prec
to be the smallest pre-order on
\Lambda\star\Pi
such that for any
\alpha,\beta\in\kappa
,
t,s,r\in\Lambda
and
\pi\in\Pi
,

\chi\star\nu_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{.}\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\begin{cases}t\star\pi&\text{if }\ \alpha<\beta,\\
s\star\pi&\text{if }\ \alpha=\beta,\\
r\star\pi&\text{if }\ \beta<\alpha.\end{cases}

{proposition}

Suppose that
\chi\in\Lambda
and
\prec
has been expanded to satisfy
Section

13.3
. Then there exists a realizer
\theta\in\mathcal{R}
such that for every
\alpha\leq\kappa
,

\theta\Vdash``\hat{\alpha}\text{ is a }\mathop{\varepsilon}\text{-Trichotomous ordinal''}.

Proof.

It will suffice to show that

\lambda b\boldsymbol{.}\lambda c\boldsymbol{.}\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}((((\chi b)c)(ub))(v))(wc)\Vdash\forall x^{\hat{\alpha}}\,\forall y^{\hat{\alpha}}(x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}y\rightarrow(x\neq y\rightarrow(y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x\rightarrow\perp))).

Fix
\beta,\gamma\in\kappa
and set
\eta=\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}((((\chi\nu_{\beta})\nu_{\gamma})(u\nu_{\beta}))(v))(w\nu_{\gamma})
. Fix
\beta,\gamma\in\kappa
,
t\Vdash\hat{\beta}\mathop{\varepsilon}\hat{\gamma}
,
s\Vdash\hat{\beta}\neq\hat{\gamma}
,
r\Vdash\hat{\gamma}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\hat{\beta}
and
\pi\in\Pi
. If
\beta<\gamma
then
\eta\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\nu_{\beta}\star\pi\succ t\star\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
since
(\hat{\beta},\nu_{\beta}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\in\hat{\gamma}
. Similarly, if
\gamma<\beta
then
\eta\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ r\star\nu_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Finally, if
\beta=\gamma
then
\lVert\hat{\beta}\neq\hat{\gamma}\rVert=\Pi
and therefore,
\eta\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ s\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

{remark}

If one restricts
\chi
to only comparing indices of Church numbers then
\chi
can in fact be constructed as a
\lambda
-term without the need for special instructions (see
Section

21
for the details). From this it follows that
\hat{\omega}
is always a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Trichotomous ordinal.

It is further shown in
[
FG20
]
that any realizability model
\mathcal{N}
satisfies “
\hat{n}
is the
n
th
ordinal” and “
\hat{\omega}
is the first limit ordinal”.

14
Implementing Pairing

The next problem we want to address is how to discuss functions in the realizability model. There are two main issues associated with this problem; what do we mean by a function, and given a function in the ground model is it possible to “lift” it to a function in the realizability model. A very typical example of the question of lifting is when given a function
1_{E}\colon a\rightarrow 2
defined by
1_{E}(x)=1
if
x\in E
and
1_{E}(x)=0
otherwise. The lifting of such functions to
\mathcal{N}
, in particular the trivial Boolean algebra operations on
\{0,1\}
, are used to prove statements about the Boolean algebra
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
. In order to address these problems, we first need a method to implement unordered and ordered pairs.

Traditionally, the ordered pair of two sets
a
and
b
,
(a,b)
, is defined to be
\{\{a,b\},\{a\}\}
. However, this turns out to be a difficult definition to encode because of the difficulty in using disjunctions in the realizability model. Instead we shall first give an encoding for unordered pairs which uses the two distinct
\lambda_{c}
-terms
\underline{0}
and
\underline{1}
to distinguish the elements of the pairs. Then we shall implement Wiener’s definition of ordered pairs, which is
\{\{\{a\},\emptyset\},\{\{b\}\}\}
. This will turn out to work because it will assign different structures to
a
from
b
, allowing us to identify which one is which. Note that the specific coding mechanism used is not important because internally within our realizability models we could easily move between different implementations of ordered pairs.

14.1
Unordered Pairs

We begin with Krivine’s implementation of singletons in the realizability model from
[
Kri12
]
. After this we will give an implementation of unordered pairs. Recall that in the proof of Pairing in
\mathcal{N}
the name for the pair of the sets
a
and
b
was
\{a,b\}\times\Pi
, however it will turn out to be beneficial to take a different implementation in order to be able to distinguish
a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\{a,b\}
from
b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\{a,b\}
.

{definition}

For
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
,
\textnormal{{sng}}(a)\coloneqq\{a\}\times\Pi
.

{theorem}

The following are realizable in
\mathcal{N}
:

i.

\forall x\forall y(\textnormal{{sng}}(x)=\textnormal{{sng}}(y)\xhookrightarrow{}x=y)
;

ii.

\forall x(\textnormal{{sng}}(x)\not\simeq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))
;

iii.

\forall x\forall y(x\in\textnormal{{sng}}(y)\rightarrow x\simeq y)
;

iv.

\forall x\forall y(x\simeq y\rightarrow\textnormal{{sng}}(x)\simeq\textnormal{{sng}}(y))
;

v.

\forall x\forall y(\textnormal{{sng}}(x)\simeq\textnormal{{sng}}(y)\rightarrow x\simeq y)
.

Proof.

i
.

Fix
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
, we shall prove that
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(a)=\textnormal{{sng}}(b)\xhookrightarrow{}a=b
. If
\textnormal{{sng}}(a)\neq\textnormal{{sng}}(b)
then
\lVert\textnormal{{sng}}(a)=\textnormal{{sng}}(b)\xhookrightarrow{}a=b\rVert=\emptyset
and the result trivially follows. So suppose that
\textnormal{{sng}}(a)=\textnormal{{sng}}(b)
. Then we obviously have that
a=b
and thus
\lVert\textnormal{{sng}}(a)=\textnormal{{sng}}(b)\xhookrightarrow{}a=b\rVert=\lVert a=b\rVert=\{t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline\pi\in\Pi,\,\forall\sigma\in\Pi(t\star\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}})\}
. Therefore,
I
realizes the desired statement.

ii
.

Fix
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
, it will suffice to prove that
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(a)\subseteq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\rightarrow\perp
. So suppose that
t\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(a)\subseteq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
and
\pi\in\Pi
. Then, by the proof of Extensionality from
Section

12
, we have that
t\Vdash\forall z(z\not\in\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{sng}}(a))
. By
Section

10.1
, this in particular gives
t\Vdash a\not\in\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\rightarrow a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{sng}}(a)
. Now
\lVert a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{sng}}(a)\rVert=\Pi
while
\lVert a\not\in\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\rVert=\emptyset
, because
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})=\emptyset
. Thus
t\star\textnormal{{I}}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, and the result follows.

iii
.

Fix
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
. It will suffice to prove that

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash a\not\simeq b\rightarrow a\not\in\textnormal{{sng}}(b).

So suppose that
t\Vdash a\not\simeq b
and
\pi\in\lVert a\not\in\textnormal{{sng}}(b)\rVert
. Then
\pi=s\boldsymbol{.}s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma
where
(b,\sigma)\in\textnormal{{sng}}(b)
,
s\Vdash a\subseteq b
and
s^{\prime}\Vdash b\subseteq a
. But this gives us that
t\star s\boldsymbol{.}s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
and thus
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi
is also in
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

iv
.

Fix
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
, it will suffice to prove that

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}(wu)v\Vdash a\subseteq b\rightarrow(b\subseteq a\rightarrow(\textnormal{{sng}}(a)\subseteq\textnormal{{sng}}(b))).

To do this, suppose that
t\Vdash a\subseteq b
,
s\Vdash b\subseteq a
,
(c,\pi)\in\textnormal{{sng}}(a)
and
r\Vdash c\not\in\textnormal{{sng}}(b)
. Since
\textnormal{{sng}}(a)=\{a\}\times\Pi
, we must have that
c=a
and therefore
r\Vdash a\not\in\textnormal{{sng}}(b)
. However, since
(b,\pi)\in\textnormal{{sng}}(b)
,
t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert a\not\in\textnormal{{sng}}(b)\rVert
. Thus,
r\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, completing the proof.

v
.

Fix
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
, it will suffice to show that

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{{cc}} }\,u\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(a)\subseteq\textnormal{{sng}}(b)\rightarrow a\subseteq b.

We also note that a very similar argument could be used to show that
\textnormal{{sng}}(a)\subseteq\textnormal{{sng}}(b)\rightarrow b\subseteq a
is also realizable.

So, fix
t\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(a)\subseteq\textnormal{{sng}}(b)
and
\pi\in\lVert a\subseteq b\rVert
. Then
t\Vdash\forall z(z\not\in\textnormal{{sng}}(b)\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{sng}}(a))
so, in particular,
t\Vdash a\not\in\textnormal{{sng}}(b)\rightarrow a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{sng}}(a)
. Now, by definition,
\lVert a\not\in\textnormal{{sng}}(b)\rVert=

\lVert a\subseteq b\rightarrow(b\subseteq a\rightarrow b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{sng}}(b))\rVert
. But, by
Section

9
,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\Vdash a\subseteq b\rightarrow(b\subseteq a\rightarrow b\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{sng}}(b))
and therefore
\textnormal{{{cc}} }\,t\star\pi\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

∎

{definition}

Let
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
. The
unordered pair
of
a
and
b
,
\textnormal{{up}}(a,b)
, is

\textnormal{{up}}(a,b)\coloneqq\{(a,\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline\pi\in\Pi\}\cup\{(b,\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline\pi\in\Pi\}.

We next show that
up
satisfies many of the basic properties one would expect an unordered pair to have in the realizability model. Namely; it is well-defined with respect to non-extensional equality, extensionally the ordered pair of
a
and
b
is the same as that of
b
and
a
, and if
z
is in the unordered pair of
a
and
b
then
z
is either extensionally equal to
a
or to
b
.

{proposition}

The following are realizable in
\mathcal{N}
:

i.

\forall x_{1}\forall x_{2}\forall y_{1}\forall y_{2}(\textnormal{{up}}(x_{1},y_{1})=\textnormal{{up}}(x_{2},y_{2})\xhookrightarrow{}(x_{1}=x_{2}\land y_{1}=y_{2}))
;

ii.

\forall x\forall y\forall z(z\mathop{\varepsilon}\textnormal{{up}}(x,y)\rightarrow z\mathop{\varepsilon}\textnormal{{up}}(y,x))
;

iii.

\forall x\forall y(\textnormal{{up}}(x,y)\simeq\textnormal{{up}}(y,x))
;

iv.

\forall x\forall y\forall z(z\in\textnormal{{up}}(x,y)\rightarrow(z\not\simeq y\rightarrow z\simeq x))
.

Proof.

i
.

For the first claim, fix
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1}
and
b_{2}
in
N
. If
\textnormal{{up}}(a_{1},b_{1})\neq\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})
then

\lVert\textnormal{{up}}(a_{1},b_{1})=\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})\xhookrightarrow{}(a_{1}=a_{2}\land b_{1}=b_{2})\rVert=\emptyset

and the result trivially follows. So suppose that
\textnormal{{up}}(a_{1},b_{1})=\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})
. Then it is obviously the case that
a_{1}=a_{2}
and
b_{1}=b_{2}
and thus
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash a_{1}=b_{1}
and
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash a_{2}=b_{2}
, from which the result follows.

ii
.

Fix
a,b
and
c
in
N
. We shall show that a realizer for the second statement is
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda i\boldsymbol{.}\big(i(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0})\big)\big(u\underline{1}\big)
. So, suppose that
t\Vdash c\mathop{\varepsilon}\textnormal{{up}}(a,b)
and
(c,\underline{i}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\in\textnormal{{up}}(b,a)
. Then either
i=0
and
c=b
or
i=1
and
c=a
. In the first case we have
t\star\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
while in the second case
t\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. The conclusion then follows from the verification that

\big(\underline{0}(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}t\underline{0})\big)\big(t\underline{1}\big)\star\pi\succ t\star\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
and
\big(\underline{1}(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}t\underline{0})\big)\big(t\underline{1}\big)\star\pi\succ t\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
.

iii
.

The third claim follows from the second claim alongside the observation that, in
\ZFepsilon
,
\forall a\forall x(x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\rightarrow x\in a)
.

iv
.

For the final claim, fix
a,b
and
c
in
N
,
t\Vdash c\not\in\textnormal{{up}}(a,b)\rightarrow\perp
,
s\Vdash c\simeq b\rightarrow\perp
and
\pi\in\lVert c\simeq a\rVert
. We shall show that

\theta_{1}\coloneqq\lambda e_{t}\boldsymbol{.}\lambda e_{s}\boldsymbol{.}cc\,(\lambda e_{k}\boldsymbol{.}e_{t}\theta_{0})

is a realizer for the desired statement, where

\theta_{0}\coloneqq\lambda e_{q}\boldsymbol{.}\lambda e_{r}\boldsymbol{.}\lambda i\boldsymbol{.}(((i\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}e_{s})e_{k})e_{q})e_{r}

will give a realizer for
c\not\in\textnormal{{up}}(a,b)
. To do this, first observe that
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\Vdash c\simeq a\rightarrow\perp
. Now, any element of
\lVert c\not\in\textnormal{{up}}(a,b)\rVert
is either of the form
q\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma
, where
q\Vdash c\subseteq a
and
r\Vdash a\subseteq c
or of the form
q\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma
, where
q\Vdash c\subseteq b
and
r\Vdash b\subseteq c
. In the first case, we have that
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\star q\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
while in the second
s\star q\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Next,

\displaystyle\theta_{0}[e_{s}\coloneqq s,e_{k}\coloneqq\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}]\star q\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma

\displaystyle\succ(((\underline{0}\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}s)\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi})q)r\star\sigma

\displaystyle\succ\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}v\star(\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}s)\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}q\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\succ\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\star q\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\sigma.

Similarly, since
\underline{1}=\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}u(v)
,
\theta_{0}[e_{s}\coloneqq s,e_{k}\coloneqq\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}]\star q\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\succ s\star q\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\sigma
. Thus, we have that
\theta_{0}[e_{s}\coloneqq s,e_{k}\coloneqq\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}]\Vdash c\not\in\textnormal{{up}}(a,b)
which means that
t\star\theta_{0}[e_{s}\coloneqq s,e_{k}\coloneqq\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}]\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Finally,

\theta_{1}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }\star(\lambda e_{k}\boldsymbol{.}t\theta_{0}[e_{s}\coloneqq s])\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\lambda e_{k}\boldsymbol{.}t\theta_{0}[e_{s}\coloneqq s]\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\theta_{0}[e_{s}\coloneqq s,e_{k}\coloneqq k]\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}.

∎

Finally, with a more involved proof, we can show that
up
is compatible with extensional equivalence.

{proposition}

It is realizable in
\mathcal{N}
that

\forall x_{1}\forall x_{2}\forall y_{1}\forall y_{2}\,(x_{1}\simeq x_{2}\rightarrow(y_{1}\simeq y_{2}\rightarrow\textnormal{{up}}(x_{1},y_{1})\simeq\textnormal{{up}}(x_{2},y_{2}))).

Proof.

Fix
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1}
and
b_{2}
in
