N
and let

\theta_{2}\coloneqq\lambda e_{t}\boldsymbol{.}\lambda e_{s}\boldsymbol{.}\lambda e_{r}\boldsymbol{.}\lambda i\boldsymbol{.}\bigg(\Big(\underline{i}\Big(\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{I}}e_{s})(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{1})\Big)\Big)\Big((\textnormal{{I}}e_{t})(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0})\Big)\bigg)\Big(e_{r}\Big).

It will suffice to prove that

\theta_{2}\Vdash a_{1}\simeq a_{2}\rightarrow(b_{1}\simeq b_{2}\rightarrow\textnormal{{up}}(a_{1},b_{1})\subseteq\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})).

First note that, by using the proof that Extensionality is realizable in
\mathcal{N}
from
Section

12
, we have that

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x\forall y\forall z\,(x\simeq y\rightarrow(y\mathop{\varepsilon}z\rightarrow x\in z)).

Also,
\lVert\textnormal{{up}}(a_{1},b_{1})\subseteq\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})\rVert=\bigcup_{c}\{r\boldsymbol{.}\underline{i}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline(c,\underline{i}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\in\textnormal{{up}}(a_{1},b_{1}),\,r\Vdash c\not\in\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})\}.

Now, fix
t\Vdash a_{1}\simeq a_{2}
,
s\Vdash b_{1}\simeq b_{2}
,
(c,\underline{i}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\in\textnormal{{up}}(a_{1},b_{1})
and
r\Vdash c\not\in\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})
. If
i=0
then we must have that
c=a_{1}
and thus
r\Vdash a_{1}\not\in\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})
. Now,
\lVert a_{2}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})\rVert=\{\sigma\divline(a_{2},\sigma)\in\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})\}=\{\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\divline\sigma\in\Pi\}
so
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0}\Vdash a_{2}\mathop{\varepsilon}\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})
. Therefore,
(\textnormal{{I}}t)(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0})\Vdash a_{1}\in\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})
and thus

(\textnormal{{I}}t)(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0})\star r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}.

In the same way, if
i=1
then
r\Vdash b_{1}\not\in\textnormal{{up}}(a_{2},b_{2})
and
(\textnormal{{I}}s)(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{1})\star r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}.
Finally, the result follows from the computations

\bigg(\Big(\underline{0}\Big(\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{I}}s)(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{1})\Big)\Big)\Big((\textnormal{{I}}t)(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0})\Big)\bigg)\Big(r\Big)\star\pi\succ(\textnormal{{I}}t)(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0})\star r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}

and

\bigg(\Big(\underline{1}\Big(\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{I}}s)(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{1})\Big)\Big)\Big((\textnormal{{I}}t)(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0})\Big)\bigg)\Big(r\Big)\star\pi\succ(\textnormal{{I}}s)(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{1})\star r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}

∎

14.2
Ordered Pairs

The previous analysis shows that
up
gives a reasonable interpretation of the unordered pair. The next objective is to define an ordered pair and repeat the analysis to show that it behaves as expected.

{definition}

Let
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
. The
ordered pair
of
a
and
b
,
\textnormal{{op}}(a,b)
, is

\textnormal{{op}}(a,b)\coloneqq\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})),\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b))).

The first thing we wish to deduce is that the ordered pair can distinguish between the first and the second element. Namely, if
\textnormal{{op}}(x_{1},y_{1})\simeq\textnormal{{op}}(x_{2},y_{2})
then we should be able to realize that
x_{1}\simeq x_{2}
and
y_{1}\simeq y_{2}
. In order to do this, we first need to prove a technical lemma. This lemma is the reason why we have chosen to using Wiener’s definition of ordered pairs.

{lemma}

For any
a,b,x\in\textnormal{{N}}
, the following are realizable in
\mathcal{N}
:

i.

\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\not\simeq\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\rightarrow\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\not\in\textnormal{{op}}(a,b)
;

ii.

\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x))\not\simeq\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b))\rightarrow\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x))\not\in\textnormal{{op}}(a,b)
;

Proof.

First, note that

\lVert\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\not\in\textnormal{{op}}(a,b)\rVert=\bigcup_{c}\{s\boldsymbol{.}s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{i}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline(c,\underline{i}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\in\textnormal{{op}}(a,b),\\
s\Vdash\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\subseteq c,\,s^{\prime}\Vdash c\subseteq\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\}.

Suppose that
t\Vdash\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\not\simeq\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))
,
(c,\underline{i}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\in\textnormal{{op}}(a,b)
and
s,s^{\prime}
realize the relevant statements. Then
c
is either
\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))
or
\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b))
. If
c=\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))
then
i=0
and
t\star s\boldsymbol{.}s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

So, suppose that
c=\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b))
and thus
i=1
. Then

s\Vdash\forall z(z\not\in\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b))\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))).

In particular,
s\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\not\in\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b))\rightarrow\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))
. Then, since
\{\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\divline\sigma\in\Pi\}=\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\rVert
,
s\underline{1}\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\not\in\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b))\rightarrow\perp
. Next, by
\thetheorem.(iii)
, we have that there exists some term
r_{s}
(dependent only on
s
) such that
r_{s}\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(b)\simeq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
. However, by
\thetheorem.(ii)
there is a realizer
r_{0}
such that
r_{0}\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(b)\not\simeq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
because
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})=\emptyset
. Thus,
r_{s}\star r_{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

The conclusion for the first claim follows from creating a realizer
r_{1}
such that
r_{1}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star s\boldsymbol{.}s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
while
r_{1}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ r_{s}\star r_{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
, which is relatively straightforward to do.

The second claim follows be a very similar argument to the one above. Namely, we take
t\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x))\not\simeq\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b))
,
(c,\underline{i}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\in\textnormal{{op}}(a,b)
,
s\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b))\subseteq c
and
s^{\prime}\Vdash c\subseteq\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b))
. If
c=\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b))
, then
i=1
and
t\star s\boldsymbol{.}s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

So suppose that
c=\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))
. Then
s^{\prime}\Vdash\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\subseteq\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(x))
and the same argument as above gives us that there is a realizer
r_{s^{\prime}}
such that
r_{s^{\prime}}\star r_{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. The conclusion then follows from creating a realizer
r_{2}
such that
r_{2}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star s\boldsymbol{.}s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
while
r_{2}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ r_{s^{\prime}}\star r_{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
.
∎

The following two propositions show that the function
op
is also compatible with extensional equality and thus gives a reasonable interpretation of ordered pairs.

{proposition}

It is realizable in
\mathcal{N}
that

\forall x_{1}\forall x_{2}\forall y_{1}\forall y_{2}\,(\textnormal{{op}}(x_{1},y_{1})\simeq\textnormal{{op}}(x_{2},y_{2})\rightarrow(x_{1}\simeq x_{2}\land y_{1}\simeq y_{2})).

Proof.

Fix
a_{1},b_{1},a_{2}
and
b_{2}
in
N
. It will suffice to show that if
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\textnormal{{op}}(a_{1},b_{1})\subseteq\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})
(that it to say, some
t
realizes this statement) then

\mathcal{N}\Vdash a_{1}\simeq a_{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{N}\Vdash b_{1}\simeq b_{2}.

For the first of these statements, suppose that
t\Vdash\forall z(z\not\in\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{op}}(a_{1},b_{1}))
. In particular, this means that

t\Vdash\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\not\in\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})\rightarrow\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{op}}(a_{1},b_{1}).

Now,
\lVert\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{op}}(a_{1},b_{1})\rVert=\{\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline\pi\in\Pi\}
which means that
t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
whenever
\sigma\in\Pi
and
s\Vdash\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\not\in\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})
. From this, we can conclude that

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(tu)\underline{0}\Vdash\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\in\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2}).

We now work internally in any model,
\mathcal{N}
, of the realizable theory
T
. By the contrapositive of
\thetheorem.(i)
,

\mathcal{N}\Vdash\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\simeq\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{2}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))

Next, we obviously have that
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1})\mathop{\varepsilon}\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))
so, by
Section

8
,
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1})\in\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{2}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))
. However, by
\thetheorem.(ii)
,
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1})\not\simeq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
so, by
\thetheorem.(iv)
,
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1})\simeq\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{2})
. Finally, by
\thetheorem.(v)
,
\mathcal{N}\Vdash a_{1}\simeq a_{2}
.

The second of these statements follows from a very similar argument: With the same assumption on
t
we have that
t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
whenever
\sigma\in\Pi
and
s\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b_{1}))\not\in\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})
and therefore

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(tu)\underline{1}\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b_{1}))\in\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2}).

Therefore, by the contrapositive of
\thetheorem.(ii)
,
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b_{1}))\simeq\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b_{2}))
. Finally, two applications of
\thetheorem.(v)
gives us that
\mathcal{N}\Vdash b_{1}\simeq b_{2}
.
∎

{proposition}

It is realizable in
\mathcal{N}
that

\forall x_{1}\forall x_{2}\forall y_{1}\forall y_{2}\,(x_{1}\simeq x_{2}\rightarrow(y_{1}\simeq y_{2}\rightarrow\textnormal{{op}}(x_{1},y_{1})\simeq\textnormal{{op}}(x_{2},y_{2}))).

Proof.

The proof of this proposition will be very similar to proof of
Section

14.1
. Fix
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1}
and
b_{2}
in
N
. If will suffice to find a realizer
\theta
such that

\theta\Vdash a_{1}\simeq a_{2}\rightarrow(b_{1}\simeq b_{2}\rightarrow\textnormal{{op}}(a_{1},b_{1})\subseteq\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})).

To do this, let
t\Vdash a_{1}\simeq a_{2}
,
s\Vdash b_{1}\simeq b_{2}
,
(c,\underline{i}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\in\textnormal{{op}}(a_{1},b_{1})
and
r\Vdash c\not\in\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})
. If
i=0
then we must have that
c=\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))
and thus
r\Vdash\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\not\in\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})
. Now, since
\lVert\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{2}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})\rVert=\{\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\divline\sigma\in\Pi\}
,
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0}\Vdash\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{2}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\mathop{\varepsilon}\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})
. Next, using
\thetheorem.(iv)
and
Section

14.1
we have that

\mathcal{N}\Vdash(a_{1}\simeq a_{2})\rightarrow(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1})\simeq\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{2}))\rightarrow(\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\simeq\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{2}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))),

so we can find a realizer
v_{t}
(dependent on
t
) such that

v_{t}\Vdash\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})))\simeq\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{2},\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})))
.
Hence, using the proof that Extensionality is realizable in
\mathcal{N}
from
Section

12
, we have that
(\textnormal{{I}}v_{t})(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0})\Vdash\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a_{1}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\in\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})
and thus

(\textnormal{{I}}v_{t})(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0})\star r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}.

In a similar way, if
i=1
then
r\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b_{1}))\not\in\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})
and we can find a realizer
v_{s}
(dependent on
s
) such that
(\textnormal{{I}}v_{s})(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{1})\Vdash\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b_{1}))\in\textnormal{{op}}(a_{2},b_{2})
, giving
(\textnormal{{I}}v_{s})(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{1})\star r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

One can see that the desired realizer is then

\lambda t\boldsymbol{.}\lambda s\boldsymbol{.}\lambda r\boldsymbol{.}\lambda i\bigg(\Big(i\Big(\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{I}}v_{s})(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{1})\Big)\Big)\Big((\textnormal{{I}}v_{t})(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0})\Big)\bigg)\Big(r\Big)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

14.3
Cartesian Products

To conclude this section, we see that this definition of ordered pairs allows us to define Cartesian products of recursive names.

{definition}

c
is said to be a
Cartesian product
of
a
and
b
if:

•

\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\forall y\mathop{\varepsilon}b\,(\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}c)
;

•

\forall z((\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\forall y\mathop{\varepsilon}b\,\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\not\simeq z)\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}c)
.

Observe that the second condition is equivalent to the claim that every element of
c
is an ordered pair consisting of an element of
a
and an element of
b
, that is
\forall z\mathop{\varepsilon}c\,\exists x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\exists y\mathop{\varepsilon}b(z\simeq\textnormal{{op}}(x,y))
.

{definition}

Given sets
a
and
b
in the ground model define

\textnormal{{Cart}}(a,b)\coloneqq\{(\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({y})),\pi)\divline x\in a,\,y\in b,\,\pi\in\Pi\}
.

{proposition}

\mathcal{N}\Vdash\textnormal{{Cart}}(a,b)\text{ is the Cartesian product of }\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})\text{ and }\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})
.

Proof.

We shall first prove that

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall w_{0}^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})}\,\forall w_{1}^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})}\,(\textnormal{{op}}(w_{0},w_{1})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{Cart}}(a,b)\rightarrow\perp).

To this end, fix
x\in a
,
y\in b
,
t\Vdash\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({y}))\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{Cart}}(a,b)
and
\pi\in\Pi
. Then, by definition of Cart,
\lVert\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({y}))\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{Cart}}(a,b)\rVert=\Pi
and thus
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

For the second claim, we shall prove that

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\theta\Vdash\forall z((\forall w_{0}^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})}\,\forall w_{1}^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})}\,\textnormal{{op}}(w_{0},w_{1})\not\simeq z)\rightarrow z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\textnormal{{Cart}}(a,b)),

where
\theta
is a fixed realizer such that
\theta\Vdash\forall x(x\simeq x)
(for an example of such a realizer see
Section

11.1
).

For this, fix
(\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({y})),\pi)\in\textnormal{{Cart}}(a,b)
and
t\Vdash\forall w_{0}^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})}\,\forall w_{1}^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})}\,\textnormal{{op}}(w_{0},w_{1})\not\simeq\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({y}))
. Then, in particular, we have that
t\Vdash\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({y}))\not\simeq\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({y}))
and thus
t\star\theta\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

15
Functions

In its most naive form, a function is a collection of ordered pairs such that whenever the first co-ordinates are equal then so are the second co-ordinates. Due to there being two versions of “equality” in
\ZFepsilon
this gives rise to multiple different interpretations of what a function should be.

{definition}

[
\ZFepsilon
]
A
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-function with domain
a
is a set
f
satisfying:

•

(
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-totality)
\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\exists y\,\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}f
;

•

\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\forall y,y^{\prime}(\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}f\land\textnormal{{op}}(x,y^{\prime})\mathop{\varepsilon}f\rightarrow y=y^{\prime})
.

We will write
\mathop{\varepsilon}\textnormal{{-Func}}(f)
to indicate that
f
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-function.

{definition}

[
\ZFepsilon
]
A
\in
-function with domain
a
is a set
f
satisfying:

•

(
\in
-totality)
\forall x\in a\,\exists y\,\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\in f
;

•

\forall x\in a\,\forall y,y^{\prime}(\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\in f\land\textnormal{{op}}(x,y^{\prime})\in f\rightarrow y\simeq y^{\prime})
.

We will write
\in\textnormal{{-Func}}(f)
to indicate that
f
is a
\in
-function.

{remark}

b
is said to be a co-domain of a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-function
f
if whenever
\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}f
then
y\mathop{\varepsilon}b
(and similarly for
\in
-functions). When we wish to identify such a
b
we will alternatively write
f
as
f\colon a\rightarrow b
, and it will be clear from the context what type of function
f
is.

{remark}

The definition of functions given here is perhaps not the most accurate definition because we have not required that every element of
f
is an ordered pair and the first component of such an ordered pair is an element of
a
. This could easily be added to the definition, however it would make proving that a given set in
N
is a function more tedious without adding any useful information. Instead we note that for any reasonable definition of function we can restrict the set using
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-Separation to only look at relevant ordered pairs.

Also, on occasion it will be simpler to phrase a function as a binary relation satisfying the relevant translations of the conditions, for example
\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\forall y,y^{\prime}(f(x,y)\land f(x,y^{\prime})\rightarrow y=y^{\prime})
. Clearly, given a function defined in this way we can find a set representing this function in the obvious way.

In most of the conservativity results between
ZF
and
\ZFepsilon
one shows that the
\epsilon
definition of a concept directly relates to the
\in
definition. However, this is not the case here. For example, take two distinct sets
x
and
y
which are extensionally equal (such as
\{(a,\pi)\divline\pi\in\Pi\}
and
\{(a,\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline\pi\in\Pi\}
). Then there is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-function
f\colon\{x,y\}\times\Pi\rightarrow\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
given by sending
x
to
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
and
y
to
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})
. However, this does not translate to a
\in
-function because such a function would need to send
x
to both
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
and
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})
which are not extensionally equal sets. Instead, for conservativity results, we need an intermediate notion of function.

{definition}

[
\ZFepsilon
]
An
extensional
function with domain
a
is a set
f
satisfying:

•

(
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-totality)
\forall x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\exists y\,\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}f
;

•

\forall x,x^{\prime}\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\forall y,y^{\prime}(x\simeq x^{\prime}\land\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}f\land\textnormal{{op}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mathop{\varepsilon}f\rightarrow y\simeq y^{\prime})
.

We will write
\textnormal{{Ext-Func}}(f)
to indicate that
f
is an extensional function.

{remark}

In the definitions of
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-functions and
\in
-functions we only needed to assume that for every
x
in
a
and
y,y^{\prime}
if
f(x,y)
and
f(x,y^{\prime})
both hold then
y
and
y^{\prime}
are equal. On the other hand, in the definition of an extensional function we have needed to strengthen this condition to the claim that if
x\simeq x^{\prime}
and both
f(x,y)
and
f(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
hold then
y\simeq y^{\prime}
. It is relatively straight forward to see that the additional
x^{\prime}
is not needed in either of the first two definitions, however its inclusion for extensional functions is needed to ensure that they translate cleanly to
\in
-functions.

{observation}

Suppose that
f
is a
\in
-function with domain
a
. Let
g
be the binary relation defined by

\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}g\Longleftrightarrow x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\land\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\in f.

Then
g
is an extensional function with domain
a
. Similarly, if
g
is an extensional function with domain
a
and
f
is the binary relation defined by

\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\in f\Longleftrightarrow\exists c\mathop{\varepsilon}a(x\simeq c\land\textnormal{{op}}(c,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}g,

then
f
is a
\in
-function with domain
a
.

As an example of conservativity, we briefly discuss a definition of
cardinals
in
\ZFepsilon
.

{definition}

A relation
f
with domain
a
is said to be a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-injection if

\forall x,x^{\prime}\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\forall y\,\big(\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}f\land\textnormal{{op}}(x^{\prime},y)\mathop{\varepsilon}f\rightarrow x\simeq x^{\prime}\big).

A relation
f
with domain
a
is said to be a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-surjection on
b
if

\forall y\mathop{\varepsilon}b\,\exists x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\,\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}f.

{definition}

An ordinal
a
is said to be a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-cardinal
if for every
b\mathop{\varepsilon}a
there is no extensional function which is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-surjection of
b
onto
a
.

{proposition}

If
a
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-cardinal, then it is a
\in
-cardinal.

Proof.

Let
a
be an
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-ordinal. We shall show that if
a
is not a
\in
-cardinal then
a
is not a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-cardinal. To this end, suppose that
b\in a
and
f\colon b\rightarrow a
was a
\in
-surjection. Fix
z\mathop{\varepsilon}a
such that
b\simeq z
and define a binary relation
g
by

\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}g\Longleftrightarrow\exists c\in a\,\big(\textnormal{{op}}(x,c)\in f\land c\simeq y\big).

We shall show that
g
defines a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-surjection from
z
onto
a
. There are three things to prove: that
g
is
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-total on
a
;
g
is an extensional function; and
g
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-surjection.

For the first claim, take
x\mathop{\varepsilon}z
. Then
x\in z
and therefore
x\in b
, by
\thetheorem.(vii)
. Since
f
is
\in
-total, we can fix
c\in a
such that
\textnormal{{op}}(x,c)\in f
and then fix
y\mathop{\varepsilon}a
such that
y\simeq c
. Hence, we have
\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}g
.

For the second claim, fix
x,x^{\prime}\mathop{\varepsilon}b
,
y,y^{\prime}\mathop{\varepsilon}a
and suppose that
\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}g
,
\textnormal{{op}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mathop{\varepsilon}g
and
x\simeq x^{\prime}
. Fix
c,c^{\prime}\in a
such that
\textnormal{{op}}(x,c)\in f
,
\textnormal{{op}}(x^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in f
,
c\simeq y
and
c^{\prime}\simeq y^{\prime}
. Since
f
is a
\in
-function,
x,x^{\prime}\in b
and
x\simeq x^{\prime}
, we must have that
c\simeq c^{\prime}
. Therefore
y\simeq c\simeq c^{\prime}\simeq y^{\prime}
.

For the final claim, fix
y\mathop{\varepsilon}a
. Then
y\in a
so, since
f
is a
\in
-surjection, we can fix
t\in b
such that
\textnormal{{op}}(t,y)\in f
holds. Since
t\in b
and
b\simeq z
,
t\in z
. Therefore, there exists some
d\mathop{\varepsilon}z
such that
t\simeq d
. Since
g
is
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-total, we can fix
y^{\prime}\mathop{\varepsilon}a
such that
\textnormal{{op}}(d,y^{\prime})\mathop{\varepsilon}g
and then, by definition,
c\in a
such that
\textnormal{{op}}(d,c)\in f
and
c\simeq y^{\prime}
. Finally, since
f
is a function and
t\simeq d
,
y\simeq c\simeq y^{\prime}
, from which we can conclude that
\textnormal{{op}}(d,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}g
as required.
∎

16
Lifting Functions

Here we give a general framework to lift functions from the ground model to the realizability model, the idea being that they should extend the (natural interpretations of the) function from the ground model. For example, if
f\colon 2\rightarrow 2
is a ground model function then there should be some
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-function
\tilde{f}\colon\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})\rightarrow\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
such that
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\tilde{f}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({i}))=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(i)})
for
i\in\{0,1\}
.

{definition}

Suppose that
a,b\in\textnormal{{V}}
and
f\colon a\rightarrow b
is a function. We define the
lift
of
f
,
\tilde{f}
as

\tilde{f}\coloneqq\{(\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})),\pi)\divline c\in a,\pi\in\Pi\}.

We begin by showing that
\tilde{f}
is a lift of the function
f
in some precise sense:

{proposition}

For any
c
in
a
,

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall z\big(\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rightarrow\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})=z\big)\text{ and }\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall z\big(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})=z\xhookrightarrow{}\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\big).

Proof.

For the first claim, suppose that
t\Vdash\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}
,
s\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})\neq z
and
\pi\in\Pi
. We shall show that
s\Vdash\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\tilde{f}
from which it shall follow that
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. But this follows from the observation that
\lVert\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\tilde{f}\rVert=\Pi
if
z=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})
and equals
\emptyset
otherwise, which means that
\lVert\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\tilde{f}\rVert=\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})\neq z\rVert
.

For the second claim, if
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})\neq z
then
\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})=z\xhookrightarrow{}\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rVert=\emptyset
and thus
I
trivially realizes this. Otherwise,
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})=z
and
\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})\xhookrightarrow{}\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)}))\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rVert=\lVert\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)}))\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rVert=\{t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline\pi\in\Pi,\forall\sigma\in\Pi(t\star\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}})\}
. Therefore, for any such
t
and
\pi
,
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

{proposition}

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}vu\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})}\forall y\forall y^{\prime}(y\neq y^{\prime}\rightarrow\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rightarrow\textnormal{{op}}(x,y^{\prime})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\tilde{f}).
Therefore,
\tilde{f}
is realized to be a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-function with domain
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})
.

Proof.

Fix
c\in a
,
d,d^{\prime}\in\textnormal{{N}}
and suppose that
t\Vdash d\neq d^{\prime}
,
s\Vdash\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),d)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}
while
(\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),d^{\prime}),\pi)\in\tilde{f}
. By definition of
\tilde{f}
, we must have that
d^{\prime}=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})
. There are now two cases;
d\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})
and
d=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})
.

If
d\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})
then
\lVert\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),d)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\tilde{f}\rVert=\emptyset
and therefore
\lVert\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),d)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rVert=\Lambda\star\Pi
. From this it follows that
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}vu\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ s\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

On the other hand, if
d=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})
then
\lVert\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),d)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\tilde{f}\rVert=\Pi=\lVert d\neq d^{\prime}\rVert
. From this it follows that
t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),d)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rVert
and therefore
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}vu\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ s\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

Finally, we can see that
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})
is a co-domain of
\tilde{f}
:

{proposition}

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})}\forall y(y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})\rightarrow\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\tilde{f})
.

Proof.

Fix
c\in a
,
d\in\textnormal{{N}}
and suppose that
t\Vdash d\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})
while
(\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),d),\pi)\in\tilde{f}
. Then, by definition, we must have that
d=f(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}))
which means that
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash d\mathop{\varepsilon}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})
. Therefore, we have
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

16.1
Lifting Functions with Multiple Arguments

If one were to try and lift a function
f\colon a_{0}\times a_{1}\rightarrow b
using the previous methodology this would give us a function
\tilde{f}\colon\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a_{0}\times a_{1}})\rightarrow b
in
\mathcal{N}
rather than a more useful function with domain
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a_{0}})\times\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a_{1}})
. However, this can be achieved by a slight alteration to the definition of the lift. Since the proof follows by the same argument as in the earlier propositions, we will omit it.

{proposition}

Suppose that
f\colon a_{0}\times a_{1}\rightarrow b
is a function. Let

\tilde{f}\colon\{(\textnormal{{op}}(\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x_{0}}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x_{1}})),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(x_{0},x_{1})})),\pi)\divline x_{0}\in a_{0},x_{1}\in a_{1},\pi\in\Pi\}.

Then
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\tilde{f}\colon\textnormal{{Cart}}(a_{0},a_{1})\rightarrow\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})\text{ is a }\mathop{\varepsilon}\text{-function}
. Moreover, for any
x_{0}\in a_{0}
and
x_{1}\in a_{1}
,

I

\displaystyle\Vdash\forall z\big(\textnormal{{op}}(\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x_{0}}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x_{1}})),z)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rightarrow\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(x_{0},x_{1})})=z\big)\text{ and }

I

\displaystyle\Vdash\forall z\big(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(x_{0},x_{1})})=z\xhookrightarrow{}\textnormal{{op}}(\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x_{0}}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({x_{1}})),z)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\big).

We recall here that, in
\mathcal{N}
,
\textnormal{{Cart}}(a_{0},a_{1})
is a name for the Cartesian product of
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a_{0}})
and
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a_{1}})
. Therefore, unless there is room for confusion, we shall also refer to this set as the lift of the function
f
and use the same notation because this is what we want to mean by lifting a function with domain
a_{0}\times a_{1}
.

{remark}

The above result could be easily further generalised to allow for the lift of a function
f\colon a_{0}\times\dots a_{n}\rightarrow b
for any finite
n
in the obvious way.

{theorem}

\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
is a Boolean algebra with binary operations
\lor
and
\land
and unary operation
-
given by the lift of the corresponding (trivial) operations on
\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

Formally, this is proven by showing that the above operations satisfy commutativity, associativity, distributivity, absorption and complementations. All of these are straightforward, and we briefly discuss one of the absorption cases as an example.

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})}\forall y^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})}\;(x\land(x\lor y)=x).

It suffices to prove that if
i,j\in\{0,1\}
then
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({i})\land(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({i})\lor\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({j}))=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({i})
. But, this is immediate from the fact that
\{0,1\}
is a Boolean algebra and the operations lift the operations on
\{0,1\}
.
∎

{remark}

We would have liked to lift the function to an extensional function in the realizability model, however in general this does not seem possible to do. The issue is that there may not be a realizer
t
such that, for any
a\neq b
,
t\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})\not\simeq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})
.

This becomes a problem when one wants to prove
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\forall z(\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z)\in\tilde{f}\rightarrow\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})\simeq z)
. If
\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z)\in\tilde{f}
then we can find some
d\mathop{\varepsilon}a
for which
\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z)\simeq\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({d}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(d)}))
. It will then be the case that, in
\mathcal{N}
,
z\simeq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(d)})
. The issue is that we cannot prove
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c})\simeq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({d})\rightarrow\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})\simeq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(d)})
and therefore it is unclear how one could conclude that
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({f(c)})\simeq z
.

16.2
Lifting Class Functions

Formally, the implementations for singletons, unordered pairs and ordered pairs have only been defined externally for elements of
N
. In this short section we outline how to internalise these functions so as to naturally view them as class functions over the structure. As mentioned in
Section

9.1
this will then allow us to enrich the theory
\ZFepsilon
by any
\mathcal{A}
-definable function in a way that allows us to internalise such functions with the theory. In order to do this, we need a general method to universally lift a function
f\colon\textnormal{{N}}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
.

{definition}

Suppose that
f\colon\textnormal{{N}}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
is a function (as defined in the ground model). We define the
universal lift
of
f
,
\tilde{f}
, as

\tilde{f}\coloneqq\{(\textnormal{{op}}(a,f(a)),\pi)\divline a\in\textnormal{{N}},\pi\in\Pi\}.

{theorem}

Suppose that
f\colon\textnormal{{N}}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
. Then, in
\mathcal{N}
,

i.

For every
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
,
\forall z\,(\textnormal{{op}}(a,z)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rightarrow z=f(a))
and
\forall z\,(f(a)=z\rightarrow\textnormal{{op}}(a,z)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f})
. That is,
\tilde{f}
agrees with
f
on
N
.

ii.

\forall x\,\exists y\,\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}
and
\forall x\,\forall y\,\forall y^{\prime}\,(\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\land\textnormal{{op}}(x,y^{\prime})\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rightarrow y=y^{\prime})
. That is,
\tilde{f}
is a class
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-function.

Since the proof to this theorem is the same as for the lift of the set function
f\colon a\rightarrow b
, we will omit it.

{remark}

Using the analogous construction to
Section

16.1
, it is also possible to lift any function
f\colon\textnormal{{N}}^{n}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
for any
n\in\omega
.

{theorem}

The following functions universally lift to the realizability model and therefore can be viewed as internal functions in
\mathcal{N}
.

•

\textnormal{{sng}}\colon\textnormal{{N}}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
,
a\mapsto\{a\}\times\Pi
;

•

\textnormal{{up}}\colon\textnormal{{N}}\times\textnormal{{N}}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
,
(a,b)\mapsto\{(a,\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline\pi\in\Pi\}\cup\{(b,\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline\pi\in\Pi\}
;

•

\textnormal{{op}}\colon\textnormal{{N}}\times\textnormal{{N}}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
,
(a,b)\mapsto\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{up}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(a),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})),\textnormal{{sng}}(\textnormal{{sng}}(b)))
;

•

a\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}}b\colon\textnormal{{N}}\times\textnormal{{N}}\rightarrow
,
(a,b)\mapsto\{(x,\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline(x,\pi)\in a\}\cup\{(x,\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)\divline(x,\pi)\in b\}
. This function gives a canonical way to define the non-extensional union of two sets;

•

w\mathcal{P}\colon\textnormal{{N}}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
,
a\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\textnormal{{dom}}(a)\times\Pi)\times\Pi
. This function gives a canonical weak power set for every set
a
.

{remark}

[A note on unions]
a

It is easy to see that, for any
a,b\in\textnormal{{N}}
,
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{0}\Vdash x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}}b\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a
,
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}u\underline{1}\Vdash x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}}b\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b
and
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\lambda i\boldsymbol{.}(i\lambda w\boldsymbol{.}v)u\Vdash x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\rightarrow(x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b\rightarrow x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}}b)
. Thus
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\forall x(x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}}b\leftrightarrow(x\mathop{\varepsilon}a\lor x\mathop{\varepsilon}b))
so
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\mathcal{N}$}}}$\crcr}}}}
does give a name for the non-extensional union of two sets.

It would have been preferable to define the name for the union as simply
a\cup b
, using the true union in the ground model. One can see that
\mathcal{N}\Vdash x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\cup b\rightarrow(x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a\lor x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}b)
and therefore
a\cup b
contains the union of the two sets, the difficulty is in the reverse direction. This is because we run into the standard issue that from
(x,\pi)\in a\cup b
we cannot decide from
\pi
whether
(x,\pi)\in a
or
(x,\pi)\in b
.

16.3
Images of Functions

Due to our coding of ordered pairs, it is cumbersome to deal with the image of a lifted function. To see the issue consider trying to solve

\forall x\tilde{f}(x)\mathop{\varepsilon}a.

Formally, to realize this we would need to realize something of the form

\forall x(\forall y(\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}a)\rightarrow\perp).

Therefore, we introduce an easier way to phrase such statements through a new falsity value.

{definition}

Suppose that
f\colon a\rightarrow b
is a function in the ground model and let
\tilde{f}
be the standard name for the lift of
f
. Then, for any formula
\varphi(u,v,\overrightarrow{w})
and
\overrightarrow{w}\in\textnormal{{N}}
,

\lVert\varphi(x,\tilde{f}(x),\overrightarrow{w})\rVert=\begin{cases}\lVert\varphi(x,\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({d}),\overrightarrow{w})\rVert&\text{ if }x=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c})\text{ and }f(c)=d\text{ for some }d\in b,\\
\emptyset&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

We note that, since
f
is a function, the above
d\in b
is uniquely defined. We now prove that this set provides a way to interpret the image of
\tilde{f}
. For simplicity, we will suppress the parameter
\overrightarrow{w}
.

{lemma}

For every formula
\varphi
,

\mathcal{N}\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})}\forall y\big(\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rightarrow(\varphi(x,y)\rightarrow\varphi(x,\tilde{f}(x)))\big).

Proof.

It will suffice to prove that

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})}\forall y(\neg\varphi(x,\tilde{f}(x))\rightarrow(\varphi(x,y)\rightarrow\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\tilde{f})).

To do this, fix
c\in a
,
z\in\textnormal{{N}}
, and
d\in B
such that
f(c)=d
. Then,
\lVert\neg\varphi(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),\tilde{f}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}))\rVert=\lVert\neg\varphi(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({d}))\rVert
. So, if
t\Vdash\neg\varphi(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({d}))
,
s\Vdash\varphi(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z)
and
(\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z),\pi)\in\tilde{f}
then we must have that
z=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({d})
. Hence,
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

{lemma}

For every formula
\varphi
,

\mathcal{N}\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})}\big(\varphi(x,\tilde{f}(x))\rightarrow\forall y(\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}\tilde{f}\rightarrow\varphi(x,y))\big).

Proof.

It will suffice to prove that

\lambda g\boldsymbol{.}\lambda h\boldsymbol{.}hg\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})}(\varphi(x,\tilde{f}(x))\rightarrow\forall y(\neg\varphi(x,y)\rightarrow\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\tilde{f})).

Again, fix
c\in a
and
d\in b
such that
f(c)=d
. Next, suppose that
t\Vdash\varphi(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({d}))
and
s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\lVert\forall y(\neg\varphi(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),y)\rightarrow\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),y)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\tilde{f})\rVert
. Then, for some
z\in\textnormal{{N}}
,
s\Vdash\neg\varphi(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z)
and
(\textnormal{{op}}(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({c}),z),\pi)\in\tilde{f}
, from which it follows that
z=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({d})
. Thus
\lambda g\boldsymbol{.}\lambda h\boldsymbol{.}hg\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ s\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

As an example of this alternative interpretation we reprove
Section

16
to show that
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})
is a codomain of
\tilde{f}
.

{proposition}

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({a})}(\tilde{f}(x)\mathop{\varepsilon}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b}))
.

Proof.

Fix
c\in a
,
t\Vdash\tilde{f}(c)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})
and
\pi\in\Pi
. Then
\lVert\tilde{f}(c)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})\rVert=\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({d})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({b})\rVert
where
d=f(c)
. But, since
d\in b
, this set is equal to
\Pi
, from which it follows that
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

17
Non-extensional Axiom of Choice and Dependant Choice

In this section we discuss the non-extensional Axiom of Choice, denoted
NEAC
, which is a version of the Axiom of Choice that holds in many realizability models. Assuming full AC in
V
, Theorem 4.18 of
[
Kri12
]
shows that
NEAC
holds in any realizability model produced from a countable realizability algebra which contains the additional instruction “
quote
”. A more generalised argument is also given in Section 4.2 of
[
FG20
]
, which adds a generalisation to quote as well as an additional instruction
7
7

7

which essentially allows one to realize that
\widehat{\alpha+1}
is the successor of
\hat{\alpha}
to obtain
NEAC
in realizability models of uncountable size.

{definition}

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}=(\Lambda,\Pi,\prec,\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}})
is a countable realizability algebra and let
t\mapsto\eta_{t}
be an enumeration of
\Lambda
in order type
\omega
. Then
\mathcal{A}
contains the instruction
quote
if there exists a special instruction
\textnormal{{q}}\in\Lambda
such that for any
t,s\in\Lambda
and
\pi\in\Pi
,

\textnormal{{q}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\underline{\eta_{s}}\boldsymbol{.}\pi,

where
\underline{\eta_{s}}
is Church numeral associated to
\eta_{s}\in\omega
.

So let us fix a countable realizability algebra
\mathcal{A}
and suppose that
\Lambda
contains the instruction
quote
.
NEAC
is the statement that every binary relation can be refined into a (non-extensional)
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-function. More formally,

{definition}

NEAC
is the statement:

\forall z\,\exists f\,(f\subseteq_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}z\land\mathop{\varepsilon}\textnormal{{-Func}}(f)\land\forall x\forall y\exists y^{\prime}(\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}z\rightarrow\textnormal{{op}}(x,y^{\prime})\mathop{\varepsilon}f)).

{observation}

The extensional version of
NEAC
(where
f
is an extensional function) implies the Axiom of Choice. To see this, suppose
A
were a family of non-empty sets and let
z
be the binary relation defined by
(i,x)\in z
if and only if
i\in A\land x\in i
. Now, if we can refine
z
to an extensional function,
f
, this will be a choice function for
A
. This is because if
i,j\in A
and
i\simeq j
then
f(i)\simeq f(j)
.

On the other hand, if we can only refine
z
to a non-extensional
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-function this will not suffice because we may have
i\simeq j
but
f(i)\not\simeq f(j)
. Therefore, we have not chosen a unique extensional element of each set in
A
.

{lemma}

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is a countable realizability algebra and
\Lambda
contains a special instruction
q
satisfying the instruction quote. Then for any formula
\varphi(u,x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
in
Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
there exists a class function
g_{\varphi}\colon\omega\times\textnormal{{N}}^{n}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
such that

\mathcal{N}\Vdash\forall x_{1},\dots,x_{n}(\exists u\,\varphi(u,x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\rightarrow\exists n^{\hat{\omega}}\varphi(\tilde{g}_{\varphi}(n,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),x_{1},\dots,x_{n})),

where
\tilde{g}_{\varphi}
is the lift of
g_{\varphi}
to a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-class function as defined in
Section

16.2
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we will assume that
\varphi(u,x)
has precisely two free variables. Fix an enumeration
(\nu_{n}\divline n\in\omega)
of
\Lambda
and let
t\mapsto\eta_{t}
be the inverse of the enumeration (so
\nu_{\eta_{t}}=t
).

Given
n\in\omega
, set
P_{n}\coloneqq\{\pi\in\Pi\divline\nu_{n}\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\not\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}\}
. Now, using the Axiom of Choice, we can define a function
g_{\varphi}\colon\omega\times\textnormal{{N}}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
such that for any
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
and
n\in\omega
, if
P_{n}\cap\lVert\forall u\neg\varphi(u,a)\rVert\neq\emptyset
then
P_{n}\cap\lVert\neg\varphi(g_{\varphi}(n,a),a)\rVert\neq\emptyset
. Finally, let
\tilde{g}_{\varphi}\coloneqq\{(\textnormal{{op}}(\textnormal{{op}}(u,a),g_{\varphi}(u,a)),\pi)\divline u,a\in\textnormal{{N}},\pi\in\Pi\}
be the lift of
g_{\varphi}
to a class
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-function.

It will suffice to show that

\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{q}}u)u\Vdash\forall x\,(\forall n^{\hat{\omega}}\neg\varphi(\tilde{g}_{\varphi}(n,x),x)\rightarrow\forall u\neg\varphi(u,x)).

Suppose, for sake of a contradiction, that this was not the case. Then we can fix
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
,
t\Vdash\forall n^{\hat{\omega}}\neg\varphi(\tilde{g}_{\varphi}(n,a),a)
and
\pi\in\lVert\forall u\neg\varphi(u,a)\rVert
such that
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{q}}u)u\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Next, observe that from this we obtain

(\textnormal{{q}}u)u\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\textnormal{{q}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\underline{\eta_{t}}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\not\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}.

Therefore
\pi\in P_{\eta_{t}}\cap\lVert\forall u\neg\varphi(u,a)\rVert
. So, by the construction of
g_{\varphi}
, we can fix some
\sigma\in P_{\eta_{t}}\cap\lVert\neg\varphi(g_{\varphi}(\eta_{t},a),a)\rVert
. But then we have the desired contradiction because
\sigma\in P_{\eta_{t}}
implies that
t\star\underline{\eta_{t}}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\not\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
while
\underline{\eta_{t}}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\lVert\forall n^{\hat{\omega}}\neg\varphi(\tilde{g}_{\varphi}(n,a),a)\rVert
gives us
t\star\underline{\eta_{t}}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

{lemma}

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is a countable realizability algebra and
\Lambda
contains a special instruction
q
satisfying the instruction quote. Then
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\textnormal{{NEAC}}
.

Proof.

First, by
Section

17
, let
g\colon\omega\times\textnormal{{N}}^{3}\rightarrow\textnormal{{N}}
be a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-function such that

\mathcal{N}\Vdash\forall z\forall x(\exists u(\textnormal{{op}}(x,u)\mathop{\varepsilon}z)\rightarrow\exists n^{\hat{\omega}}(\textnormal{{op}}(x,\tilde{g}(n,z,x))\mathop{\varepsilon}z)).

Now, working within any model of the realized theory, since
\hat{\omega}
is a Trichotomous
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-ordinal (
Section

13.3
) it satisfies the Least Ordinal Principle (
Section

13.3
); if
\exists u(\textnormal{{op}}(x,u)\mathop{\varepsilon}z)
then
\exists n\in\hat{\omega}
such that
\textnormal{{op}}(x,\tilde{g}(n,z,x))\mathop{\varepsilon}z
and for all
m<n
,
\textnormal{{op}}(x,\tilde{g}(m,z,x))\mathop{\varepsilon}z
.

Therefore, we can define a function
f
in
\mathcal{N}
such that
\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}f
if and only if
\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}z
and
y=\tilde{g}(n,z,x)
where
n
is the minimal such element of
\hat{\omega}
(whenever it exists). Clearly, we have
f\subseteq_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}z
and for any
x,y
there exists some
y^{\prime}
such that if
\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}z
then
\textnormal{{op}}(x,y^{\prime})\mathop{\varepsilon}z
.

To show that
f
is a
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-function, suppose that
\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}f
and
\textnormal{{op}}(x,y^{\prime})\mathop{\varepsilon}f
. Then
y=\tilde{g}(n,z,x)
and
y^{\prime}=\tilde{g}(n^{\prime},z,x)
for some
n,n^{\prime}\mathop{\varepsilon}\hat{\omega}
both of which are minimal. Since they are both minimal and
\hat{\omega}
is extensionally an ordinal, in the extensional structure it must be the case that
n\simeq n^{\prime}
and therefore
n=n^{\prime}
by
Section

13.3
.
∎

{theorem}

\ZFepsilon\vdash\textnormal{{NEAC}}\rightarrow\textnormal{{DC}}_{\in}
. Hence, if
\mathcal{A}
is a countable realizability algebra and
\Lambda
contains a special instruction
q
satisfying the instruction quote, then the extensional version of
DC
is realized in
\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

We take
\textnormal{{DC}}_{\in}
in the following form:

\forall a\forall R(\forall x\in a\exists y\in a\,\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\in R\\
\rightarrow\exists g(\textnormal{{Ext-Func}}(g)\land g\colon\hat{\omega}\rightarrow a\land\forall n\in\hat{\omega}\,\textnormal{{op}}(g(n),g(n+1))\in R)).

Fixing
a
and
R
, suppose that
\forall x\in a\exists y\in a\,\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\in R
and set

z\coloneqq\bigcup_{x\in a}\{\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\divline y\in a\land\textnormal{{op}}(x,y)\in R\}.

By
NEAC
we can fix a non-extensional function
f\subseteq_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}z
which refines the relation
z
. Now, fix
x\mathop{\varepsilon}a
. Then we can define a function
g\colon\hat{\omega}\rightarrow a
recursively by setting
g(0)=x
and
g(n+1)
to be the unique
y
such that
\textnormal{{op}}(g(n),y)\mathop{\varepsilon}f
, where we observe that uniqueness follows from
f
being a non-extensional function. To see that
g
is the required extensional function, we first have that
g
is clearly a total function on
\hat{\omega}
since
f\subseteq_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}z
. Now, fix
n,n^{\prime}\mathop{\varepsilon}\hat{\omega}
and
y,y^{\prime}\mathop{\varepsilon}a
and suppose that
n\simeq n^{\prime}
,
\textnormal{{op}}(n,y)\mathop{\varepsilon}g
and
\textnormal{{op}}(n^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mathop{\varepsilon}g
. Then, by
Section

13.3
, we must have that
n=n^{\prime}
and therefore
y=y^{\prime}
. In particular, this means that
y\simeq y^{\prime}
, so
g
is indeed extensional.
∎

18
Non-trivial Realizability Models

Here we repeat the analysis from Section 4 of
[
Kri18
]
to show that it is possible to produce a non-trivial realizability model such that the Boolean algebra
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
has exactly
4
elements. To produce this model we will use the first three Church numerals;
\underline{0}=\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}v
,
\underline{1}=\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}uv
and
\underline{2}=\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}(\underline{1}u)(uv)=\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}u(uv)
. We begin with the observation by Krivine that while
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
may contain
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-elements which are neither
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
or
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})
, all such sets are empty.

{proposition}

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})}\forall y(x\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}x)
.

Proof.

By
Section

10.1
it suffices to prove the claim for
x=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
and
x=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})
. Firstly, if
x=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
then
\lVert a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\rVert=\emptyset
for any
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
and thus
\lVert\forall y(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\rightarrow y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0}))\rVert=\emptyset
. Therefore, in this case any term realizes the statement. On the other hand, if
x=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})
then
\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\rVert=\Pi
. Therefore, for any
a\in\textnormal{{N}}
, if
t\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})
and
(a,\pi)\in\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})
,
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\Pi
. Thus
I
realizes the statement.
∎

{definition}

Say that
(\bar{\Lambda},\bar{\Pi})
is
generated
by
(T,X)
if
\bar{\Lambda}
and
\bar{\Pi}
are the smallest classes satisfying:

•

\forall t\in T\;t\in\bar{\Lambda}
,

•

\forall\omega\in X\;\omega\in\bar{\Pi}
,

•

Every variable is in
\bar{\Lambda}
,

•

If
t,s\in\bar{\Lambda}
then
ts\in\bar{\Lambda}
,

•

If
u
is a variable and
t\in\bar{\Lambda}
then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}t\in\bar{\Lambda}
,

•

\textnormal{{{cc}} }\in\bar{\Lambda}
,

•

\forall\pi\in\bar{\Pi}\;\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\in\bar{\Lambda}
,

•

If
t\in\bar{\Lambda}
and
\pi\in\bar{\Pi}
then
t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\bar{\Pi}
.

Namely,
(\bar{\Lambda},\bar{\Pi})
is generated by
(T,X)
if the collection of special instructions is contained in
T
and the collection of stack constants is contained in
X
.

We consider the realizability algebra
\mathcal{A}=(\Lambda,\Pi,\prec,\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}})
given as follows:

{definition}

•

(\Lambda,\Pi)
is generated by
(\{\textnormal{{d}}\},\{\pi^{0},\pi^{1}\})
. That is, there is exactly one special instruction,
d
and exactly two stack constants,
\pi^{0}
and
\pi^{1}
.

•

For
i\in\{0,1\}
we let
(\Lambda^{i},\Pi^{i})
be the sets generated by
(\{\textnormal{{d}}\},\{\pi^{i}\})
.

•

For
i,j\in\{0,1\}
we define
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
to be the least set
P\subseteq\Lambda^{i}\star\Pi^{i}
such that:

1.

\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{j}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in P
for every
\pi\in\Pi^{i}
,

2.

If
t\star\pi\in\Lambda^{i}\star\Pi^{i}
and
s\star\sigma\in P
then
t\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma\Rightarrow t\star\pi\in P
,

3.

If at least two out of the three processes
t\star\pi
,
s\star\pi
,
r\star\pi
are in
P
then
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in P
.

•

\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
is defined by
(\Lambda\star\Pi)\setminus\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}=(\Lambda^{0}\star\Pi^{0})\setminus\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{0}_{0}\,\cup\,(\Lambda^{1}\star\Pi^{1})\setminus\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{1}_{1}
. That is, a process
t\star\pi
is in
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
if and only if either:

1.

t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{0}_{0}\cup\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{1}_{1}
,

2.

t\star\pi\in(\Lambda\star\Pi)\setminus(\Lambda^{0}\star\Pi^{0}\cup\Lambda^{1}\star\Pi^{1})
, i.e. both stack constants
\pi^{0}
and
\pi^{1}
appear in
t\star\pi
.

{remark}

Since there is no additional requirement on
d
in terms of how it interacts with the pre-order,

\textnormal{{d}}\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\textnormal{{d}}\star\pi=s\star\sigma.

Also, by the minimality requirement on the ordering, for any processes
t\star\pi
and
s\star\sigma
, if
t\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma
then there must exist a finite sequence

t\star\pi=t_{0}\star\pi_{0}\succ_{1}\dots\succ_{1}t_{k}\star\pi_{k}=s\star\sigma

of distinct processes such that each reduction
t_{n}\star\pi_{n}\succ_{1}t_{n+1}\star\pi_{n+1}
is a one-step evaluation given by one of the defining constraints on
\succ_{1}
. In this case the only restrains on
\succ_{1}
are the four basic requirements of push, grab, save, and restore. Moreover, by inspection, it is clear that only one of these can give a possible reduction of a given term and thus this finite sequence is uniquely determined. From this we can conclude that (again, for this realizability algebra):

•

If
t\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma
then there is no infinite sequence of distinct processes
(t_{n}\star\pi_{n}\divline n\in\omega)
such that
t\star\pi=t_{0}\star\pi_{0}\succ t_{1}\star\pi_{1}\succ\dots\succ t_{n}\star\pi_{n}\succ\dots\succ s\star\sigma
.

•

If
t\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma
and
t\star\pi\succ r\star\tau
then either
s\star\sigma\succ r\star\tau
or
r\star\tau\succ s\star\sigma
.

Due to the nature of the structure, there is an obvious isomorphism between
\Lambda^{0}\star\Pi^{0}
and
\Lambda^{1}\star\Pi^{1}
, which is the one generated by sending
\pi^{0}
to
\pi^{1}
. Namely, define
\Xi\coloneqq\Lambda^{0}\cup\Pi^{0}\rightarrow\Lambda^{1}\cup\Pi^{1}
recursively by the following rules:

•

\Xi(\pi^{0})=\pi^{1}
;

•

If
u
is a variable, then
\Xi(u)=u
;

•

If
u
is a variable and
t\in\Lambda^{0}
, then
\Xi(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}t)=\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(t)
;

•

If
u
is a variable and
t,s\in\Lambda^{0}
, then
\Xi(t[u\coloneqq s])=\Xi(t)[u\coloneqq\Xi(s)]
;

•

If
s,t\in\Lambda^{0}
, then
\Xi(ts)=\Xi(t)(\Xi(s))
;

•

\Xi(\textnormal{{{cc}} })=\textnormal{{{cc}} }
and
\Xi(\textnormal{{d}})=\textnormal{{d}}
;

•

If
\pi\in\Pi^{0}
, then
\Xi(\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi})=\textnormal{{k}}_{\Xi(\pi)}
;

•

If
t\in\Lambda^{0}
, and
\pi\in\Pi^{0}
then
\Xi(t\boldsymbol{.}\pi)=\Xi(t)\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(\pi)
.

This then extends to
\Xi\colon\Lambda^{0}\star\Pi^{0}\rightarrow\Lambda^{1}\star\Pi^{1}
by
\Xi(t\star\pi)=\Xi(t)\star\Xi(\pi)
. We now see that this map provides an isomorphism between the structures.

{lemma}

For all
t\star\pi,s\star\sigma\in\Lambda^{0}\star\Pi^{0}
,
s\star\sigma\succ t\star\pi
if and only if
\Xi(s\star\sigma)\succ\Xi(t\star\pi)
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the equivalence for the four basic constraints, which follow from the following observations:

\Xi\big(ts\star\pi\big)=\Xi(ts)\star\Xi(\pi)=\Xi(t)(\Xi(s))\star\Xi(\pi)\succ\Xi(t)\star\Xi(s)\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(\pi)=\Xi(t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi)
.

\Xi(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi)=\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(t)\star\Xi(s)\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(\pi)\succ\Xi(t)[u\coloneqq\Xi(s)]\star\Xi(\pi)=\Xi(t[u\coloneqq s]\star\pi)
.

\Xi(\textnormal{{{cc}} }\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi)=\textnormal{{{cc}} }\star\Xi(t)\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(\pi)\succ\Xi(t)\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\Xi(\pi)}\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(\pi)=\Xi(t\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)
.

\Xi(\textnormal{{k}}_{\sigma}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi)=\textnormal{{k}}_{\Xi(\sigma)}\star\Xi(t)\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(\pi)\succ\Xi(t)\star\Xi(\sigma)=\Xi(t\star\sigma)
.
∎

{lemma}

For
j\in\{0,1\}
,
\Xi\textnormal{``}\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{0}_{j}=\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{1}_{j}
.

Proof.

Fix
j\in\{0,1\}
. Let
B^{i}\coloneqq\{\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{j}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline\pi\in\Pi^{i}\}
, and for any collection of processes
X
let

G_{0}(X)\coloneqq\{t\star\pi\divline\exists s\star\sigma\in X\;(t\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma)\}

and

G_{1}(X)\coloneqq\{\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\divline\text{at least two of }t\star\pi,s\star\sigma\text{ and }r\star\pi\text{ are in }X\}.

It is then clear by the construction of
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
that if we set
T^{i}_{0}=B^{i}
and
T^{i}_{n+1}=G_{0}(T^{i}_{n})\cup G_{1}(T^{i}_{n})
then
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}T^{i}_{n}
. We shall inductively show that
\Xi\textnormal{``}T^{0}_{n}=T^{1}_{n}
and thus
\Xi\textnormal{``}\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{0}_{j}=\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{1}_{j}
. For this it suffices to only prove that
\Xi\textnormal{``}T^{0}_{n}\subseteq T^{1}_{n}
, with the reverse inclusion following by considering the inverse of
\Xi
.

Take
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{j}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in T^{0}_{0}
. Then, since
\underline{j}
is a
\lambda_{c}
-term which contains no appearance of
\pi^{0}
or
\pi^{1}
,
\Xi(\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{j}\boldsymbol{.}\pi)=\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{j}\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(\pi)\in T^{1}_{0}
.

Next, suppose that
t\star\pi\in G_{0}(T^{0}_{n})
and fix
s\star\sigma\in T^{0}_{n}
such that
t\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma
. Then, by the inductive hypothesis,
\Xi(s\star\sigma)\in T^{1}_{n}
and, by
Section

18
,
\Xi(t\star\pi)\succ\Xi(s\star\sigma)
. Thus,
\Xi(t\star\pi)\in G_{0}(T^{1}_{n}).

Finally, suppose that
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in G_{1}(T^{0}_{n})
. Without loss of generality, we shall assume that
t\star\pi
and
s\star\pi
are in
T^{0}_{n}
. Then, by the inductive hypothesis,
\Xi(t\star\pi)=\Xi(t)\star\Xi(\pi)
and
\Xi(s\star\sigma)=\Xi(s)\star\Xi(\sigma)
are in
T^{1}_{n}
. Thus,
\Xi(\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi)=\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(t)\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(s)\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(r)\boldsymbol{.}\Xi(\pi)\in G_{1}(T^{1}_{n})
.
∎

{lemma}

If
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
and
t\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma
, then
s\star\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
.

Proof.

Suppose for a contradiction that
t\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma
and
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
but
s\star\sigma\not\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
.
Using
Section

18
, without loss of generality we can suppose that
t\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma
by exactly one of the basic reduction steps. We shall show that
Q=\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}\setminus\{t\star\pi\}
satisfies the three conditions in the definition of
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
, contradicting the minimality requirement.

1.

Take
\tau\in\Pi^{i}
. If
t\star\pi=\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{j}\boldsymbol{.}\tau
then
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{j}\boldsymbol{.}\tau\succ s\star\sigma
. Therefore, again by the remark,
s\star\sigma=\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{j}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
, contradicting the assumption on
s\star\sigma
. Thus,
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{j}\boldsymbol{.}\tau\in Q
.

2.

Assume that
r\star\tau\in\Lambda^{i}\star\Pi^{i}
,
r^{\prime}\star\tau^{\prime}\in Q
and
r\star\tau\succ r^{\prime}\star\tau^{\prime}
. Then
r\star\tau\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
. Now, if
r\star\tau=t\star\pi
then
t\star\pi\succ r^{\prime}\star\tau^{\prime}
and
t\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma
. Therefore, since the second one of these was assumed to be a one step reduction, we must have that
s\star\sigma\succ r^{\prime}\star\tau^{\prime}
. Therefore, by the second condition in the definition of
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
,
s\star\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
, which is again a contradiction. Thus,
r\star\tau\in Q
.

3.

Suppose at least two of
r\star\tau
,
u\star\tau
and
v\star\tau
were in
Q
. Then
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}u\boldsymbol{.}v\boldsymbol{.}\tau\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
. Now, suppose that
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}u\boldsymbol{.}v\boldsymbol{.}\tau=t\boldsymbol{.}\pi
. Then
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}u\boldsymbol{.}v\boldsymbol{.}\tau\succ s\star\sigma
and thus
s\star\sigma=\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}u\boldsymbol{.}v\boldsymbol{.}\tau\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
, which is a contradiction. Thus,
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}u\boldsymbol{.}v\boldsymbol{.}\tau\in Q
.

∎

{lemma}

For
i\in\{0,1\}
,
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{0}\cap\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

We first show that for any
\pi\in\Pi^{i}
,
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}\setminus\{\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\}
satisfies the three conditions for the definition of
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
and therefore, by the minimality requirement,
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\not\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
.

•

For any
\sigma\in\Pi^{i}
,
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
and
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\neq\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
, so
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma
is in the set.

•

Suppose
s\star\sigma\in\Lambda^{i}\star\Pi^{i}
,
r\star\tau\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}\setminus\{\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\}
and
s\star\sigma\succ r\star\tau
. Then
s\star\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
so, if
s\star\sigma=\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
, then
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ r\star\tau
and therefore
d\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi=r\star\tau\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}\setminus\{d\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\}
, which is a contradiction. Thus
s\star\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}\setminus\{\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\}
.

•

Suppose at least two of
t\star\sigma
,
s\star\sigma
and
r\star\sigma
were in
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}\setminus\{\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\}
. Then
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
and
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\neq\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
.

We shall now prove that
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{0}\subseteq(\Lambda^{i}\star\Pi^{i})\setminus\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
. To do this, it suffices to prove that
\Lambda^{i}\star\Pi^{i}\setminus\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
satisfies the three defining properties for
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{0}
.

•

By the first claim, we have that for any
\pi\in\Pi
,
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in(\Lambda^{i}\star\Pi^{i})\setminus\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
.

•

Suppose that
t\star\pi\in\Lambda^{i}\star\Pi^{i}
,
s\star\sigma\in(\Lambda^{i}\star\Pi^{i})\setminus\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
and
t\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma
. If
t\star\pi
were in
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
then, by
Section

18
,
s\star\sigma
would also be in
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
, which is a contradiction.

•

Suppose that
t\star\pi
,
s\star\pi
,
r\star\pi
were all in
\Lambda^{i}\star\Pi^{i}
but
t\star\pi,s\star\pi\not\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
. We shall show that
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\not\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
. As per usual, this will be done by showing that
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}\setminus\{\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\}
satisfies the three defining properties for
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
:

–

For any
\sigma\in\Pi^{i}
,
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}\setminus\{\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\}
.

–

Suppose
u\star\sigma\in\Lambda^{i}\star\Pi^{i}
,
u\star\sigma\succ v\star\tau
and
v\star\tau\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}\setminus\{\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\}
. Then
u\star\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
. Now, if
u\star\sigma=\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi
then
v\star\tau=\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}\setminus\{\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\}
, which is a contradiction.

–

Suppose at least two of
u_{1}\star\sigma
,
u_{2}\star\sigma
and
u_{3}\star\sigma
were in
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}\setminus\{\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\}
. Then
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}u_{1}\boldsymbol{.}u_{2}\boldsymbol{.}u_{3}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
. Now, if
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}u_{1}\boldsymbol{.}u_{2}\boldsymbol{.}u_{3}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma=\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi
then we must have that
u_{1}=t
,
u_{2}=s
,
u_{3}=r
, and
\sigma=\pi
. Therefore, at least one of
t\star\pi
and
s\star\pi
is in
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{1}
, which contradicts our initial assumption.

∎

{theorem}

\mathcal{A}
is a coherent realizability algebra.

Proof.

Let
t\in\mathcal{R}
be a realizer and suppose that
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
for every
\pi\in\Pi
. As
t
is a realizer, note that
t
cannot contain any instance of
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi^{0}}
or
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi^{1}}
and therefore
t\star\pi^{i}\in\Lambda^{i}\star\Pi^{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
. In particular,
t\in\Lambda^{0}\cap\Lambda^{1}
and therefore
t\star\pi^{0}\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{0}_{0}
and
t\star\pi^{1}\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{1}_{1}
. Moreover, the isomorphism
\Xi
must fix
t
and therefore
\Xi(t\star\pi^{0})=t\star\pi^{1}
. Thus, by
Section

18
,
t\star\pi^{1}\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{1}_{0}
which means that
t\star\pi^{1}\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{1}_{0}\cap\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{1}_{1}
, contradicting
Section

18
.
∎

{lemma}

\textnormal{{d}}(\underline{2})\Vdash\text{``the Boolean algebra }\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})\text{ has at most four }\mathop{\varepsilon}\text{{-}elements''}
.

Proof.

We shall show that

\textnormal{{d}}(\underline{2})\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})}\,\forall y^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})}\,(x\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\land y\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\land x\neq y\rightarrow x\land y\neq x).

To see why this suffices, first observe that if
\mathbb{B}
is a Boolean algebra and there exists an
a
such that
\mathbb{0}<a<\mathbb{1}
and both
a
and
-a
are atoms
8
8

8

a
is said to be an atom is whenever
b<a
then
b=\mathbb{0}
, then
|\mathbb{B}|=4
.
So suppose that
|\mathbb{B}|>4
and fix
x
such that
\mathbb{0}<x<\mathbb{1}
and
x
is not an atom. This means that we can fix
y\in\mathbb{B}
such that
\mathbb{0}<y<x
. Then
x,y
witness the failure of the above statement.

To prove the claim, let
m,n\in\{0,1\}
,
t\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({m})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
,
s\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({n})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})
,
r\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({m})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({n})
and
\pi\in\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({m})\land\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({n})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({m})\rVert
. Since
\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({m})\land\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({n})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({m})\rVert\neq\emptyset
, by definition
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({m})\land\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({n})=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({m})
, from which it follows that
m\leq n
. There are then three possible cases for
(m,n)
,
(0,0)
,
(0,1)
and
(1,1)
, and in all of these cases at least two of the terms
t,s,r
realize
\perp
and thus the resulting term is in
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}^{i}_{j}
for any
i,j\in\{0,1\}
. Hence, by the construction of the realizability model,
\textnormal{{d}}\star\underline{2}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

{theorem}

The Boolean algebra
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
has exactly four
