\mathop{\varepsilon}
-elements.

Proof.

We begin by defining two elements of
N
:

\gamma_{0}=(\{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\}\times\Pi^{0})\cup(\{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\}\times\Pi^{1})\qquad\gamma_{1}=(\{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\}\times\Pi^{0})\cup(\{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\}\times\Pi^{1}).

By construction, we have that
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\subseteq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
. Now:

•

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\neg\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})}(x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\gamma_{0})
. To see this, let
t\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})}(x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\gamma_{0})
and
\pi\in\Pi
. If
\pi\in\Pi\setminus(\Pi^{0}\cup\Pi^{1})
then
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
by definition. So suppose that
\pi\in\Pi^{0}\cup\Pi^{1}
. Then, since
\lVert\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})}(x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\gamma_{0})\rVert=\bigcup_{i\in 2}\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({i})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\gamma_{0}\rVert=\Pi^{0}\cup\Pi^{1}
, we must also have that
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
as required.

•

\textnormal{{d}}(\underline{0})\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\gamma_{0}
and
\textnormal{{d}}(\underline{1})\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}\gamma_{0}
,

Therefore
\gamma_{0}
is not
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-empty and every
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-element of
\gamma_{0}
is not equal to
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
or
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})
. Hence, we must have that
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\exists x(x\mathop{\varepsilon}\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})\land x\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\land x\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1}))
from which we can conclude that
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
has size precisely
4
in
\mathcal{N}
.
∎

{remark}

In fact, as proven in
[
Kri18
]
, one can realize that
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
are singletons and therefore their
\mathop{\varepsilon}
-elements are precisely the two atoms of
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
.

19
Forcing as a Realizability Algebra

It is claimed that realizability is a generalisation of the method of forcing because any forcing Boolean algebra can be naturally seen as a realizability algebra. In this section we explicitly describe this translation, the presentation of which comes from
[
FG20
]
, and expand on the previous work in order to formally state the relationship between forcing and realizability models. We will also assume a lot of background material about Boolean algebras and Boolean valued models, and we refer the reader to Chapters 7 and 14 of
[
Jec03
]
for the necessary background. It is worthwhile to clearly state here that while such a translation exists, the resulting realizability model “
loses its computation content
”. By this we mean there is essentially a unique realizer for all realized statements in the model so from a realizer for a proof we can not extract any computational data.

Let
\mathbb{B}=(\mathbb{B},\mathbb{1},\mathbb{0},\land,\lor,\neg)
be a complete Boolean algebra. We define a realizability algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{B}}=(\Lambda_{(A,B)},\Pi_{(A,B)},\prec,\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}})
as follows: Firstly, set
A=\emptyset
, that it to say there are no special instructions. Next, let
(\omega_{p}\divline p\in\mathbb{B})
be the set of stack bottoms under some fixed enumeration. To define the pre-order and the pole, we begin by inductively defining a function
\tau\colon\Lambda^{\textnormal{{open}}}\cup\Pi\rightarrow\mathbb{B}
;

•

for every stack bottom
\omega_{p}
, we let
\tau(\omega_{p})\coloneqq p
;

•

for every variable
x
,
\tau(x)\coloneqq\tau(\textnormal{{{cc}} })\coloneqq\mathbb{1}
;

•

for every term
t
and stack
\pi
, we let
\tau(t\boldsymbol{.}\pi)=\tau(t)\land\tau(\pi)
;

•

for all
\lambda_{c}
-terms
t,s
, we let
\tau(ts)\coloneqq\tau(t)\land\tau(s)
;

•

for every variable
u
and every term
t
, we let
\tau(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}t)\coloneqq\tau(t)
;

•

for every stack
\pi
, we let
\tau(\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi})\coloneqq\tau(\pi)
.

This is then naturally extended to the set of all processes by the assignment

\tau(t\star\pi)\coloneqq\tau(t)\land\tau(\pi).

Then, we define the pre-order by
t\star\pi\succ s\star\sigma
if and only if
\tau(t\star\pi)\leq\tau(s\star\sigma)
and we let
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
be the set of all processes
t\star\pi
such that
\tau(t\star\pi)=\mathbb{0}
.

{notation}

There is obviously a large amount of overlapping notation between Boolean-valued models and realizability models. In order to hopefully make the notation clearer for this section, while keeping the notation for realizability harmonious with this rest of this work, we will do the following:

•

\lVert\varphi\rVert
and
\lvert\varphi\rvert
will refer to their usual meanings in the realizability model.

•

The Boolean value of a formula
\varphi
will be denoted by
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
.

•

\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}
refers to the realizability relation over the structure
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{B}}
.

•

\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}
refers to forcing with the Boolean algebra. Namely,
p\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\varphi
if and only if
p\leq\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
.

•

\textnormal{{N}}^{\mathcal{A}}
will refer to the domain of the realizability model while
\textnormal{{V}}^{\mathbb{B}}
will refer to the domain of the Boolean-valued model.

We continue by observing that if two terms are mapped to the same element of the Boolean algebra then they realize the same formulas. From this it follows that the realizability model “
loses its computational content
”. This is because there is essentially only one realizer which gives us one single program for all proofs.

{proposition}

For any
t,s\in\Lambda
and formula
\varphi\in Fml_{\mathop{\varepsilon}}
, if
\tau(t)=\tau(s)
then
t\in\lvert\varphi\rvert\Longleftrightarrow s\in\lvert\psi\rvert
.

In particular, if
t
is a realizer then
t\Vdash\varphi\Longleftrightarrow\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\varphi
.

Proof.

Suppose that
t\Vdash\varphi
and
\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rVert
. By the definition of
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
this means that
\mathbb{0}=\tau(t\star\pi)=\tau(t)\land\tau(\pi)=\tau(s)\land\tau(\pi)=\tau(s\star\pi)
. Thus
s\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
so, since
\pi
was arbitrary,
s\Vdash\varphi
.

For the second statement it suffices to observe that if
t
is a realizer, then
\tau(t)=\mathbb{1}
and thus all realizers realize the same formulas. Formally, this is proven by induction on the construction of all - possibly open -
\lambda_{c}
-terms by showing that if
t
does not contain any continuation constants then
\tau(t)=\mathbb{1}
. But this is obvious from the definition of
\tau
since
\tau(\textnormal{{{cc}} })=\mathbb{1}
as does
\tau(u)
for any variable
u
and if
t
is a
\lambda_{c}
-term such that
\tau(t)=\mathbb{1}
then
\tau(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}t)=\tau(t)=\mathbb{1}
.
∎

From this it follows that in this case the realizability model is trivial, namely the Boolean algebra
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
has only 2 elements. We shall prove a converse to this result in
Section

20
.

{proposition}

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})}(x\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\rightarrow(x\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\rightarrow\perp))
.

Proof.

As per usual, it suffices to prove that

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash x\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\rightarrow(x\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\rightarrow\perp)

for
x=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
and
x=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})
. Fix
t\Vdash x\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
,
s\Vdash x\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})
and
\pi\in\Pi
. If
x=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})
then
\lVert\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\rVert=\Pi
and therefore
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. On the other hand, if
x=\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})
then
\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}v\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ s\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Thus,
\underline{0}\Vdash\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\rightarrow(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\rightarrow\perp)
. Since
\underline{0}
is a realizer, by
Section

19
we also have that
I
realizes the same sentence, completing the proof.
∎

In remains to prove that the Boolean-valued model and the realizability model prove the same sentences. To do this, recall that the Boolean-valued model
\textnormal{{V}}^{\mathbb{B}}
is defined recursively as

•

\textnormal{{V}}_{0}^{\mathbb{B}}=\emptyset
;

•

\textnormal{{V}}_{\alpha+1}^{\mathbb{B}}
is the set of all functions
b
whose domain is a subset of
\textnormal{{V}}_{\alpha}^{\mathbb{B}}
and whose range is a subset of
\mathbb{B}
;

•

If
\lambda
is a limit ordinal then
\textnormal{{V}}_{\lambda}^{\mathbb{B}}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\lambda}\textnormal{{V}}_{\alpha}^{\mathbb{B}}
;

•

\textnormal{{V}}^{\mathbb{B}}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\textnormal{{Ord}}}\textnormal{{V}}_{\alpha}^{\mathbb{B}}
.

On the other hand, the defining property for the domain of the realizability model was that
\textnormal{{N}}_{\alpha+1}^{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{P}(\textnormal{{N}}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{A}}\times\Pi)
. It is easy to extend
\tau
to map any name in the realizability model to one in the Boolean-valued model and similarly we can define a new function
\sigma
which does the reverse. We note here that this interpretation is very similar to how one goes between the Boolean-valued approach and the partial order approach for forcing and is where we require the Boolean algebra to be complete.

{definition}

We recursively define
\tau(a)\in\textnormal{{V}}^{\mathbb{B}}
, for
a\in\textnormal{{N}}^{\mathcal{A}}
, to be the function
\tau(a)\colon\tau\textnormal{``}\textnormal{{dom}}(a)\rightarrow\mathbb{B}
given by

\tau(a)(\tau(x))\coloneqq\bigvee\{\tau(\pi)\divline(x,\pi)\in a\}.

Similarly, we recursively define
\sigma(b)\in\textnormal{{N}}^{\mathcal{A}}
, for
b\in\textnormal{{V}}^{\mathbb{B}}
, as

\sigma(b)\coloneqq\{(\sigma(x),\omega_{p})\divline b(x)=p\}.

{proposition}

For any
a\in\textnormal{{N}}^{\mathcal{A}}
and
b\in\textnormal{{V}}^{\mathbb{B}}
,

\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}a\simeq\sigma(\tau(a))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{1}\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}b=\tau(\sigma(b)).

Proof.

We prove the first statement by induction on the rank of
a
. So suppose that for all
c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)
,
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}c\simeq\sigma(\tau(c))
. Using
Section

19
, it will suffice to show that
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash a\subseteq\sigma(\tau(a))
and
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\sigma(\tau(a))\subseteq a
.

For the first of these, fix
c\in\textnormal{{N}}
and suppose that
t\Vdash c\not\in\sigma(\tau(a))
while
(c,\pi)\in a
. By the induction hypothesis
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}c\simeq\sigma(\tau(c))
. Now, by definition of the translations, we have
(\sigma(\tau(c)),\omega_{p})\in\sigma(\tau(a))
where
p=\tau(a)(\tau(c))=\bigvee\{(\tau(\pi^{\prime})\divline(c,\pi^{\prime})\in a\}\geq\tau(\pi)
. Therefore,
t\star\textnormal{{I}}\boldsymbol{.}\omega_{p}\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. However,
\tau(t)\land\tau(\pi)\leq\tau(t)\land p=\tau(t)\land\tau(\textnormal{{I}})\land\tau(\omega_{p})=0
. Thus
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
so
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash a\subseteq\sigma(\tau(a))
.

For the reverse direction, fix
c\in a
and suppose that
t\Vdash\sigma(\tau(c))\not\in a
while
(\sigma(\tau(c)),\omega_{p})\in\sigma(\tau(a))
. Now, by the induction hypothesis,
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\sigma(\tau(c))\simeq c
. Therefore, for any
\pi
such that
(c,\pi)\in a
,
t\star\textnormal{{I}}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, from which it follows that
\tau(t)\land\tau(\pi)=0
. So, since
p
is the supremum of
\{\tau(\pi^{\prime})\divline(c,\pi^{\prime})\in a\}
it must be the case that
\tau(t)\land p
is also equal to
0
. Thus
\textnormal{{I}}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\omega_{p}\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, as required.

We again prove that second statement by induction on the rank of
b
. So suppose that for all
c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(b)
,
\mathbb{1}\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}c=\tau(\sigma(c))
. As before we consider the cases
b\subseteq\tau(\sigma(b))
and
\tau(\sigma(b))\subseteq b
separately. To prove this, observe that if
c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(b)
then
(\sigma(c),\omega_{b(c)})\in\sigma(b)
and therefore
\tau(\sigma(c))\in\textnormal{{dom}}(\tau(\sigma(b)))
and
\tau(\sigma(b))(\tau(\sigma(c)))=b(c)
.

For the first case, we shall prove that
\llbracket b\subseteq\tau(\sigma(b))\rrbracket\coloneqq\bigwedge\{b(c)\rightarrow\llbracket c\in\tau(\sigma(b))\rrbracket\divline c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(b)\}=\mathbb{1}
. To do this, it suffices to prove that for every
c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(b)
,
\llbracket c\in\tau(\sigma(b))\rrbracket\geq b(c)
. But this follows from the inductive hypothesis since

\displaystyle\llbracket c\in\tau(\sigma(b))\rrbracket

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigvee\{\llbracket c=d\rrbracket\land\tau(\sigma(b))(d)\divline d\in\textnormal{{dom}}(\tau(\sigma(b)))\}

\displaystyle\geq\llbracket c=\tau(\sigma(c))\rrbracket\land\tau(\sigma(b))(\tau(\sigma(c)))

\displaystyle=b(c).

For the reverse direction, we have that

\llbracket\tau(\sigma(b))\subseteq b\rrbracket=\bigwedge\{\tau(\sigma(b))(\tau(\sigma(c)))\rightarrow\llbracket\tau(\sigma(c))\in b\rrbracket\divline c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(b)\}.

Then, as before, using the inductive hypothesis we have that
\llbracket\tau(\sigma(c))\in b\rrbracket\geq\llbracket\tau(\sigma(c))=c\rrbracket\land b(c)=b(c)=\tau(\sigma(b))(\tau(\sigma(c)))
. From this it follows that
\llbracket\tau(\sigma(b))\subseteq b\rrbracket=\mathbb{1}
, as required.
∎

{theorem}

For any formula
\varphi\in Fml_{\in}
,
t\in\Lambda
and
a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in\textnormal{{N}}^{\mathcal{A}}

t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n})\Longleftrightarrow\tau(t)\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\varphi(\tau(a_{0}),\dots,\tau(a_{n})).

In order to prove this theorem when the formula is of the form
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
we will make use of the following lemma which identifies the stacks which falsify a given formula. For ease of notation we will omit variables.

{lemma}

Suppose that
p\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\varphi\Longrightarrow\forall t\in\Lambda(\tau(t)=p\rightarrow t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi)
. Then

(\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rVert\Longrightarrow p\land\tau(\pi)=\mathbb{0}).

Proof.

Suppose that the assumption of the lemma holds and fix
\pi\in\lVert\varphi\rVert
. Then, since
\tau(\textnormal{{k}}_{\omega_{p}})=\tau(\omega_{p})=p
, we must have that
\textnormal{{k}}_{\omega_{p}}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi
. Thus,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\omega_{p}}\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, that is,
\mathbb{0}=\tau(\textnormal{{k}}_{\omega_{p}})\land\tau(\pi)=p\land\tau(\pi)
.
∎

Proof of
19
.

We shall prove this by induction on the complexity of formulas. For this it will suffice to consider in turn the following cases:
\top
,
\perp
, atomic formulas, implications, bounded universal quantification.

Firstly, it is clear that for every
p\in\mathbb{B}
and
t\in\Lambda
,
p\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\top
and
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\top
so the conclusion of the theorem is immediate here. Similarly,
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\perp
if and only if for every
\pi\in\Pi
,
\tau(t)\land\tau(\pi)=\mathbb{0}
. In particular, since
\tau(\omega_{\mathbb{1}})=\mathbb{1}
,
\tau(t)=\tau(t)\land\mathbb{1}=0
. Conversely,
p\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\perp
if and only if
p=\mathbb{0}
.

We prove the two atomic cases by induction on rank. Suppose that
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}a\subseteq b
, in order to prove that
\tau(t)\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\tau(a)\subseteq\tau(b)
it suffices to prove that for any
c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)
,
\tau(t)\leq\tau(a)(\tau(c))\rightarrow\llbracket\tau(c)\in\tau(b)\rrbracket
. By the laws of Boolean operations, this is equivalent to proving that for any
c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(a)
,

\mathbb{0}=\tau(t)-(-\tau(a)(\tau(c))\lor\llbracket\tau(c)\in\tau(b)\rrbracket)=\tau(t)\land\tau(a)(\tau(c))\land\llbracket\tau(c)\not\in\tau(b)\rrbracket.

To do this, let
p=\llbracket\tau(c)\not\in\tau(b)\rrbracket
. Then by the inductive hypothesis,
\textnormal{{k}}_{\omega_{p}}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}c\not\in b
. Therefore, by the assumption on
t
, if
(c,\pi)\in a
then
t\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\omega_{p}}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Thus,

\tau(t)\land\tau(a)(\tau(c))\land\llbracket\tau(c)\not\in\tau(b)\rrbracket\leq\tau(t)\land\bigvee\{\tau(\pi)\divline(c,\pi)\in a\}\land p=\mathbb{0}.

For the reverse implication, assume that
\tau(t)\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\tau(a)\subseteq\tau(b)
,
(c,\pi)\in a
and
s\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}c\not\in b
. Then, by the inductive hypothesis, we have
\tau(s)\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\tau(c)\not\in\tau(b)
and

\tau(t)\land\tau(s)\land\tau(\pi)\leq\tau(t)\land\llbracket\tau(c)\not\in\tau(b)\rrbracket\land\tau(a)(\tau(c))=\mathbb{0}.

Thus,
t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
so
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}a\subseteq b
.

Moving onto the second atomic case, suppose that
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}a\not\in b
. Again, in order to prove that
\tau(t)\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\tau(a)\not\in\tau(b)
, it suffices to prove that

\tau(t)\leq-\bigvee\{\llbracket\tau(a)=\tau(c)\rrbracket\land\tau(b)(\tau(c))\divline c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(b)\}.

Using the De Morgan laws it can be seen that this is equivalent to showing that for every
c\in\textnormal{{dom}}(b)
,
\tau(t)\leq-\llbracket\tau(a)=\tau(c)\rrbracket\lor-\tau(b)(\tau(c))
. By definition of
\llbracket\tau(a)=\tau(c)\rrbracket
, this reduces to showing that

\tau(t)\leq-(\llbracket\tau(a)\subseteq\tau(c)\rrbracket\land\llbracket\tau(c)\subseteq\tau(a)\rrbracket\land\tau(b)(\tau(c))),

which gives
\tau(t)\land\llbracket\tau(a)\subseteq\tau(c)\rrbracket\land\llbracket\tau(c)\subseteq\tau(a)\rrbracket\land\tau(b)(\tau(c))=\mathbb{0}
. To prove this, set
p=\llbracket\tau(a)\subseteq\tau(c)\rrbracket
and
p^{\prime}=\llbracket\tau(c)\subseteq\tau(a)\rrbracket
. Using the inductive hypothesis we have
\textnormal{{k}}_{\omega_{p}}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}a\subseteq c
and
\textnormal{{k}}_{\omega_{p^{\prime}}}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}c\subseteq a
. Therefore, by the assumption on
t
, if
(c,\pi)\in b
then
t\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\omega_{p}}\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{k}}_{\omega_{p^{\prime}}}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Thus,

\tau(t)\land\llbracket\tau(a)\subseteq\tau(c)\rrbracket\land\llbracket\tau(c)\subseteq\tau(a)\rrbracket\land\tau(b)(\tau(c))\leq\tau(t)\land p\land p^{\prime}\land\bigvee\{\tau(\pi)\divline(c,\pi)\in b\}=\mathbb{0}.

For the reverse implication, assume that
\tau(t)\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\tau(a)\not\in\tau(b)
,
(c,\pi)\in b
,
s\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}a\subseteq c
and
s^{\prime}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}c\subseteq a
. Then, by the inductive hypothesis, we have
\tau(s)\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\tau(a)\subseteq\tau(c)
,
\tau(s^{\prime})\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\tau(c)\subseteq\tau(a)
and so

\tau(t)\land\tau(s)\land\tau(s^{\prime})\land\tau(\pi)\leq\tau(t)\land\llbracket\tau(a)\subseteq\tau(c)\rrbracket\land\llbracket\tau(c)\subseteq\tau(a)\rrbracket\land\tau(a)(\tau(c))=\mathbb{0}.

Thus,
t\star s\boldsymbol{.}s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, so
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}a\not\in b
.

Next, suppose that the claims holds for
\varphi
and
\psi
. Suppose that
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
and let
p=\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
. Then, by the inductive hypothesis,
\textnormal{{k}}_{p}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi
so, by
Section

10.1
,
t\textnormal{{k}}_{p}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\psi
. Thus, by the inductive hypothesis again,
\tau(t\textnormal{{k}}_{p})\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\psi
. Therefore,
\tau(t)\land\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket=\tau(t)\land\tau(\textnormal{{k}}_{p})\leq\llbracket\psi\rrbracket
, which can easily be seen to give
\tau(t)\leq\llbracket\varphi\rightarrow\psi\rrbracket
.

For the reverse direction, suppose that
\tau(t)\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
,
s\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi
and
\pi\in\lVert\psi\rVert
. By the inductive hypothesis,
\tau(s)\leq\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
and so
\tau(t)\land\tau(s)\leq\llbracket\psi\rrbracket
. Then, by
Section

19
, since
\pi\in\lVert\psi\rVert
,
\tau(t)\land\tau(s)\land\tau(\pi)=\mathbb{0}
which gives us that
t\star s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Hence
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
, as required.

For the final case, suppose that the claim holds for
\varphi(x)
. Suppose that
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\forall x\varphi(x)
and fix
b\in\textnormal{{V}}^{\mathbb{B}}
. Then
\sigma(b)\in\textnormal{{N}}^{\mathcal{A}}
from which it follows that
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi(\sigma(b))
. Therefore, by the inductive hypothesis,
\tau(t)\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\varphi(\tau(\sigma(b))
. Since
\mathbb{1}\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}b=\tau(\sigma(b))
, this gives us that
\tau(t)\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\varphi(b)
and thus
\tau(t)\leq\llbracket\varphi(b)\rrbracket
. Since
b
was an arbitrary element of
\textnormal{{V}}^{\mathbb{B}}
it follows that
\tau(t)\leq\llbracket\forall x\varphi(x)\rrbracket
, as required.

For the reverse direction, suppose that
\tau(t)\Vdash_{\mathbb{B}}\forall x\varphi(x)
and fix
a\in\textnormal{{N}}^{\mathcal{A}}
. Then
\tau(a)\in\textnormal{{V}}^{\mathbb{B}}
and thus
\tau(t)\leq\llbracket\varphi(\tau(a))\rrbracket
. Therefore, by the inductive hypothesis,
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi(a)
. Thus, since
a\in\textnormal{{N}}^{\mathcal{A}}
was arbitrary,
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\forall x\varphi(x)
.
∎

We end by concluding that the Boolean-valued model and the realizability model are “the same” in some precise way.

{corollary}

For any sentence
\varphi\in Fml_{\in}
,
\mathcal{N}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi
if and only if
\varphi
is valid
9
9

9

that is
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket=\mathbb{1}
in
\textnormal{{V}}^{\mathbb{B}}
.

Proof.

\mathcal{N}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi
if and only if there is some realizer
t
such that
t\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi
. Since
\tau(t)=\mathbb{1}
for any realizer,
\mathcal{N}\Vdash_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi
is equivalent to asserting that
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket=\mathbb{1}
.
∎

20
When
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
is trivial

Here we discuss a partial converse to the previous section, which is the claim by Krivine that when
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
10
10

10

or, with the original formulation,
\text{\Large\cjRL{g}}{2}
is trivial then the realizability model is in fact a forcing model. This proof has not appeared in print before but is contained in Krivine’s slides on “Some properties of realizability models” given in Chambéry in June 2012
[
Kri
]
. We note here that this proof requires that the realizability algebra is
countable
. Therefore, it is unknown if the more general claim, that when
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
is trivial then the realizability model is a forcing model, is true when working without uncountable realizability algebras. For simplicity, we will also assume that there are no special instructions.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is a countable realizability algebra such that in the realizability model
\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})
is trivial. This means that there exists a realizer
\textnormal{{r}}\in\mathcal{R}
such that

\textnormal{{r}}\Vdash\forall x^{\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({2})}\,(x\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\rightarrow(x\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\rightarrow\perp)).

In particular, we have that
\textnormal{{r}}\Vdash(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\rightarrow(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\rightarrow\perp)
and

\textnormal{{r}}\Vdash(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({0})\rightarrow(\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\neq\text{\Large\cjRL{r}}({1})\rightarrow\perp)
. Which is to say,

\textnormal{{r}}\in\lvert\top\rightarrow(\perp\rightarrow\perp)\rvert\cap\lvert\perp\rightarrow(\top\rightarrow\perp).\rvert

Let
\textnormal{{r}}^{\prime}=\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}\textnormal{{{cc}} }\Big(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}\big(\textnormal{{r}}(k(u))\big)(k(v))\Big)
. We shall show that
r^{\prime}
satisfies the instruction
fork
.

{lemma}

For any terms
t,s\in\Lambda
and stacks
\pi\in\Pi
, if either
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
or
s\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
then
\textnormal{{r}}^{\prime}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

Proof.

First, observe that if
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
then for any
\sigma\in\Pi
,

\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t\star\sigma\succ\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\star t\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\succ t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}},

and therefore
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t\Vdash\perp
. Now,

\begin{split}\textnormal{{r}}^{\prime}\star t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}\pi&\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }\Big(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{r}}(kt))(ks)\Big)\star\pi\succ\textnormal{{{cc}} }\star\Big(\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{r}}(kt))(ks)\Big)\boldsymbol{.}\pi\\
&\succ\lambda k\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{r}}(kt))(ks)\star\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ(\textnormal{{r}}(\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t))(\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}s)\star\pi.\end{split}

Since either
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t
or
\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}s
realizes
\perp
by our initial assumption, by the construction of
r
we must have that
(\textnormal{{r}}(\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t))(\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}s)\Vdash\perp
. Thus
(\textnormal{{r}}(\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}t))(\textnormal{{k}}_{\pi}s)\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, yielding the result.
∎

What we shall see next is that there is a single realizer which uniformly realizes every realizable statement in
\mathcal{N}
. In order to do this, we shall focus on subsets of
\Pi
rather than formulas, and we briefly explain here why this generality is sufficient. Given
X\subseteq\Pi
, if we let
1_{X}\coloneqq\{(0,\pi)\divline\pi\in X\}
and
\theta_{X}\equiv 0\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}1_{X}
, then
\lVert\theta_{X}\rVert=\{\pi\divline(0,\pi)\in 1_{X}\}=X
. Moreover, for any formula
\varphi
,
\lVert\theta_{\lVert\varphi\rVert}\rVert=\lVert\varphi\rVert
and therefore
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\theta_{\lVert\varphi\rVert}
and
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\theta_{\lVert\varphi\rVert}\rightarrow\varphi
. From this we can see that there is a one-to-one correspondence between falsity values and subsets of
\Pi
.

Expanding our notation, given
X\subseteq\Pi
, we shall say that
t\Vdash X
iff
t\Vdash\theta_{X}
, namely for every
\pi\in X
,
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Analogous notation will also be used for the statement
A\rightarrow B
.

{theorem}

\exists\Phi\in\mathcal{R}\;\forall t\in\mathcal{R}\;\forall X\subseteq\Pi\;(t\Vdash X\Rightarrow\Phi\Vdash X)
.

Proof.

We will use the result from
\lambda
-calculus that there is a single
\lambda
-term which enumerates all other closed
\lambda
-terms. An involved proof of this, for the standard
\lambda
-calculus, can be found in Chapter 8 of
[
Bar84
]
, and this can be adapted to the
\lambda_{c}
-calculus.

Therefore, we fix a realizer
e
such that for any realizer
t
there is some
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
\pi\in\Pi
,
\textnormal{{e}}(\underline{n})\star\pi\succ t\star\pi
. Next, let
\textnormal{{s}}=\lambda n\boldsymbol{.}\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}(nu)(uv)
and recall that for every
n
we have
\textnormal{{s}}\underline{n}=\underline{n+1}
.

Now, define
q\coloneqq\lambda x\boldsymbol{.}\lambda y\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{r}}^{\prime}(\textnormal{{e}}y))(xx(\textnormal{{s}}y))
and let
p\coloneqq qq
. Then, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

p(\underline{n})=q[x\coloneqq q,y\coloneqq\underline{n}]=(\textnormal{{r}}^{\prime}(\textnormal{{e}}\underline{n}))(p(\textnormal{{s}}\underline{n})).

and thus, for any
\pi\in\Pi
,

p\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\textnormal{{r}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{e}}\underline{n})\boldsymbol{.}(p(\textnormal{{s}}\underline{n}))\boldsymbol{.}\pi.

We shall show that
p\underline{0}
is our desired realizer.

Suppose that
t\in\mathcal{R}
,
X\subseteq\Pi
and
t\Vdash X
. Fix
\pi\in X
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\textnormal{{e}}\underline{n}\star\pi\succ t\star\pi
. Then, since
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
, we must have that
\textnormal{{e}}\underline{n}\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. So, by
Section

20
,
p\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\textnormal{{r}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{e}}\underline{n})\boldsymbol{.}(p(\textnormal{{s}}\underline{n}))\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.

The theorem will follow once we show that if
p\star\underline{m+1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
then so is
p\star\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
. But, if
p\star\underline{m+1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
then, since
p(\textnormal{{s}}\underline{m})\star\pi\succ p\star\underline{m+1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
the former is also in
\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Therefore, by
Section

20
,
p\star\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\textnormal{{r}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{.}(\textnormal{{e}}\underline{n})\boldsymbol{.}(p(\textnormal{{s}}\underline{m}))\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
.
∎

Now, let
\mathbb{B}=\mathcal{P}(\Pi)
be the Boolean algebra of truth values and define a partial order on
\mathbb{B}
by

A\leq B\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\exists t\in\mathcal{R}(t\Vdash A\rightarrow B)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\Phi\Vdash A\rightarrow B.

Observe that
\theta_{A}\land\theta_{B}\equiv(0\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}1_{A})\land(0\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}1_{B})\equiv 0\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\varepsilon$\hfil\cr\hfil$/$\hfil\cr}}}1_{A\cup B}=\theta_{A\cup B}
and similarly
\theta_{A}\lor\theta_{B}\equiv\theta_{A\cap B}
. Therefore,
\Pi\leq A\cup B\leq A\leq A\cap B\leq\emptyset
for every
A,B\in\mathcal{P}(\Pi)
. Thus the meet operator of
\mathbb{B}
is given by intersections while the join operator is given by unions, and the partial order extends reverse inclusion.

{theorem}

\mathbb{B}
is a complete Boolean algebra.

Proof.

It will suffice to prove that if
\{X_{i}\divline i\in I\}\subseteq\mathbb{B}
then
\textnormal{{sup}}_{i\in I}X_{i}=\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
. Let
A\leq X_{i}
for every
i\in I
. Suppose that
t\Vdash A
and
\pi\in\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
, then
\pi\in X_{j}
for some
j
. Since
A\leq X_{j}
,
\Phi\Vdash A\rightarrow X_{j}
and thus
\Phi\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Hence,
\Phi\Vdash A\rightarrow\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
and so
A\leq\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
.

Conversely, if
t\Vdash\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
then for any
j\in I
and
\pi\in X_{j}
,
t\star\pi\in\mathbin{\text{$\bot\mkern-11.0mu\bot$}}
. Thus
\textnormal{{I}}\Vdash\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}\rightarrow X_{j}
, from which it follows that
\Phi\Vdash\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}\rightarrow X_{j}
and
\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}\leq X_{j}
.
∎

Since
\mathbb{B}
is a complete Boolean Algebra, we can produce the Boolean-valued forcing model
\textnormal{{V}}^{\mathbb{B}}
. Recall that a formula
\psi
is said to be
valid
in this model if
\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{\mathbb{B}}=\emptyset
(the maximal element of the Boolean algebra).

{lemma}

For any formula
\psi\in Fml_{\in}
,
\lVert\psi\rVert=\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{\mathbb{B}}
.

Proof.

Formally, this will be done by induction on the complexity of formulas, but we shall only sketch the details below. For atomic formulas it suffices to show that:
\lVert x\simeq x\rVert=\emptyset
;
\lVert x\simeq y\rVert=\lVert y\simeq x\rVert
;
\lVert x\simeq y\rVert\cdot\lVert y\simeq z\rVert\leq\lVert x\simeq z\rVert
and
\lVert x\in y\rVert\cdot\lVert v\simeq x\rVert\cdot\lVert w\simeq y\rVert\leq\lVert v\in w\rVert
.

Observe that the propositional logical counterparts of these statements are:
x\simeq x\rightarrow\top
;
x\simeq y\leftrightarrow y\simeq x
;
x\simeq y\land y\simeq z\rightarrow x\simeq z
and
x\in y\land v\simeq x\land w\simeq y\rightarrow v\in w
. Since all of these are true statements, and the realizability model preserves logic deductions, all of these statements are realized. Thus, they are all realized by
\Phi
from which it follows that
\lVert\cdot\rVert
satisfies these four properties.

The logical connectives
\land
and
\lor
follow from the fact that meets correspond to intersections while joins correspond to unions. Meanwhile, the proof for implication comes directly from the definition of the partial order. Finally the proofs for the quantifiers will follow from
\mathbb{B}
being a complete Boolean algebra with
\textnormal{{sup}}_{i\in I}X_{i}=\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
and
\textnormal{{inf}}_{i\in I}X_{i}=\bigcap_{i\in I}X_{i}
.
∎

{corollary}

For any formula
\psi\in Fml_{\in}
,
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\psi
if and only if
\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{\mathbb{B}}=\emptyset
. Thus,
\mathcal{N}
is a forcing model.

Proof.

Fix
\psi
. First recall that
\mathcal{N}\Vdash\psi
means that there exists a realizer
t\in\mathcal{R}
such that
t\Vdash\psi
which, by
Section

20
, is equivalent to saying that
\Phi\Vdash\psi
. Then
\Phi\Vdash\psi\Longleftrightarrow\Phi\Vdash\top\rightarrow\psi\Longleftrightarrow\emptyset=\lVert\top\rVert\leq\lVert\psi\rVert\Longleftrightarrow\text{the meet of }\emptyset\text{ and }\lVert\psi\rVert\text{ equals }\emptyset\Longleftrightarrow\lVert\psi\rVert=\emptyset\Longleftrightarrow\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{\mathbb{B}}=\emptyset
.
∎

21
Comparing numerals

In this section we explicitly give the realizer that was mentioned in
Section

13.3
. This is a term
\chi
such that for any
n,m\in\omega
,
t,s,r\in\Lambda
and
\pi\in\Pi
,

\chi\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\begin{cases}t\star\pi&\text{if }\ n<m,\\
s\star\pi&\text{if }\ n=m,\\
r\star\pi&\text{if }\ m<n.\end{cases}

{lemma}

Let
X\coloneqq\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\underline{1}
,
Y\coloneqq\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\underline{0}
,
A\coloneqq\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}vu
and
B\coloneqq\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}u(Av)
. Finally, let

\theta\coloneqq\lambda i\boldsymbol{.}\lambda j\boldsymbol{.}\bigg(\big((jA)(AX)\big)\big(iB\big)\bigg)\Big(Y\Big).

Then, for any
n,m\in\omega
and
\pi\in\Pi
, if
n<m
then
\theta\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{0}\star\pi
and if
n\geq m
then
\theta\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{1}\star\pi
.

Proof.

Fix
n,m\in\omega
and
\pi\in\Pi
. Then
\theta\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{m}\star A\boldsymbol{.}AX\boldsymbol{.}\underline{n}B\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi
. We shall first prove that

\underline{m}\star A\boldsymbol{.}AX\boldsymbol{.}\underline{n}B\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{n}\star B\boldsymbol{.}A^{m}X\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi

where
x^{0}y\coloneqq y
and
x^{k+1}y\coloneqq x(x^{k}y)
. To do this, we need to consider the case for
m=0
separately;

\underline{0}\star A\boldsymbol{.}AX\boldsymbol{.}\underline{n}B\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ AX\star\underline{n}B\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ A\star X\boldsymbol{.}\underline{n}B\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ(\underline{n}B)X\star Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{n}\star B\boldsymbol{.}X\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi.

Now, for
m>0
,

\displaystyle\underline{m}\star A\boldsymbol{.}AX\boldsymbol{.}\underline{n}B\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ A^{m}(AX)\star\underline{n}B\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ A\star A^{m}X\boldsymbol{.}\underline{n}B\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ(\underline{n}B)(A^{m}X)\star Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{n}\star B\boldsymbol{.}A^{m}X\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi,

where we have used that
A^{m}(AX)=A^{m+1}X=A(A^{m}X)
.

We shall next prove that
\underline{n}\star B\boldsymbol{.}A^{m}X\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ A^{m}X\star A^{n}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi
. In order to do this, we again need to consider the case
n=0
separately;

\underline{0}\star B\boldsymbol{.}A^{m}X\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ A^{m}X\star Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi.

Now, for
n>0
,

\displaystyle\underline{n}\star B\boldsymbol{.}A^{m}X\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ B^{n}(A^{m}X)\star Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ B\star B^{n-1}(A^{m}X)\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ B^{n-1}(A^{m}X)\star AY\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ\dots\succ A^{m}X\star A^{n}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi.

The proof shall be completed once we have shown that
A^{m}X\star A^{n}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
if
n<m
and
A^{m}X\star A^{n}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{1}\boldsymbol{.}\pi
if
n\geq m
.

Firstly, if
m=0
then

X\star A^{n}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\equiv\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\underline{1}\star A^{n}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{1}\star\pi.

Next, if
n=0
and
m>0
then

A^{m}X\star Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ A\star A^{m-1}X\boldsymbol{.}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ Y\star A^{m-1}X\boldsymbol{.}\pi\equiv\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}\underline{0}\star A^{m-1}X\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{0}\star\pi.

Finally, if
n
and
m
are both greater than
0
then

\displaystyle A^{m}X\star A^{n}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ A\star A^{m-1}X\boldsymbol{.}A^{n}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ A^{n}Y\star A^{m-1}X\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ A\star A^{n-1}Y\boldsymbol{.}A^{m-1}X\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ A^{m-1}X\star A^{n-1}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi.

Continuing in this way, for any
0\leq k\leq\textnormal{{min}}(n,m)
we have

A^{m}X\star A^{n}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ A^{m-k}X\star A^{n-k}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi.

Therefore, if
n<m
then

A^{m}X\star A^{n}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ A^{m-n}X\star Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ A\star A^{m-n-1}X\star Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ Y\star A^{m-n-1}X\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{0}\boldsymbol{.}\pi

and if
n\geq m
then

\displaystyle A^{m}X\star A^{n}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ A^{m-(m-1)}X\star A^{n-(m-1)}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ A\star X\boldsymbol{.}A^{n-(m-1)}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ A^{n-(m-1)}Y\star X\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ A\star A^{n-(m-1)-1}Y\boldsymbol{.}X\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ X\star A^{n-(m-1)-1}Y\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{1}\star\pi,

completing the proof.
∎

{lemma}

Fix
\theta
as in the previous lemma. Let

\chi\coloneqq\lambda i\boldsymbol{.}\lambda j\boldsymbol{.}\lambda e_{t}\boldsymbol{.}\lambda e_{s}\boldsymbol{.}\lambda e_{r}\boldsymbol{.}(((\theta i)j)(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(((\theta j)i)(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}e_{s}))e_{r}))e_{t}.

Then for any
n,m\in\omega
, terms
t,s
and
r
and
\pi\in\Pi
,

i.

if
n<m
then
\chi\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\boldsymbol{.}\pi
;

ii.

if
n=m
then
\chi\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ s\boldsymbol{.}\pi
;

iii.

if
n>m
then
\chi\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ r\boldsymbol{.}\pi
.

Proof.

Fix
n,m\in\omega
,
t,s,r\in\Lambda
and
\pi\in\Pi
. Then

\chi\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\theta\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(((\theta\underline{m})\underline{n})(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}s))r)\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}\pi.

If
n<m
then
\theta\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\succ\underline{0}\star\sigma
for any
\sigma\in\Pi
. Therefore,

\chi\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{0}\star(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(((\theta\underline{m})\underline{n})(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}s))r)\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ t\star\pi.

Next, if
n\geq m
then
\theta\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\succ\underline{1}\star\sigma
for any
\sigma\in\Pi
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\chi\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ\underline{1}\star(\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(((\theta\underline{m})\underline{n})(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}s))r)\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\lambda u\boldsymbol{.}(((\theta\underline{m})\underline{n})(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}s))r\star t\boldsymbol{.}\pi

\displaystyle\succ(((\theta\underline{m})\underline{n})(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}s))r\star\pi\succ\theta\star\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}s)\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi.

Now, if
n=m
then
\theta\star\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\sigma\succ\underline{1}\star\sigma
for any
\sigma\in\Pi
. Therefore,

\chi\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{1}\star(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}s)\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}s)r\star\pi\succ\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}s\star r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ s\star\pi.

Finally, if
n>m
then

\chi\star\underline{n}\boldsymbol{.}\underline{m}\boldsymbol{.}t\boldsymbol{.}s\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ\underline{0}\star(\lambda v\boldsymbol{.}s)\boldsymbol{.}r\boldsymbol{.}\pi\succ r\star\pi.

∎
